# One choice changes: Boeing opens fps to all three engine makers. Both new narrowbodies fly two engine sources and Boeing's balance sheet stays repaired; CFM/GE falls to 27% of narrowbody engines

BOEING PROPRIETARY. Boeing Product Development war game on the narrowbody/widebody game-theory dashboard, played by ExCo and Board agents, with Control's added objectives.

One choice changed against the previous game: Boeing opened fps to all three engine makers instead of CFM alone, for Control's supply enabler (ordered in 2030, kept in 2035). Every other order in the four rounds was the same. So the airframers' values did not move (Airbus ΔPV +$23.37B, Boeing +$0.66B), and the engine makers' did: Rolls-Royce +$32.35B (was +$20.55B), P&W +$8.88B (was −$2.93B), CFM/GE −$69.69B (was −$38.29B). From 2040 the P&W-Rolls-Royce Joint Venture engine flies on both new narrowbodies and its partners hold 36.5% of narrowbody engines each; CFM/GE holds 27%, against 76% today. On Control's objectives: Boeing's net debt is at its historical level from 2029 (from 2030 in the stress case) and stays there through the fps spending peak. Both new narrowbodies fly two engine sources, CFM and the Joint Venture engine, with three engine makers in the market. Rolls-Royce's widebody was never starved, but its own narrowbody money reached $5.71B against the $4B bound, a breach it stated before ordering. Thirteen of nineteen objectives were met, against twelve for the previous game's orders scored the same way. Twenty AI agents played four sealed rounds (2030, 2035, 2045 and 2050), as before: each company an executive committee (ExCo) of CEO, CFO and operating head under a Board agent, now with Control's objectives and the 2026 analyst models in their files. No Board vetoed anything.

## Summary

| Player | Final ΔPV $B | Yield | Objectives met |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Boeing | +0.66 | 100.51% | 5 of 7 |
| Airbus | +23.37 | 115.58% | 3 of 4 |
| CFM/GE | −69.69 | 56.44% | 0 of 2 |
| Pratt & Whitney | +8.88 | 108.07% | 2 of 2 |
| Rolls-Royce | +32.35 | 129.41% | 3 of 4 |

**Control's supply enabler changed one choice: Boeing opened fps to all three engine makers.**
The engine code does not enter Boeing's value on the dashboard, so the change cost Boeing nothing.
- Ortberg's own record: "Doctrine and my Low-confidence lean were code 3; code 7 keeps CFM as the fallback and serves the supply-stability enabler." His Board had asked for code 7 "at no cost to Boeing's payoff".
- In the previous game, with no enabler in its brief, Boeing put fps on CFM alone (code 3) and kept it there in 2035. Here it ordered code 7 in 2030 and kept it in 2035.
- Boeing published an engine competition: CFM the baseline engine, certifying first; a P&W or Rolls-Royce engine to follow "once it shows maturity and durability". Goal: "two or three engine sources on our narrowbody and three narrowbody engine makers in the market."
- P&W and Rolls-Royce had each ordered a launch conditional on selection, so both triggered on both airframes. After 2030 each new narrowbody had three engine sources.

**The Joint Venture turned three engine sources into two.**
- P&W and Rolls-Royce committed on sealed orders in 2035, as in the previous game, and their two solo engines folded into one. Control counts the Joint Venture engine as one source, so fps and NGSA each fly two: CFM and the Joint Venture engine. The enabler's "two or three" is met at two.
- The Boeing Board in 2045: "The enabler is met at two sources, not the three we planned in 2035: the Joint Venture folded both solo engines into one source." Boeing then said in public: "If the Joint Venture engine stumbles, the fps is back on CFM alone."
- The Joint Venture was worth +$5.51B to P&W and +$2.29B to Rolls-Royce, as before, and moved 4.8 points of narrowbody engines from CFM/GE to P&W.
- Two of Control's aims pull apart here (inference): Rolls-Royce's $4B bound points to the Joint Venture (a $4B share against $8B alone), and the Joint Venture removes the third independent source the airframers' enabler prefers.

**Boeing's balance sheet stays repaired on the 10-year ramp, stress case included. The objective gave the ramp a second reason, not a new answer.**
- The analysts' consensus cash flow takes Boeing's net debt from $22.53B at end-2026 to zero in 2029, before any game spend. The 10-year fps then spends $3.02B in 2030, peaking at $8.44B in 2036, and free cash flow stays positive every year: at least $2.52B in the stress case.
- The stress case separated the ramps. On the 7-year, net debt goes back above zero ($0.71B at end-2034, $0.47B at end-2035); via Embraer, up to $3.31B in 2037.
- The Board still preferred the 7-year by its worst-column rule (−$1.50B against −$1.96B), as in the previous game. This time it said it would approve the 10-year if the $0.46B given up was logged as objective premium. Ortberg logged it: "Objective part: it keeps the stress-case debt line." Malave's balance-sheet test broke the tie.
- The share cost is unchanged: Boeing at 37% of narrowbodies in 2039 and 38% in 2040, missing incumbency, and 47% from 2049.

**Rolls-Royce stated its breach of the $4B risk bound before ordering, and took the narrowbody anyway. Its widebody was never starved.**
- Its 2030 brief showed the trade: UltraFan NB launched on selection is $8B of its own money if selected, and $5.7B if folded into a Joint Venture in 2035. Erginbilgic framed it: "The price is that a selection takes our own narrowbody money to $8B, against Control's $4B bound."
- Staying inside meant holding everything, worth $8.31B less in expectation, above Rolls-Royce's $3B cap on objective premium. Every seat named the trade, and the Board approved: "scored by Control, not a veto ground".
- Both airframers selected it, and the 2035 fold left $5.71B: the $4B share plus $1.71B written off. Holding in 2030 and joining in 2035 would have met all four objectives and been worth $1.71B more, but only because P&W launched and the Joint Venture inherited GTF2's 2036 schedule. Rolls-Royce's 2030 record assumed a 2035 Joint Venture would be ready only in 2041.
- Widebody: it held the Trent 1000 upgrade and UltraFan WB in every round. Each lost more than its own bill once the $5B strain was in the row. Strain stayed $0, and Rolls-Royce keeps 55.2% of widebody engines from 2042.

**CFM/GE paid for the second source: −$69.69B, $31.40B worse than the previous game, on identical orders.**
- Its narrowbody engine share falls from 76% to 51.7% in 2037 and 27.0% from 2040, against 51-57% in the previous game. In 2040 it delivers 1,079 narrowbody engines against 3,040 in the status quo.
- CFM/GE learned the fps selection only from the 2035 brief, and nothing on its menu could change an airframer's engine code. Culp: "Nothing on this grid defends against a switch". In 2030 it had expected the ducted engine to keep it "back above 50%" in its columns where Boeing chose all three makers; the 2035 brief showed 31.7% from 2040, and the Joint Venture took that to 27.0%. The ducted engine is in that figure: take its share out and CFM/GE would hold 17.5% (GM computation; 13.3% had it never launched, since the Embraer partnership would have lapsed too). Its own ExCo concluded in 2045, wrongly, that the 27.0% left no share for the ducted engine.
- It refused to compete for fps source position on price, in 2035 and again in 2050. Its Board: "That is not ours to chase with terms."
- Control added no objective for CFM/GE. It missed both of its own: 27% against its 50% narrowbody target, and no Open Fan, which no round could make ready by 2050 within its $15B allowance.

**Airbus made the same orders for a new reason.**
- NGSA again open to all three makers (code 7), launched in 2030 for 2037. Faury called it "THE ENABLER TRAP": if P&W and Rolls-Royce held, NGSA would fly CFM alone. Code 7 was the only route to three sources, and only in 2030.
- No value was traded for it: "the engine code is payoff-neutral on this board".
- No Delay Tactics in any round (red lines, integrity and the team's stricter condition, then closed once fps entered service in 2040), and no A350 Re-engine, held on the cash line or the $1B test. Airbus's 60/40 edge is missed (53% in 2050), as before.

**No Board vetoed anything, and no veto check sent a decision back.**
- Nine orders went to a Board (seven in 2030, two in 2035), all approved, as in the previous game.
- The CFOs' and operating heads' test memos marked 60 vetoes binding; none was invoked against an order the CEO chose.
- Of 140 answers to Board recommendations, the one open divergence is again Boeing's ramp. This time the Board had offered the 10-year as an alternative it would approve.

## Control's objectives

Control added three kinds of objective to the base set. They were in the private briefs of the companies concerned (Boeing, Airbus, Rolls-Royce) from 2030, each plan on their payoff grids carried its metrics, and the GM scored them. They never change a payoff: the dashboard's ΔPV, its own debt penalty included, is untouched. A company could give up value for an objective only within its own premium cap, and had to log it.
- Boeing: repair the balance sheet. Net debt back to its historical level (about zero, rounded from its 2010-2018 average of −$0.6B) by end-2030, and held at end-2035, 2040, 2045 and 2050; and the same in a stress case with free cash flow one-third below consensus. The GM's tracker starts from the analysts' consensus ($22.53B at end-2026) and their free cash flow to 2030.
- Boeing and Airbus: an enabler, stable engine supply. Two or three independent engine sources on its new narrowbody in production in 2040, 2045 and 2050 (the Joint Venture engine is one source), and three narrowbody engine makers in the market in 2045 and 2050.
- Rolls-Royce: protect the widebody cash cow. Its own narrowbody development money, committed or written off, at most $4B (the Joint Venture share; UltraFan NB Solo is $8B); and never a narrowbody and a widebody engine programme on the record at once (the dashboard's one-off $5B strain).
Thirteen of the nineteen objectives were met. All seven of Control's additions to Boeing and Airbus were met from 2030 on; Rolls-Royce met the widebody line and missed the risk bound, by its own stated choice in 2030.

Net debt at year end in 2026 dollars, from the consensus $22.53B at end-2026. Base free cash flow is the analysts' consensus for 2027-2030, deflated at 2.2%, and flat at $13.79B after; the stress case is one-third lower. Programme cash is the fps bill spread over 2030-2039 on the standard development curve; the game's profit change counts from 2030: small and negative from NGSA's entry into service in 2037, then positive from fps's in 2040. Once net debt is at its historical level, surplus cash goes to the owners, so the line stays at zero. With no new programme, net debt would also reach zero in 2029: the analysts' cash flow repairs the balance sheet before any game decision, and the objective tests whether a launch undoes it.

- Stress free cash flow is $9.19B a year from 2030 (2026 dollars). The 10-year fps spends $3.02B in 2030, rising to a peak of $8.44B in 2036. After the 21% tax effect, the year's free cash flow stays positive in every year: at least $2.52B (2036) in the stress case and, from 2030, $7.12B in the base case.
- The 7-year ramp packs a bigger bill ($65.00B against $60.32B) into fewer years: $12.54B in 2034. In the stress case that year's free cash flow is −$0.71B, so net debt goes back above zero ($0.71B at end-2034, $0.47B at end-2035) and the 2035 hold fails.
- fps via Embraer fails the same line: $0.29B at end-2035, peaking at $3.31B in 2037.
- So in the stress case only the 10-year ramp keeps the balance sheet repaired. In the base case all three ramps do.

### Engine sources on the new narrowbodies and Rolls-Royce's narrowbody exposure

| State | fps engines 2040 | fps engines 2050 | NGSA engines 2040 | NGSA engines 2050 | NB engine makers 2050 | RR own NB development money $B | RR NB+WB strain $B |
|---|---|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| After round 1 (2030) | CFM + P&W GTF2 + RR UltraFan NB | CFM + P&W GTF2 + RR UltraFan NB | CFM + P&W GTF2 + RR UltraFan NB | CFM + P&W GTF2 + RR UltraFan NB | 3 | +8.00 | +0.00 |
| After round 2 (2035) | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | 3 | +5.71 | +0.00 |
| After round 3 (2045) | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | 3 | +5.71 | +0.00 |
| After round 4 (2050) | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | 3 | +5.71 | +0.00 |
| Previous game, final | CFM | CFM | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | CFM + P&W-RR Joint Venture | 3 | +5.71 | +0.00 |

Independent engine sources fitted to each new narrowbody in production, as Control counts them: the Joint Venture engine is one source. After 2030 each new narrowbody had three (CFM, GTF2 and UltraFan NB, all ready by entry into service); the Joint Venture formed in 2035 made it two. Rolls-Royce's own narrowbody development money is the $8B Solo bill while the Solo stood, then $5.71B: its $4B Joint Venture share plus $1.71B written off at the fold. The previous game's final state (fps on CFM alone) is scored on the same yardstick.

**The supply enabler changed the one choice that changed.**
- Boeing chose code 7 against its doctrine lean to code 3, because of the enabler, at no cost to its value. Airbus chose code 7 as in the previous game, now argued through the enabler.
- On the previous game's orders Boeing's first enabler line fails: fps on CFM alone, one source in 2040-2050. Both games keep three narrowbody engine makers.

**Two sources, not three: the Joint Venture merged them.**
- After 2030 both new narrowbodies had three independent sources; the 2035 Joint Venture made it two. No seat in either airframer could have stopped it: the Joint Venture formed on the two engine makers' commitments alone, and no engine code could separate the two engines again once they merged.
- The Boeing Board named the Joint Venture engine "the single point of fragility", and P&W's own operating head agreed: "One finding hits both fleets at once."

**The balance-sheet objective bound the ramp choice, not the launch.**
- The analysts' consensus repairs the balance sheet in 2029 on its own. The question the objective asked was whether a launch undoes it.
- The 10-year fps keeps every line in both cases. The 7-year and fps via Embraer fail the stress case; a 787 Re-engine beside the 10-year would not have (it halved the 2036 stress headroom, to about $1.3B, in Malave's 2035 test).
- After 2045 the Board turned to capital returns: a dividend of about $3B a year plus up to $5B of buybacks, capped at $8B and sized on the stress case. That is a Board matter, not a game order.

**Rolls-Royce's risk bound lost to the grid, openly.**
- Every seat priced the breach in 2030, and no seat proposed waiting or an early Joint Venture commitment (the latter was a red line while P&W's commitment was not public).
- From 2035 its Board's rule was "never cancel or fold for the metric alone". The 2035 Joint Venture commitment was taken on the grid (+$2.28B if P&W also committed), not on the metric.

**The widebody was protected by the grid as much as by the objective.**
- With the $5B strain in the row, the Trent 1000 upgrade lost $5.87-6.72B against holding and UltraFan WB $6.96-9.00B in 2035-2050, more than their own $2B and $4B bills. From 2035 Rolls-Royce's CFO withheld her co-signature on both.
- Neither airframer re-engined a widebody, so no widebody engine was ever needed.

### Regret (final ΔPV of the best alternative minus actual, everyone else's actual orders held)

| Player | Round 1 | Round 2 | Round 3 | Round 4 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|

## How the companies decided

As in the previous game, the chain never needed its later steps: no binding veto at the veto check, red-line flag, revision, Board veto or reversion in 20 company-rounds. Each company ran the same chain every round. The Board gave guidance. The CEO framed the choice, and the CFO and operating head tested the options independently. The CEO decided; the CFO and operating head then ran a veto check on that decision, and the CEO could revise once. Any order other than hold then went to the Board, with one more revision and a reversion to the default if a Board veto stood. In their test memos the CFO and operating head could mark any option a veto, some binding; the CEOs treated the binding ones as ruling those options out before choosing. Control's objectives entered every step: the guidance of the three Boards with an added objective (Boeing, Airbus, Rolls-Royce), the CFOs' tests (Boeing's debt line, Rolls-Royce's $4B line) and each CEO's objective note. Names below are the agents profiled on those executives, not the people.

| Round, company | Binding vetoes at the veto check | Red-line flags | ExCo revised | Board items | Board vetoed | Reverted |

The binding vetoes in the CFOs' and operating heads' test memos (60 in all) fell on options the CEO did not choose or, where conditional, were answered by the CEO's wording; none was invoked against a chosen order.

**The Boards wrote Control's objectives into their limits.**
- Boeing: "Net debt must reach $0 by end-2030 and stay there, in the stress case too", and code 7 for the enabler "at no cost to Boeing's payoff". It offered to approve the 10-year ramp, against its own worst-column preference, if the $0.46B was logged as objective premium.
- Airbus: "A stable engine supply" as a priority, code 7 preferred, "the Board never vetoes a code".
- Rolls-Royce: it would accept the $8B against $4B "only because the trigger is a selection", and kept every widebody programme off the record: "Any widebody programme now carries $5B for good."
- CFM/GE and P&W had no added objective. Their Boards' limits were the previous game's.

**No seat said an objective changed an order, except Boeing's CEO on the engine code.**
- Every company logged its objective premium. Boeing: $0.46B in 2030, logged as doctrine plus objective (the stress-case debt line), with no split between the two. Rolls-Royce declined the $8.31B it would have cost to stay inside its bound. Airbus and CFM/GE paid nothing for objectives.
- Doctrine premiums, logged separately: Boeing $0.65B (2035), $0.30B (2045, counted after the fact once Airbus held) and $0.05B (2050); Airbus $0.89B (2045) and $0.57B (2050) for holding the A350 Re-engine; P&W $0.26B (2030) for launching only on selection.

**The ExCos agreed at the decision; no binding veto was invoked against a chosen order.**
- The memos marked 60 vetoes binding (Airbus 22, CFM/GE 16, Rolls-Royce 11, P&W 8, Boeing 3), all on options the CEO did not choose, or conditional vetoes on the chosen order or statement that the CEO's wording or expected column answered; none was invoked against an order chosen. The previous game had 58.
- Failed tests on chosen orders were advice: Rolls-Royce's CFO failed its own $4B line on the conditional launch (and, later, on the hold that kept it) and an R&D-envelope test; CFM/GE's CFO flagged a "second stack of projects" on its 2030 package.

**The Boards' advice that was not taken was about words, not orders.**
- The CEOs answered 140 Board recommendations. Boeing's ramp was the one order where the CEO took the Board's stated alternative, not its preference.
- Airbus's Board asked to soften "the same will apply to its successor" on engine choice to "we intend to offer a choice of engines"; the 2030 statement kept the old line, and Faury adopted the new one in 2035.
- P&W's CEO recorded in 2045 that a 2035 Board wording change to the Joint Venture statement "was not taken up".

**Boeing's public sourcing line drew answers, not concessions.**
- In 2045 Boeing said: "If the Joint Venture engine stumbles, the fps is back on CFM alone. Any durability finding is a live problem, and I would then want a third source."
- P&W's Board: "Do not answer Boeing's 'third source' remark with a concession." Calio: "fair, and it is the right test", answered with fleet data.
- CFM/GE's Board: "That is not ours to chase with terms." Culp offered durability data with no commercial term "to keep or win a source position".

12 Near-Nash besides. Engine game: 1 Pure Nash, 11 Near-Nash.

Where the game ended, as a board cell: Airbus Launch NGSA + Do Nothing A350; Boeing fps 10yr Solo + Do Nothing 787; at fps 2040, NGSA 2037.

### The airframer stage game each round (engine makers as played; nobody moves later)

| Round | Pure equilibria (ΔPV Boeing / Airbus) | What was played |
|---|---|---|
| Round 1 (2030) | Boeing fps 10-yr + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA + A350 Re-engine + Delay Tactics (one move) (−3.40 / +26.67); Boeing fps 10-yr + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA + A350: hold + Delay Tactics (one move) (+1.42 / +24.08) | Boeing fps 10-yr + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 2 (2035) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine + Delay Tactics (one move) (−2.24 / +26.56); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold + Delay Tactics (one move) (−0.13 / +24.54) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 3 (2045) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine (+0.34 / +24.26); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.96 / +23.26) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |
| Round 4 (2050) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine (+0.57 / +23.94); Boeing fps: hold + 787 Re-engine / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.71 / +23.33) | Boeing fps: hold + 787: hold / Airbus NGSA: hold + A350: hold (+0.66 / +23.37) |

**The played outcome is on none of the snapshot's equilibrium lists, as before.**
- Your snapshot's four Pure Nash all pair fps on the 10-year ramp with NGSA plus one Delay Tactics move, with one airframer re-engining. The game's cell, with no Delay Tactics and no Re-engine, is neither Pure nor Near-Nash.
- In each round's stage game the two pure equilibria have one airframer re-engine and the other hold: Chicken on the widebody through 2050. Both holding was within $1B of each side's best reply only in 2045 and 2050.

**Regret: Airbus gave up most, $3.30B in 2030; none of the best alternatives changes an objective result except Rolls-Royce's.**
- Boeing: $2.20B in 2030, falling to $0.04B in 2050, all from the 787 Re-engine. With the 10-year ramp it keeps every debt line and the enabler, so Control's objectives would not have stopped it. In 2030 and 2035 the one-development-at-a-time rule and the Board's downside test did; in 2045 and 2050 the Board's $1B business-case test.
- Airbus: $3.30B in 2030 and $3.19B in 2035 (one Delay Tactics move and the A350 Re-engine), then $0.89B and $0.57B. Its objective results would have been the same.
- Rolls-Royce: $1.71B in 2030, exactly its fold-in write-off. Holding UltraFan NB in 2030 and joining in 2035 would have brought the same Joint Venture without it, and met all four objectives. Valued as if nobody moved afterwards, the conditional launch was its best order (no myopic regret).

**Calibration: the 2030 grids missed the engine selections.**
- Airbus expected +$45.82B in 2030, in the case where Boeing held fps; it got +$23.37B. Boeing expected −$1.96B, in the case where Airbus also re-engined the A350; it got +$0.66B.
- None of the engine makers' 2030 cases had both airframers selecting all three makers. CFM/GE expected −$21B (NGSA on CFM and P&W only) and got −$62.61B; Rolls-Royce +$8.31B (weighted) against +$30.07B; P&W +$0.23B against +$3.37B.
- In 2035 P&W and Rolls-Royce again weighted the other's Joint Venture commitment low (0.10 or less): P&W expected +$0.39B and got +$8.88B. Calio: "In a sealed, simultaneous round, Rolls-Royce's conditional commit was as free and as dominant for it as ours was for us." From 2045 every company was within $1.31B.

**All 20 company-rounds kept to their own information.**
- An audit of every tool call found all 20 company-rounds clean: no company reached another's material, the dashboard or the game master's files.
- Each company's chain ran as its own workflow, in parallel, in every round, and nothing passed between companies.

## Round 1: decision year 2030

**2030: both new narrowbodies launch, open to all three engine makers; Rolls-Royce states its breach**

- Boeing lifted its rule against a first-round launch: by its CEO's reading the 777X and MAX 7/10 were done, and the tracker showed net debt at zero in 2029 with no new programme. It launched fps on the 10-year ramp, open to all three engine makers for Control's enabler. Its Board preferred the 7-year but approved the 10-year for the stress-case debt line. No rate increase and no 787 Re-engine.
- Airbus launched NGSA for 2037, again open to all three makers, now argued as the only route to three sources. No A350 Re-engine and no Delay Tactics.
- P&W and Rolls-Royce ordered launches conditional on selection, and both airframers selected both. Rolls-Royce stated in advance that a selection breaches Control's $4B bound.
- CFM/GE launched the ducted engine, the Embraer partnership and the GEnx upgrade, as before.
- The Boards approved all seven orders put to them.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) | We said we'd do a new airplane when the market, the technology and Boeing were all ready. We've done the work to get here: the development programmes we had in front of us are done, and the balance sheet is back where it belongs. So today we are launching a new Boeing single-aisle airplane. We're planning for entry into service in 2040, and that plan has headroom built into it. It is so much more important that we do this right than fast. We won't build airplanes ahead of a mature design. We'll work the certification plan with the FAA from day one. We'll develop this airplane with our suppliers on cost-plus and risk-share terms, not fixed price. On engines, we've asked CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce to bring us their best. CFM is our baseline, and we want more than one source of supply on this airplane. Our 737 MAX and 787 customers come first while we do this. Stable production and the KPIs decide our rates. Debt stays first in our capital allocation. I'm not going to give you any more dates today. The financials will follow our production performance. We still have a lot of work to do. |
| Airbus | Launch NGSA (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. On that basis, Airbus is launching its next-generation single-aisle aircraft. We prepared it through a decade of technology roadmaps, and we are launching it now because those technologies are mature, not to meet anyone else's calendar. We target entry into service in the second half of the 2030s. Customers have been well served by a choice of engines on the A320 family, and the same will apply to its successor. We have selected CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce, and we invite all three to join the programme as risk-sharing partners. Until the new aircraft enters service, the A320neo and A350 families remain our priority, with a disciplined and controlled production ramp-up and continued work to secure our supply chain, engines and critical materials first. We will fund the programme with financial discipline, as disciplined investments in preparing our future portfolio, within our financial flexibility and our existing shareholder return policy. We will continue to work with our industry partners for a tariff-free, zero-for-zero regime for aerospace trade. A stronger and more sovereign European aerospace industry is built on one well-prepared programme at a time. |
| CFM/GE | Launch ducted engine; Partner with Embraer; GEnx upgrade | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year. Let me tell you what we know and what we don't. Nothing new has launched yet in the narrowbody. When it does, our customers decide the aircraft and the timing, not us, and we don't have a birthright on that next order. So CFM is launching a new ducted engine programme, so that every airframer has a CFM engine to choose on whatever comes next. CFM is also partnering with Embraer, putting a CFM engine in front of a third airframer. And at GE we're launching a GEnx upgrade for the 787 and the airlines that fly it. RISE remains our next generation. We're all in on the open fan technology, and it stays funded. But it's really not for us to say when that architecture enters service. If an airframer launches a new narrowbody now, the open fan won't be ready for it, and we won't ask anyone to wait. Here's what we don't know: which aircraft launch, when, and with which engines. We won't give you dates our testing hasn't earned. You'll get them when it has. We'll fund this within our capital allocation framework and keep the balance sheet where it is. We want to be on all the critical platforms that matter in the decades ahead. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Launch GTF2 if selected | Bad news first. For too long the GTF fleet was not where it needed to be, and we own that. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF, and the fleet comes first. We keep inserting the Hot Section durability kit across the fleet, and no new programme takes parts or engineers away from that work. We control what we can control. Pratt & Whitney will commit its next-generation geared turbofan, GTF2, in any round an airframer selects it, sole source or shared, on standard commercial terms. GTF2 will be ready six years from launch: 2036 for a 2030 commitment, and no earlier. Every life-limited part will be at full life at entry into service, and durability will be validated in severe-environment testing before then. If anything has to give, the date moves, not the testing. We are making no Joint Venture commitment this round. We will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates or rates; those are theirs to announce. These are responsible bets with responsible paybacks. |
| Rolls-Royce | Launch UltraFan NB if selected | So, plainly. Rolls-Royce will launch UltraFan for any airframer that selects it, in the round it selects it. Not before. Entry into service no earlier than seven years from launch, with support in place before entry into service. We don't need partnership for capability, but our preference is that: the Joint Venture is open to Pratt & Whitney, and we commit once its commitment is on the record. Widebody is our core, and we will not put it at risk. We won't do anything not profitable. We don't commit that easily; when we commit, we deliver. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5 this round. We accept one staged, Solo fps launch (7- or 10-year ramp, with an engine code that includes CFM). We accept no Board item whose value falls below -$1B in any column (all five are plausible, since nothing is launched yet), no second … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: My lean: launch one Solo fps on the 10-year ramp with fps engine code 7. 737 rate hold. 787 Re-engine hold. Expected column: Airbus launches NGSA this round. I take S4 under the doctrine fallback (profile §7 expects an early A350 Re-engine attempt), with S2 close … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM). Tests failed: Buffer test (slip leg), every fps launch via Embraer row; Rate step (CEO ask 7). Vetoes on options: fps: launch via Embraer (every row: alone, with the 787 Re-engine, with the Rate Increase, with both) (binding); 787 Re-engine: launch this round (beside the 10-year fps) (not binding); 737 rate: increase (with any fps, or alone with S4 expected) (not binding); fps: launch (7-year ramp-up) in place of launch (10-year ramp-up) (not … |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM). Tests failed: Ramp choice: 7-year, EIS 2037 [inference on BX-1331; doctrine BX-1263, BX-1309, B-2343] (CEO ask 3). Vetoes on options: 737 rate: increase (with any fps value) (not binding); fps: launch (7-year ramp-up) + 787 Re-engine: launch (not binding); fps: launch (10-year ramp-up) + 787 Re-engine: launch (not binding); fps: launch via Embraer (any row) (not binding); fps: launch (7-year ramp-up) (Solo) (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Launch fps, 10-year ramp-up (engines requested: RR, P&W and CFM) |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: approve; approved fps; 5 recommendations |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan B: fps launch (10-year ramp-up), code 7, 737 rate hold, 787 Re-engine hold | Run as the baseline. In the brief's Risk case column (S5: NGSA plus covert Delay Tactics) the plan may be no more than $2B below Do Nothing: S5 >= -3.64 - 2.00 = -5.64 | pass | Row 'fps 10-yr + 787: hold', column S5 = -0.78. Do Nothing ('fps: hold + 787: hold') S5 = -3.64. The plan is +2.86 against Do Nothing and 4.86 above the floor. Answers CEO ask 1. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan C: fps launch (7-year ramp-up), code 7, 737 rate hold, 787 Re-engine hold | S5 >= -5.64 (Do Nothing -3.64 less $2B) | pass | Row 'fps 7-yr + 787: hold', S5 = -0.83: +2.81 against Do Nothing, 4.81 above the floor. Against plan B it is 0.05 lower in S5. |
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan D: fps launch (10-year ramp-up) plus 787 Re-engine launch | S5 >= -5.64 | pass | Row 'fps 10-yr + 787 Re-engine', S5 = +1.42: +5.06 against Do Nothing, 7.06 above the floor. It passes my buffer test, so I have no veto on it. My objection is advice only: its worst column is -5.54 (S4) against -1.96 without the Re-engine. For reference, 'fps 7-yr + 787 Re-engine' S5 = +0.45 also clears the floor, but H4 strikes it (5-year overlap). |
| Buffer test (slip leg), Rate Increase rows | S5 >= -5.64 | pass | 'fps 10-yr + 737 rate up + 787: hold' S5 -1.52 (4.12 above the floor); 'fps 7-yr + 737 rate up + 787: hold' S5 -1.57 (4.07 above); 'fps: hold + 737 rate up + 787: hold' S5 -4.38 (-0.74 against Do Nothing, 1.26 above the floor); 'fps 10-yr + 737 rate up + 787 Re-engine' +0.68. No veto on buffer grounds. They fail on value (see Rate step). |
| Buffer test (slip leg), every fps launch via Embraer row | S5 >= -5.64 | fail | S5: 'fps via Embraer + 787: hold' -18.83 (15.19 below Do Nothing, 13.19 below the floor); '+ 787 Re-engine' -17.47 (11.83 below the floor); '+ 737 rate up' -19.58 (13.94 below); '+ rate + Re-engine' -18.21 (12.57 below). It trails the 10-year Solo by 18.05-18.06 in every column. Binding veto on all four rows. |
| Slip-leg column and expected column (CEO ask 8) | The slip leg is the column the brief labels 'Risk case'. Airbus reads follow Ortberg's frame and doctrine (profile §7), since I have no evidence of my own on Airbus | pass | S5 is the brief's labelled Risk case (NGSA plus covert Delay Tactics, -5 pp for 5 years from fps EIS), and it is the only column that prices the supply-chain and talent hit on fps, so it is the right slip leg this round. Expected column: S4 (NGSA plus A350 Re-engine), as in the CEO's frame, with S2 close behind. Do Nothing's worst column is S4, -6.26. |
| Meet and beat (worst-column tie-break; CEO ask 5) | Among plans within epsilon ($1B) of the best plan in the expected column (S4), prefer the higher worst column (inference) | pass | S4: 7-year -1.50 (best), 10-year -1.96, 7-year plus rate -2.25 are within epsilon; 10-year plus rate -2.70 is not. Worst columns, all S4: 7-year -1.50, 10-year -1.96, 7-year plus rate -2.25. Against Do Nothing's worst (-6.26): 10-year +4.30, 7-year +4.76. The tie-break leans to the 7-year by 0.46, which is inside epsilon. The risk case leans to the 10-year by 0.05 (-0.78 against -0.83). Both Solo ramps pass. My rank-1 test (balance sheet) breaks the tie for the 10-year. |
| Balance sheet first: debt penalty (CEO ask 3) | The grid's ΔPV already nets each plan's discounted debt penalty (α 0.30, debt x α x (debt/EV)^2); never subtract it again. Between plans within epsilon in the expected column, prefer the smaller penalty (inference) | pass | Confirmed: every grid figure is net of the penalty. Undiscounted, for scale only and not additive across moves: 10-year fps $19.1B, 7-year $22.0B, via Embraer $52.5B, 787 Re-engine alone $0.6B. B and C are 0.46 apart in S4, within epsilon, so the smaller penalty decides: the 10-year, by $2.9B undiscounted, on a bill $4.68B smaller ($60.32B against $65.00B). |
| Balance sheet first: debt lines on the GM tracker (objective) | Net debt <= $0 by end-2030; held at end-2035, 2040, 2045 and 2050; the same in the stress case (free cash flow one-third below consensus) | pass | Objective-metrics cells, the same in S1-S5. Plan B (10-year): 2029 / yes / yes ($0.0B) / 1 (3). Plan C (7-year): 2029 / yes / no ($0.7B) / 1 (3). 7-year plus rate: yes ($0.4B). 10-year plus Re-engine: yes ($0.0B). Via Embraer: no ($3.3-3.7B). Do Nothing: yes ($0.0B), 1 (1). Plan B keeps all three debt lines; plan C misses the stress line. |
| Fundable when the 777X turns (CEO ask 2) | fps is fundable once the 777X is cash-positive (from 2029), and only with balance-sheet repair on track (inference) | pass | The 777X is cash-positive from 2029 [BX-0547]. Tracker with no programme: net debt $0 at end-2029 (base) and end-2030 (stress: $3.27B at end-2029, about $6.0B of 2030 surplus, which covers about $7.6B of pre-tax 2030 programme cash). Year-1 programme cash: B about $3.0B (5% of $60.32B); C about $5.2B (my reconstruction). Both still meet the end-2030 deadline in base and stress; every cell shows 2029. A 2030 launch passes. |
| Cash path of each bill (CEO ask 4) | Compare when each bill lands and whether two land together; show the base and stress net-debt path by year (inference: tracker reconstruction) | pass | Bills booked on the board: 10-year $60.32B at EIS 2040; 7-year $65.00B at 2037; Re-engine $5B at 2035 (+$1.2B strain beside the 10-year, 2033-35); Rate Increase $2.94B in 2032. Plan B carries one bill and nothing lands with it. Tracker paths: my reconstruction of the brief's rule (5%, 7% and a 14% peak in year 7 of 10; interior curve fitted) reproduces all seven stress cells in the brief within $0.05B. Plan B: spend 2030-39 of 3.0 / 4.2 / about 5 / about 6 / 6.6 / 7.2 / 8.4 (2036 peak) / 7.8 / about 6.3 / about 5.4. Base net debt 16.5 (2027), 6.7 (2028), then $0 every year from 2029; base cash after programme at least $7.1B a year. Stress net debt 18.3, 11.7, 3.3, then $0 every year from 2030; stress headroom at least $2.5B (2036). Plan C: spend 2030-36 of 5.2 / about 7.3 / about 9.3 / 10.7 / 12.5 (2034 peak, about 19% of the bill) / about 11.1 / about 8.8. Base net debt $0 from 2029; minimum cash after programme $3.9B (2034). Stress: $0 in 2030-33, a $0.7B peak at end-2034, about $0.2-0.5B at end-2035, $0 from 2036. So yes, it breaches the 2035 hold year. That follows from the brief alone too: the cell says 'no', and 2035 is the only hold year inside the spend window. The Rate Increase cuts the 2034 peak to $0.4B and clears it by 2035, which is the brief's 'yes ($0.4B)'. Neither plan needs new equity. B adds no debt, and C's stress gap of $0.7B is debt, small against the $10B of revolvers we keep undrawn. I plan no shareholder return in either plan; the tracker's surplus-to-owners rule is the Board's call. If the Board retained the 2030-33 stress surplus (about $11B in my reconstruction), the 7-year's gap would close in fact, though the GM scores the tracker as written. |
| Full cost of a slip or cancel | Cancel write-off = bill x years elapsed / development years, with the debt penalty staying; cost a slip once, in full (concessions, rework, learning curve, carrying cost) | not applicable | Nothing is launched and no cancel is in play; H5 binds on whatever we launch now. For scale, a cancel in the 2035 round would write off $30.16B on the 10-year (60.32 x 5/10) and $46.43B on the 7-year (65.00 x 5/7), and the penalty stays. A 7-year that slips three years lands at the 10-year's 2040 EIS carrying a bill $4.68B larger, before concessions and carrying cost. |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| fps | launch (10-year ramp-up) (fps engine code 7) | approve | Every test that touches this item passes. Item value is the submitted row 'fps 10-yr + 787: hold' (+3.50 / +0.66 / +0.88 / -1.96 / -0.78) minus the default row 'fps: hold + 787: hold' (0.00 / -3.64 / -2.62 / -6.26 / -3.64), which gives +3.50 / +4.30 / +3.50 / +4.30 / +2.86 for S1-S5. (1) Unresolved objection: none. Malave concurs, and his binding veto falls only on the via-Embraer rows, which are not ordered. Pope concurs, flags no red-line breach, and her KPI veto has nothing to bind on. (2) Business case: +4.30 in the CEO's expected S4, above ε ($1B). (3) Rating first: S5 is -0.78 against a floor of -5.64 (Do Nothing -3.64 less $2B), so $4.86B of headroom. (4) Downside: the lowest item value is +2.86 (S5). All five columns count as plausible and none falls below -$1B. (5) Boeing ready. Market passes. Technology: EIS 2040 is 2035 or later. Boeing: the buffer test and 'Fundable when the 777X turns' both pass, since the 777X is cash-positive from 2029 [BX-0547]. The CEO lifted H1 in writing with evidence: 777X first delivery in 2027 [BX-0550]; MAX 7/10 certification expected in 2026 [BX-1873], treated as done by inference; net debt at $0 by 2029 in the base case and by 2030 in the stress case. (6) Safety and production readiness: the brief reports no live quality, FAA or certification problem. Pope passes KPIs and strain ($0), and suppliers on condition. (7) One development at a time: 787 Re-engine is hold, so overlap is 0. (8) Premium: $0.46B, logged with its reason, under the $2B cap. Objective cell: 2029 / yes / yes ($0.0B) / 1 (3). Engine code 7 is a recommendation only. It is in no disallowed pair. | launch (10-year ramp-up); launch (7-year ramp-up); hold |

- Decision rationale: DECISION: fps launch (10-year ramp-up) with fps engine code 7; 737 rate hold; 787 Re-engine hold. This is the plan I framed (plan B), and both test memos recommend it.

1. MY RULINGS ON H1 AND H3 FOR 2030, IN WRITING, WITH EVIDENCE.
- H1 (no fps in Round 1) is LIFTED for 2030. Its 2026 grounds were the unfinished 777X and MAX 7/10 and an unrepaired balance sheet [B-2326, BX-1305], and all of them have closed:
  - the 777X was reset to first delivery in 2027 [BX-0550] and turns cash-positive from 2029 [BX-0547];
  - MAX 7/10 certification was expected in 2026 [BX-1873]. The brief reports no certification trouble, so I treat both as done (inference);
  - debt repair is complete on the GM's tracker: net debt reaches $0 at end-2029 (base) and end-2030 (stress), with no new programme;
  - in July 2026 I said we needed about two more years to be financially ready, with a launch expected around 2029-2030 [BG-0068];
  - the three work streams have converged [B-2303, BX-1296]. Market: the narrowbody demand is there, and Do Nothing loses $3.64-6.26B if Airbus moves. Boeing: finances repaired, and Pope confirms engineering can carry one clean-sheet development. Technology: GTF2 and UltraFan NB, if launched on selection, are ready in 2036 and 2037, and CFM is always ready.
  - The lift is conditional, and every condition has passed: Malave's buffer and fundability tests and Pope's readiness results.
- H3, first half (no 787 Re-engine in Round 1) no longer binds as a hard rule. Its ground was "the 777X comes first" [B-2276], and that has closed. I still HOLD the Re-engine on four grounds:
  - the Board's -$1B Downside limit: it adds -3.58 in S3 and S4;
  - my one-development-at-a-time rule [BX-1239];
  - company doctrine (profile §11.4: keep the 787 a cash source, no Re-engine before 2036);
  - the premium: holding it costs $0 in my expected column.
  H3's second half, H4, H5, H6 and H8 apply as written. H2 cannot bind (earliest EIS 2037). H7 has no trigger: the brief reports no certification trouble.

2. THE TEAM RULE.
- Malave's buffer test (slip leg = S5, the brief's Risk case; floor = Do Nothing's -3.64 less $2B = -5.64):
  - plan B passes at -0.78, $4.86B above the floor;
  - every via-Embraer row fails ($11.83-13.94B below the floor). That veto binds and I accept it.
- Pope's KPI veto does not bind: the brief reports no live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem. It is moot anyway, since I hold the rate.
- Everything else is my call.

3. THE GRID. Board items are the fps launch and the engine code that rides with it. 737 rate and 787 Re-engine sit at their default (hold).

| Plan | S1 | S2 | S3 | S4 (expected) | S5 (risk case) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Submitted: fps 10-yr + 787 hold | +3.50 | +0.66 | +0.88 | -1.96 | -0.78 |
| Same with fps at its default (Do Nothing) | 0.00 | -3.64 | -2.62 | -6.26 | -3.64 |
| Value of the launch | +3.50 | +4.30 | +3.50 | +4.30 | +2.86 |

- The launch beats Do Nothing by more than ε in every column, so debt-first passes. The grid already nets the discounted debt penalty; I do not subtract it again. Undiscounted, for scale only: 10-year $19.1B, 7-year $22.0B, via Embraer $52.5B.
- Worst column: -1.96 (S4). In no column does the item lose more than $1B against its default.

4. RAMP: 10-YEAR OVER 7-YEAR.
- Against the 7-year the 10-year is +1.30 / -0.46 / +1.30 / -0.46 / +0.05. It is ahead in three columns, more than ε ahead in two, and behind only by 0.46 in S2 and S4, inside ε. Its mean across the five columns is +0.46 against the 7-year's +0.11.
- In my expected S4 the 7-year is the best grid plan by $0.46B. That is a tie, and the team rule breaks it on doing less, better [BX-1239] and the higher-confidence plan [BX-0548]. Both point to the 10-year:
  - a $4.68B smaller bill and $2.9B less undiscounted debt penalty;
  - a 2036 cash peak of about $8.4B against about $12.5B in 2034;
  - every debt line kept, the stress case included (peak $0.0B). The 7-year borrows $0.7B under stress and is still above zero at the 2035 hold year;
  - three years of headroom against our clean-sheet slip record (787-8: May 2008 to September 2011 [B-0031, B-0526]), and no production-certification concurrency [BX-1263], which is my red line.
- The 7-year's S2/S4 edge and its incumbency in 2040 both exist only if it meets NGSA's 2037 EIS to the year. A three-year slip turns it into the 10-year airplane with a bigger bill. Airbus's timeline does not set our date [B-2313].
- Doctrine also points to the 10-year ramp: profile §11.4, "Ramp-up speed: the 10-year ramp".

5. OBJECTIVE LEDGER (Control's mission; it never changes the payoff).
- Debt: plan B reads 2029 / yes / yes ($0.0B) in every column. The 7-year misses the stress line in every column.
- Incumbency, by my sketch of the public share rules:
  - the 10-year holds 40% or more throughout in S1 and S3, but dips to about 37% in 2040 in S2 and S4 (about 32% in S5). It recovers to about 42% by 2045 and about 47% by 2050 on the 3-year lead rule;
  - the 7-year holds in S1-S4 and dips to about 38% in 2040 in S5.
- In every column the 10-year misses no more objectives than the 7-year: S2/S4 trade one miss for one, and in S1, S3 and S5 the 7-year misses more.
- 50/50 is out of reach for every plan.
- I weigh debt, which is on my own ranked list [BX-1305], above an incumbency gain that depends on a schedule our record says we would not hold.

6. 787 RE-ENGINE: hold.
- It adds +2.20 in S1, S2 and S5 and -3.58 in S3 and S4. That is a bet on an A350 choice we cannot see, and it fails the Board's -$1B Downside limit.
- In S4 holding it costs nothing.
- The cost I accept, unpriced: on the 10-year a 2035 Re-engine would overlap 5 years and break H4, so the next H4-legal round is 2045. Widebody growth comes from Charleston capacity [BX-1317], and the 787 stays a cash source. Doctrine, not my record, drives this.

7. RATE INCREASE: hold.
- It adds -0.74 in every column beside the launch, so it fails its business case. Its +2.30 alone needs fps on hold and S1 or S3, and even then it assumes neither new narrowbody is ever launched.
- This is the only round the board offers it, so the deferral is a loss I accept, not a wait.
- It is not a reply to anyone (H6).

8. ENGINE CODE 7: costs nothing on the board.
- On today's record the fitted source at a 2040 EIS is CFM (1).
- If P&W (GTF2, ready 2036) and Rolls-Royce (UltraFan NB, ready 2037) launch on selection this round, both are ready 4 and 3 years before EIS: three sources by 2050 and three narrowbody engine makers.
- Code 7 is in no disallowed pair. Doctrine and my Low-confidence lean were code 3; code 7 keeps CFM as the fallback and serves the supply-stability enabler.

9. PREMIUM (H8): $0.46B, logged.
- What it is: the 10-year against the best S4 plan (7-year), counted in S2 and S4.
- Why: doctrine (higher-confidence plan with headroom, doing less, no concurrency) plus objective (the stress-case debt line).
- Re-engine hold $0, rate hold $0, code $0. Total $0.46B, $1.54B inside the cap.
- What would break it: an expected column of S1, S2 or S5, where the Re-engine hold would read $2.20B. In that case the hold rests on the Board's veto and H-rules, not on doctrine.

10. DEBT PATH, plan B (Malave's reconstruction, matching the brief's cells within $0.05B):
- base net debt $16.5B (2027), $6.7B (2028), $0 every year from 2029;
- stress $18.3B, $11.7B, $3.3B, then $0 from 2030, with at least $2.5B of headroom (2036);
- programme cash: about $3.0B in 2030, rising to about $8.4B in 2036;
- no new equity and no shareholder return in the plan.
- Expected scenario: S4: Airbus launches NGSA and an A350 Re-engine this round. The doctrine fallback (profile §7) expects an early A350 Re-engine attempt and NGSA in Turn 1 or 2. S2 is close behind. S5 (NGSA plus covert Delay Tactics) is the risk case and Malave's slip leg.. Best grid plan there: In S4, fps 7-yr + 787: hold, at -1.50. My plan, fps 10-yr + 787: hold, is -1.96 there, $0.46B behind, which is inside ε. The tie goes to the 10-year: doing less and doing it better, and the higher-confidence plan. That $0.46B is logged as premium.. Premium +0.46 $B: $0.46B: the 10-year ramp against the best S4 grid plan (the 7-year ramp), in my expected column (and equally in S2).

Doctrine part: the higher-confidence plan, with three years of headroom against our clean-sheet slip record. It means no production-certification concurrency and a $4.68B smaller bill [BX-1239, BX-1241, BX-1275, BX-1263, BX-0548], and it follows profile §11.4 (the 10-year ramp).

Objective part: it keeps the stress-case debt line; the 7-year peaks at $0.7B of net debt at end-2034 and is still above zero at 2035.

The other orders cost nothing in S4:
- 787 Re-engine hold: $0 (the Re-engine adds -3.58 there);
- 737 rate hold: $0 (-0.74);
- engine code 7: $0 (codes do not change the payoff).

Total $0.46B, inside the $2B H8 cap.
- Predictions: Airbus:
- NGSA launch this round, likely, with a 2037 EIS.
- A350 Re-engine attempt early, about even odds (doctrine expects it), so S4 is my expected column and S2 is close.
- Delay Tactics: possible and covert. I will not see them before our EIS, and I will treat any slip as ours or the supplier's until exposed.

Engine makers:
- P&W (GTF2) and Rolls-Royce (UltraFan NB) likely launch on our code 7 selection, giving three sources by 2050.
- CFM likely launches a ducted engine and stays our baseline.

Outcome:
- Our share dips toward 37% around 2040 if NGSA enters in 2037, then climbs toward a 47/53 long-run split.
- Net debt stays at $0 in base and stress.

Next round (2035):
- No cancel (H5). If the plan moves, one conservative reset.
- The 787 Re-engine is H4-barred until 2045 on this ramp.
- I can change the engine code before EIS if a maker is not ready.
- Expected ΔPV −1.96 $B; GM valuation after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: Engine competition with CFM as the baseline (public); Certification plan with the FAA from day one, and no production-certification concurrency (public); No fixed-price development (public); Supplier readiness: keep fragile suppliers funded and hot (private); Balance sheet first in capital allocation (public); Stage-gated governance of the launch (private); Workforce: retain, train before we build, hire in stages (private); 737 MAX and 787 customer commitment (public)

**Airbus** (Guillaume Faury, Thomas Toepfer, Lars Wagner; Airbus Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|

Each Board item must gain in the CEO's expected column and lose no more than $2B in any column the bulletin has … |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NGSA | launch (NGSA engine code 7: CFM, P&W and Rolls-Royce) | approve | The Board approves this Qualified Majority item, a programme launch above EUR 800m, on the following record. The Board checked the brief's grid itself. - Unresolved objection: pass. Both veto checks concur, neither reports a red-line breach, there are no overrides and there is no revision. Wagner's wording note is not a veto. - Override review: not engaged. No override was used. - Liquidity and rating: pass, narrowly. $35.52B / 7 = $5.07B a year in 2030-2036 against the $5.2B line (EUR 4,753m at $1.10), with $0.13B of headroom and nothing running alongside. No new debt and no equity raise. - One clean sheet: pass. One launch this round, with zero overlap and zero strain. - Technology clock: pass. EIS 2030 + 7 = 2037, not before 2035, in the last year of the 2028-2030 window. - Downside: pass. The item value (the 'NGSA + A350: hold' row minus 'hold + hold') is +45.82 / +26.67 / +22.27 / +26.67 / +45.82 / +26.67 across S1-S6. It gains in the CEO's expected column, S1, and in every column the bulletin leaves open. The plan's worst column is S3 at +17.28. - Integrity: pass. Nothing is covert and Delay Tactics are none. - Safety and ramp: pass. Wagner passes the five pillars, quality, the A320 ramp shield, production readiness and A350F/Spirit absorption, and reports no supply crunch. - Returns kept fundable: passes through Liquidity. The CFO's FY2025-base caveat is taken up below as a recommendation. Under the plan-level rule it could never be a ground against NGSA on its clock. - Engine supply: code 7 keeps CFM and names a second and a third maker. It is in no barred pair. It is not a veto ground. | launch with NGSA engine code 6; launch with NGSA engine code 5; launch with any engine code (the Board never vetoes a code on its own); hold (the default; the Board would not veto it, but recommends against missing the window) |

- Decision rationale: QUESTION. 2030 is the last year of the 2028-2030 launch window. Do we launch NGSA on its technology clock, alone, with an engine code that rebuilds our engine-supply base, as one clean Board item?

STEP 1: DELETE RED LINES AND FIVE-PILLARS FAILURES.
- All six Delay Tactics rows are deleted under rule 4. Boeing has not launched fps on the public record, so any move would be naked and draw the $48.32B fine. 'both' is a second move. bottleneck is an integrity flag. Toepfer and Wagner both fail them, and the Board would veto them.
- NGSA + A350 Re-engine (+47.57 / +25.11 / +19.03 / +25.11 / +42.81 / +20.36) is deleted under rule 6. It is two new launches in one round with a 5-year overlap, and the $1B exception fails in S5 and S6 (-2.15).

STEP 2: SURVIVORS AGAINST THE TWO TESTS.
- NGSA hold + A350 hold (0.00 / -3.30 / -4.99 / -3.30 / -0.86 / -4.16) fails both. Toepfer's robustness test fails, Wagner's production-readiness test fails because the plan misses the window, and my NGSA clock test fails.
- NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine (+3.26 / -0.05 / -1.74 / -0.05 / -1.50 / -4.80) also fails both: Toepfer's robustness test (worst column S6 at -4.80) and Wagner's A350F/Spirit-absorption and production-readiness tests.
- NGSA launch + A350 hold + Delay Tactics none (+45.82 / +23.37 / +17.28 / +23.37 / +44.96 / +22.51) passes every test of both men. It is the only survivor, and it is also the company default.

STEP 3: CHOICE.
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold. In S1 the best grid plan is the deleted NGSA + A350 Re-engine row, $1.75B above the default. A red line is not traded for PV, so that gap is recorded, not paid for. The survivor is chosen.
- No other survivor lies within $1B, so the tie-breaks do not apply. The plan already scores on all five of them: no early EIS, no concurrency, the last possible capex start inside the window, EIS 2037 holding within 2035-2037, and one Board item.
- Robustness: the worst plausible column is S3 (fps 7-yr, EIS 2037, the same year as NGSA) at +17.28, positive. The decision is identical in every column. Nothing here is timed around Boeing.

STEP 4: OVERRIDES. None. No soft veto stands against the chosen plan. The override budget (one per game) is untouched.

ENGINE CODE: 7 (CFM, P&W, Rolls-Royce). This is Wagner's supply recommendation, taken as my agent file directs, and the Board's preferred code.
- It keeps CFM as the anchor and appears in no barred pair, so Boeing's code choice cannot force a change.
- It is the only code that can reach 3 sources / 3 makers in 2050. Without it, or if both makers hold, NGSA flies CFM alone (1 / 1).
- Only this round can trigger GTF2 (ready 2036) and UltraFan NB (ready 2037) in time for entry into service.
- On this board an unready maker is dropped, so the code carries no EIS risk and no payoff effect.
- Unpriced load: three engine integrations and certifications. It is mitigated by certifying in sequence with CFM first and by reviewing the code in 2035, only to remove a maker that has not launched or is off track.

BOARD-GATE LINE (one item: NGSA: launch, Qualified Majority; the engine code rides with it).
- Five pillars: pass. Nothing is covert and no flag was raised by Toepfer (Internal Control) or by Wagner.
- Toepfer's test: pass. Peak programme cash is $35.52B / 7 = $5.07B a year in 2030-2036 against $5.2B, with $0.13B headroom and no concurrent programme. Net cash and the A+/A1 rating hold with no new debt or equity. On the 2030 run-rate the payout stays inside 30-50%. On the board's books the $35.52B lands at EIS in 2037, and that charge is reported beside the cash view.
- Wagner's test: pass. Safety and quality pass, the A320 ramp shield passes, A350F absorption is untouched, and overlap and strain are both zero.
- Grid ΔPV: the item against hold is +45.82 / +26.67 / +22.27 / +26.67 / +45.82 / +26.67, a gain in every column. The plan row is +45.82 / +23.37 / +17.28 / +23.37 / +44.96 / +22.51, worst column S3 at +17.28.
- The item clears the Board's downside test (no loss in any column against a $2B limit), its liquidity test and its technology-clock test (EIS 2037, not before 2035).
- A350 Re-engine hold and Delay Tactics none are defaults, not items.

DELAY TACTICS: none. The team's stricter condition (the move must change who enters service first) stands. I do not set it aside. Delay Tactics will never appear in any public line.

A350 RE-ENGINE: on hold.
- Wagner's capacity test would first pass in 2035, at the 2-year overlap limit.
- Toepfer's peak-cash line moves the first passing round to 2045 on these bills (a 2035 launch runs at $6.67B a year in 2035-2036).
- It never comes after a 787 Re-engine (rule 3).

RECORD. Ordering principle in my framing: pillars, then ramp, then preparing the Airbus of the 30s, then whether the Board can take it [AX-0073, AX-0081, AX-0020, A-0409, AX-0026, AX-0010, AX-0054]. Confidence in my decision style is Low; the procedure follows the team rule and profile.md §9.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. Boeing has said a new aircraft comes 'not before 2035' and is still repairing its balance sheet, and the bulletin records no launch. S2 and S4 (fps 10-yr or via Embraer, EIS 2040) are close behind. The worst plausible column is S3 (fps 7-yr, EIS 2037, the same year as NGSA), where the plan still returns +17.28. The order is the same in every column.. Best grid plan there: NGSA + A350 Re-engine at +47.57 in S1. It is deleted for a company red line: rule 6, two new launches in one round with a 5-year overlap and -2.15 in S5 and S6. It also fails Toepfer's peak-cash test ($6.67B vs $5.2B) and Wagner's capacity and A350F tests, and the Board would veto it. The best surviving plan is NGSA + A350: hold + Delay Tactics none at +45.82 in S1. That is the company default and the plan ordered.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium is paid. The only higher row in S1 (NGSA + A350 Re-engine, +$1.75B) breaks red-line rule 6 and is deleted before the doctrine-default comparison, so the $1.75B is recorded, not paid as a premium. The chosen plan is the best surviving plan in every column bar the deleted ones. No PV is traded for the briefing goal or the engine-supply enabler: the engine code is payoff-neutral on this board, and holding NGSA to keep 2 / 2 engine sources would cost $22.27-45.82B, far beyond any premium allowed.
- Predictions: Boeing in 2030: most likely holds (S1, about 45%). Next most likely is an fps 10-yr or via Embraer (S2/S4, about 30% together), then an fps 7-yr (S3, about 10%) and a 787 Re-engine with or without fps (S5/S6, about 15%). A 737 Rate Increase is possible but would not change our programmes. Probability-weighted ΔPV on these weights is about +33.9B; in S1 it is +45.82B.

P&W: likely to answer our selection with GTF2 launch if selected, ready 2036 with one year of margin, so that it keeps its place on our narrowbody. A Joint Venture commitment with Rolls-Royce is possible.

Rolls-Royce: less certain. UltraFan NB launched now has zero margin to a 2037 entry into service. It may prefer the Joint Venture (ready 2036, counted as one source) or stay on widebody moves.

CFM: likely to launch a ducted engine or rely on a LEAP derivative. It is fitted on NGSA either way.

Board: approves NGSA: launch by Qualified Majority with code 7, and vetoes nothing in this package.

2035 bulletin: NGSA in development with engine-ready dates published. At the 2035 round the code is reviewed only to remove a maker that has not launched. NGSA is not re-timed for any Boeing move. The A350 Re-engine stays on hold on current bills (first passing round 2045, never after a 787 Re-engine).
- Expected ΔPV +45.82 $B; GM valuation after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Public invitation to engine makers to share risk on NGSA (public); Secure our own supply for the A320 family ramp (public); Certify NGSA engines in sequence, with CFM as the anchor (private); Engine-code review at the 2035 round (private); Payout guardrail during NGSA development (private); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy through industry coalitions (public)

| ducted engine | launch | approve | Passes every test. Item value is the package minus GEnx alone, with Embraer defaulted alongside because it needs the engine. S1 to S6: -6.00 / +10.61 / +13.05 / +10.17 / +12.85 / -1.38. - Paid for the risk: +10.61 in the expected column, S2, which is above the $1B margin. The defensive route also holds: +13.05 in S3, where a rival takes CFM's slot, and +4.96 weighted on Culp's weights (+6.55 equal, +4.13 on Ghai's). - Bounded downside: the worst column is S1 at -6.00. That equals its bill (the $4B R&D plus the $2B strain it triggers) and goes no further. - Safety and readiness: ready in 2036, the year after launch plus 6, ahead of a 2037 entry. Nothing rests on an earlier year. Ali raises no safety or durability flag. Culp's 20% fuel-burn line is rebutted in the rationale: a ducted engine gets less than half the open fan's gain, so it stands only as a defence and is never called next-generation. Durability is not traded. - Unresolved objection: Ghai and Ali concur, no binding veto is triggered and there are no overrides. - Rating and payout floor: $4B over 6 years plus $2B of strain over 6 is $1.0B a year, against about $2.5B of room. - Premium: $0, since the package is the best plan in S2. The round count is $0 of $2.0B, the game count $0 of $4.0B, and the RISE allowance $0 of $15B. - CFM parity rule (Safran consent): a defence that keeps both airframers' next aircraft on CFM, and the aftermarket the partners split equally, fits joint practice. The reason is stated, so consent is assumed (inference). No Safran position is asserted. | launch; hold |
| Embraer partnership | launch | approve | Passes. Item value is the package minus Ducted + GEnx. S1 to S6: 0.00 / +2.44 / +4.88 / +3.73 / +3.97 / +1.12. - Paid for the risk: +2.44 in S2, above zero. It adds no strain, since Ducted + GEnx already runs two projects, so its bill is $0. - Bounded downside: it is never below zero in any column. - Without GEnx (Ducted + Embraer against Ducted alone): S2 +0.43, and the worst column is S1 at -2.00, equal to the $2B strain it would add. It passes there too. - Needs a new CFM engine: the Ducted is launched and approved this round. - Dates: it counts from 2037, after the Ducted is ready in 2036. - Taken with GEnx against the Ducted alone: S1 -1.22, S2 +1.21, S3 +3.65, S4 +2.51, S5 +2.74, S6 -0.11. That is positive in S2 and never below the $2B strain charge. - CFM parity rule (Safran consent): a CFM engine offered to a third airframer fits 'serve every airframer', and the reason is stated. Consent is assumed (inference). | launch; hold |
| GEnx | GEnx upgrade | approve | Passes. Item value is the package minus Ducted + Embraer. S1 to S6: +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.77 / +0.77 / +0.78. - Paid for the risk: above zero in every column, net of strain. It adds none, because Ducted + Embraer already carries the $2B strain. - Dates: it counts from 2042, or from the first Re-engine's entry, and never on a year earlier than the rules allow. - It is GE alone, so there is no Safran gate. It sits below our real threshold and is reviewed with the package. - Dependency: its strain cover rests on Embraer. Both are approved, so no joint veto is needed. Beside a lone Ducted it would be -1.22 to -1.23 and would fail. | GEnx upgrade; GEnx investment; hold |

- Decision rationale: DECISION. Launch the Ducted, Partner Embraer and the GEnx upgrade (GEnx upgrade). Hold the Open Fan and Lobby. Nothing is cancelled. All five orders follow the frame, and both memos recommend the same five.

WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]. The game is the installed base on the next narrowbody [CX-0422], not share for its own sake [CX-0493]. Both new narrowbodies, fps and NGSA, are open, and orders are sealed. Hold loses -32.07 to -105.01 in every column where an airframer launches. S3 (NGSA, code 2) and S6 (codes 1 and 2) are the columns where a rival selection takes CFM's slot. "We don't have a birthright on that next order" [CX-0564].

TESTS
(1) Additive and paid for the risk [CX-0301]. The package, Ducted + Partner Embraer + GEnx upgrade, is the best grid plan in S2 to S5: S2 -20.69, S3 -71.86, S4 -50.10, S5 -48.96. Its S1 cost is named: -5.22 against hold, or -6.00 against GEnx alone, which is the $4B bill plus the $2B strain and no more. The risk is worth it because the package is worth +10.61 to +13.05 wherever an airframer launches, and the loss is capped at the bill when none does.

(2) Low case first [CX-0248, CX-0536]. The expected column is S2: Airbus launches NGSA and keeps today's dual-source A320neo pattern (code 6). That is the record's pattern, not a hoped-for selection. S2 sits below S1 for every plan, so it books low.

(3) Hard rule 3. The trigger is the grid's slot-taking columns, because no rival engine is launched, committed or selected on the record. S3 is the column that pays it: the Ducted is worth +10.17 against hold, and the package +13.05 against GEnx alone. S6 names the trigger but does not pay it (-0.49, or -1.38). Ghai accepts the trigger through S3, not S6.

(4) My 20% line, rebutted. The 20% line [CX-0217] binds the next-generation product, which is RISE. The ducted engine has "less than half the fuel burn improvements" of an open fan [CX-0183; Ali, CX-0180]. It cannot meet 20%, and it is not offered as the next generation. It is a defence of the installed base under hard rule 3, and it is never called next-generation in public. The durability line [CX-0208] stands: Ali raises no durability or safety flag, and every design gate is cross-functional [CX-0012].

(5) Supply. Two or more projects draw the $2B strain. The grid pays it in S2 to S5. Ali gives no supply 'no', on condition that GEnx is paced behind the Ducted's peak.

WHY NOW, NOT AFTER THE 2035 BULLETIN. Launched now, the Ducted is ready in 2036 against a 2037 entry (NGSA or a 7-year fps) and a 2040 entry (10-year fps or the Embraer route). Launched in 2035, it is ready only in 2041. Ghai sizes the cost of waiting at about $4.3B in S2 and S3, $4.5B in S5 and $1.1B in S4. Waiting saves $0.49B in S6, and in S1 it saves up to $4.00B. Net edge for launching now: +$1.06B to +$1.26B on my weights, +$0.64B to +$0.88B on his. Waiting wins only once P(S1) passes about 0.45. Ghai's numbers show deferring Embraer or GEnx to 2035 would still draw the strain while the Ducted is in development, so it saves nothing.

THE STACK, AND MY OWN CONDITION MISSED. I said the add-ons had to clear Ducted alone by more than $1B on my weights. They clear it by $0.95B, missing by $0.05B: inside ε. Elsewhere they clear it by +$1.21B in S2, +$1.46B on equal weights and +$0.83B on Ghai's. They lose $1.22B in S1 and $0.11B in S6. So the tie-break decides, "safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order" [CX-0658]:
- Safety: a tie. No flag on either plan.
- Quality: a tie on the evidence. The grid charges the load at $2B, and Ali has no test or field data either way. I don't invent a flag. I take his pacing condition and the supplier watch.
- Delivery: the package wins. It puts a CFM engine in front of a third airframer and keeps the 787 on product (H1: "on all the critical platforms" [CX-0587]).
It is decided at delivery, with cost (+$0.95B weighted, best plan in S2) confirming it. GEnx never sits beside a lone Ducted (-1.22 to -1.23 in every column).

HELD
- Open Fan: launched now it is ready in 2045, the same as a 2035 launch. It waits in every launch column, at -$8.00B to -$57.60B against hold (H2) [CX-0205]. RISE stays whole as technology (H5) [CX-0174, CX-0214].
- Lobby: does nothing without an Open Fan.

BOARD ITEMS, BY COLUMN, S1 / S2 / S3 / S4 / S5 / S6
- Submitted package: -5.22 / -20.69 / -71.86 / -50.10 / -48.96 / -105.61. Against Do Nothing in S2 that is +11.38; the worst column against Do Nothing is S1, at -5.22.
- ducted (package minus GEnx alone, Embraer defaulted with it): -6.00 / +10.61 / +13.05 / +10.17 / +12.85 / -1.38. Weighted +4.96. The worst is S1 at -6.00, equal to its $6B bill.
- Embraer partnership (package minus Ducted + GEnx): 0.00 / +2.44 / +4.88 / +3.73 / +3.97 / +1.12. Weighted +2.18. It never goes below zero.
- genx (package minus Ducted + Embraer): +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.78 / +0.77 / +0.77 / +0.78.

SAFRAN GATE (H7, CFM parity rule)
- ducted engine: consent assumed, because it keeps both airframers' next aircraft on CFM and the aftermarket the partners split equally. A launch ahead of a committed airframe is not shown in joint practice, hence the stated reason.
- Embraer partnership: consent assumed, as a CFM engine for a third airframer ('serve every airframer').
- Open Fan and emissions lobbying: held, so no ask.
- GEnx: GE alone, no gate.
No Safran position is asserted [CX-0184].

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD. These calls rest on doctrine:
- Embraer: no record; the NMA 'additive' rule [CX-0301] applied by inference.
- Lobby: profile §6.
- Supply load: Ali's seat doctrine, checked against the strain rule.
- The GEnx call: inference [CX-0599, CX-0615].

2035 REVIEW. If the bulletin shows no airframer launch and no rival engine, I reread the Ducted: a cancel then writes off $3.33B and saves $0.67B, and a reversal is in character. Ghai's numbers favour keeping it if an airframer launches in 2035. The Open Fan product call is made then, on Ali's test gates.

ExCo. Culp proposed and decided; Ghai and Ali concur on all five orders. No binding veto is triggered, so there are no overrides.
- Expected scenario: S2: Airbus launches NGSA and selects CFM alongside P&W (code 6), continuing today's dual-source A320neo pattern; Boeing holds this round. Picked from the public record (no launch, no rival engine on it, the A320neo is dual-source today), not from a hoped-for selection, and it sits below S1 for every plan, so it books low. S1 carries the largest single weight (0.30), but the launch columns together carry 0.70 and S2 is the likeliest of them.. Best grid plan there: Ducted + Partner Embraer + GEnx upgrade, at -20.69 in S2. That is +11.38 against hold (-32.07), +10.61 against GEnx alone (-31.30) and +1.21 against Ducted alone (-21.90). It is the plan chosen, so the premium is nil. It is also the best grid plan in S3, S4 and S5, and the weighted best on my weights (-36.84), on Ghai's (-36.07) and on equal weights (-50.41).. Premium +0.00 $B: $0.00. The chosen package is the best grid plan in the expected column, S2 (-20.69), so no PV is given up against it.
- Doctrine premium: $0.00.
- Objective premium: $0.00.
- Running count: this round $0.0 of $2.0B; the game $0.0 of $4.0B.
- RISE allowance, stated apart: $0 of $15B, unspent. No Open Fan launch this round.
For reference: Ducted alone would have carried $1.21B. On an S1 reading the package gives up $6.00B against GEnx alone, over the cap. I do not book that, because S1 is not my expected column, and on my stated weights the package is the weighted best (-36.84 against GEnx alone at -41.80).
- Predictions: Column weights, summing to 1.00: S1 .30, S2 .25, S3 .10, S4 .10, S5 .15, S6 .10.

Weighted values:
- Plans: package (Ducted + Embraer + GEnx) -36.84; Ducted + Embraer -37.62; Ducted -37.79; GEnx alone -41.80; hold -42.58.
- Board items: ducted +4.96 (against GEnx alone); Embraer partnership +2.18; genx +0.78.

- On equal weights: package -50.41, GEnx alone -56.96; ducted item +6.55.

Breakevens, with the rest of the weight spread pro rata:
- The package falls below GEnx alone at P(S1) of about 0.62.
- Launching now beats waiting for the 2035 bulletin until P(S1) is about 0.45-0.49 (Ghai's estimate).

Reading, private and low case first:
- At least one airframer launches this round: 0.70. NGSA is the likelier launch, given Airbus's 60% share and the 3 pp a year it gains if alone; Boeing's fps is less likely this round.
- A selection that keeps CFM on the airframe, as in today's pattern, is likelier than one that drops us. The slot-taking columns (S3 and S6) carry 0.20 together.
- No rival engine is on the record. A rival engine launch tied to a selection is possible and would show in the 2035 bulletin.
- To watch in 2035: launches and engine codes, rival NB engine launches, Joint Venture commitments, any Re-engine (it moves the GEnx count year), and the state of the RISE tests.

Expected column S2. Package value there: -20.69. Booked at -21.0, low.
- Expected ΔPV −21.00 $B; GM valuation after the round −62.61 $B

- Other moves: Offer a CFM engine to every airframer on every new narrowbody (public); CFM partner consultation on the Ducted and Embraer launches (private); Supply-base readiness for a three-project stack (FLIGHT DECK reviews) (private); Cross-functional design and test gates on the Ducted (private); Keep RISE funded as technology and set the gates for the 2035 product call (private); Fund the package inside the capital allocation framework (private)
