| Joint Venture technical conditions (applied to Joint Venture with RR: commit, options D, E, F) | Commit only once Rolls-Royce is on the public record or a live airframe carries code 1, 4, 5 or 7; Joint Venture engine ready by the EIS served; gear and core kept. | fail | No RR commitment on the record and no RR-code airframe live: the conditions fail, so I propose hold. Readiness for the record: RR commits 2030 and we commit 2035, so it forms 2035 and is ready 2041. That serves no 2030 airframe (2037, 2040), only a 2035 launch (EIS 2042 or 2045). With a 2030 GTF2 folded in, it is ready 2036. Not one of my veto grounds; the Board vetoes commit this round anyway. |

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GTF2 Solo | launch if selected | approve | Every test passes. I recomputed each one against the brief's grid: row 'GTF2 Solo if selected' against row 'GTF2: hold'. (1) Unresolved objection: PASS. Mitchill concurs, with no veto on this order. His binding ground-4 veto on a Joint Venture commit is respected through Joint Venture with RR: hold, and his conditional ground-2 veto on an unconditional launch does not apply because none is submitted. Eddy concurs and invokes neither ground. No red-line flag is raised, and overrides is empty. (2) Return hurdle: PASS. The item value by column, S1-S7, is 0 / +50.49 / 0 / +22.16 / +49.76 / 0 / 0. Weighted, that is +13.69 at Mitchill's weights (0.30/0.20/0.20/0.05/0.05/0.10/0.10) and +16.20 at Calio's. Both are positive at a 10% discount rate, so the item clears the hurdle and beats a buyback of the same $2B. (3) Committed airframe: not applicable, because this is the conditional form our doctrine prefers. Calio declares shared-source doctrine relief for codes 6 and 7 in rationale section 9. (4) Bounded downside: PASS. The minimum item value is 0.00, which is never below hold and well inside the $2B bill. (5) Proof before commitment: PASS. The value in S2, S4 and S5 is the order's own trigger, not upside booked in advance. The booked number is $0 in S1. The off-grid code-switch case is not booked. (6) Durability first: PASS. Ready in 2036, against NGSA 2037, fps 7-year 2037 and fps 10-year or Embraer 2040. Under the rules the order fires only if GTF2 is ready by the selecting airframe's EIS. Eddy passes both of his grounds and requires that the date moves, not the testing. (7) Dividend and envelope: PASS. Spend is $0 unless the order fires, then $0.33B a year (5.6% of the ~$6B envelope). No stretch is used, the dividend is covered and debt stays on its pre-ASR path. (8) Declared premium: not needed, because the item value is positive (Board premium 0). Calio's $0.26B is a doctrine premium against an unconditional launch, inside the $1B cap. (9) Miss a cycle: satisfied. | launch if selected; hold |

- Decision rationale: DECISION: `GTF2 Solo: launch if selected`, `Joint Venture with RR: hold`. Eddy proposed it, Mitchill recommends it, and the Board approves it at any weights. Overrides: none.

1. CONSTRAINT FIRST: safety and durability [PX-0035][PX-0073]
- Eddy's readiness check binds me, and it passes. GTF2 launched in 2030 is ready in 2036:
  - NGSA EIS 2037: 1 year of margin;
  - fps 7-year EIS 2037: 1 year;
  - fps 10-year or via Embraer EIS 2040: 4 years.
- A 2035 trigger would be ready in 2041, too late for any 2030 airframe. Only a 2030 order can answer a 2030 selection.
- One year of margin is thin. Eddy's own certification slipped from mid-2024 to 2025, and my operating dates have slipped by between one quarter and two years [PX-0017][PX-0098][PX-0104]. So I discount my own dates and take his condition: the durability programme stays intact, and if anything gives, the date moves, not the testing.
- Parts: the bulletin shows no live fleet, quality or supply problem. As Eddy says, this passes on the board, not on data. GTF2 test hardware and the ramp are ring-fenced away from the AOG recovery and kit insertion. The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080].

2. BINDING VETOES, all respected
- Mitchill, ground 4, binding: no `Joint Venture with RR: commit`. Rolls-Royce is not on the record and no live airframe carries an RR code. I hold the Joint Venture.
- Mitchill's conditional ground-2 veto on `launch` is not invoked at his weights (+$0.34B edge with the corrected cancel). It does not bite, because I do not submit `launch`.
- Eddy: no binding veto. Both his grounds pass for `launch if selected`.
- The `overrides` field is empty.

3. THE GRID, rows S1-S7 ($B)
- `launch if selected`: 0 / +22.73 / -27.76 / +19.31 / +22.00 / -27.76 / 0.
- Do Nothing (`hold`): 0 / -27.76 / -27.76 / -2.85 / -27.76 / -27.76 / 0.
- Board item, `launch if selected` minus `hold`: 0 / +50.49 / 0 / +22.16 / +49.76 / 0 / 0. It is never negative.
- `launch` minus `launch if selected`: -2.00 / 0 / +3.79 / 0 / 0 / +3.79 / -2.00.

4. EXPECTED VALUES
- My weights: S1 0.28 / S2 0.22 / S3 0.17 / S4 0.05 / S5 0.08 / S6 0.10 / S7 0.10. I blended my frame prior toward Mitchill's timing read.

| Plan | My weights | Mitchill's weights |
|---|---:|---:|
| `launch if selected` + `hold` | +0.23 | -1.72 |
| `launch` + `hold` | +0.49 | -1.38 |
| Do Nothing | -15.97 | -15.41 |
| `launch if selected` + commit | +0.03 | |

- `launch if selected` against `hold`: +$16.20B at my weights, +$13.69B at Mitchill's.

5. THE HEDGE TEST
- Edge of `launch` over `launch if selected` = 3.79 × (pS3 + pS6) − 2.00 × (pS1 + pS7):

| Weights | Edge ($B) |
|---|---:|
| Mine | +0.26 |
| Mitchill's | +0.34 |
| My frame prior | +0.25 |
| Equal | +0.51 |

- At my weights and at Mitchill's, the edge is positive, so it clears Mitchill's ground 2. It is below the Board's +$0.5B franchise-defence bar, which only equal weights reach, just barely. Even crediting a 2035 cancel in S1 and S7, it reaches only +$0.39B (mine) and +$0.47B (Mitchill's). The Board wants the launch to stand without the option.
- Its case rests on S3 and S6 alone. S2, S4 and S5 are identical for both orders.
- The Board would veto `launch`, and I have no override against the Board. So `launch if selected` is both the doctrine order and the best plan that passes.

6. RESPONSIBLE BET: premium declared [PX-0010]
- Doctrine premium: $0.26B at my weights ($0.34B at Mitchill's). That is the PV I give up against the unconditional `launch` to keep the 'no new engine without a committed airframer' rule [P-0746][PX-0109][PX-0009].
- Objective premium: $0, explained in section 7.
- Total: $0.26B, inside the $1B cap.
- No Board item trails `hold`, so no Board premium is needed.

7. OBJECTIVE
- `launch if selected` and `launch` meet Restore credibility in the same columns: S2, S4 and S5 (p = 0.35).
- `launch if selected` meets Capitalise (ΔPV ≥ 0) in S1, S2, S4, S5 and S7 (p = 0.73). `launch` meets it only in S2, S4 and S5, because S1 and S7 come in at -2.00.
- So the objective does not favour `launch` on the grid. The off-grid chance of answering a 2035 NGSA switch to a P&W code is not booked (Mitchill's ground 4, confirmed).

8. MISS-A-CYCLE TEST [PX-0071][P-1347]
- Losing our half of the A320neo slot costs -27.76. `launch if selected` answers any 2030 selection in time.
- 'Miss a cycle' beats 'cross that bridge' only on visible demand. `launch if selected` makes the spend coincide with that demand.
- 'Miss a cycle' was said of RTX's businesses in general; applying it to GTF2 is inference.

9. SHARED-SOURCE DOCTRINE RELIEF, declared
- `launch if selected` fires on codes 6 and 7 as well as 2. Every selection column on this grid is shared: S2 (code 6), S4 (code 7), S5 (codes 7 and 6).
- I accept shared source against the sole-source rule [P-0746] as declared doctrine relief. The company accepts 40-50% of a shared platform [P-1292], and we aimed for about 40% of the A320neo [PX-0058].
- On NGSA it is also franchise defence (inference).

10. CANCEL TEST
- I adopt Mitchill's correction. For a triggered GTF2 that later loses its code: cancel in 2035 only if no live new narrowbody carries a P&W code, and only if the 2035 hold row does not beat the cancel row. The write-off is $1.67B and the cancel saves $0.33B.
- I reject the literal test 'cancel if no selection'.

11. DIVIDEND AND DEBT [PX-0081][P-0669][P-0686]
- Nothing is spent unless an airframer selects us. Then the cost is about $0.33B a year from 2030 to 2035, about 5.6% of the ~$6B envelope.
- On the analyst baseline, this competes with buybacks only. The dividend is covered, net debt is below 1x EBITDA by 2030E, and no envelope stretch is needed.

12. VALUE, NOT SHARE
- Standard terms. The Hot Section kit is charged. No concession in other moves or in the statement [PX-0055][PX-0100].

13. JOINT VENTURE
- `hold`. Revisit in 2035 on the trigger written into other moves.
- I have no record of my own here, so the view is doctrine plus Mitchill's and Eddy's tests.

14. BIASES, displayed
- Optimism on dates: I state no EIS, and I give no ready year before launch + 6.
- Incrementalism: I assumed the next single-aisle 'mid-next decade' [PX-0093]. Its failure mode, missing a round-1 selection, is closed by `launch if selected`.
- Supply-first caution: this is partly why I keep `launch if selected` over `launch`. Its cost is declared as the $0.26B premium.
- Architecture loyalty: GTF2 first, Joint Venture on hold. That is consistent with the unmet conditions, but flagged.

15. THIN RECORD
- I have made no launch decision, so my launch thresholds are inference (Medium).
- 'Miss a cycle' and 'demand visibility' are RTX-wide statements.
- My Joint Venture view is Low and rests on doctrine.
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold (0.28), narrowly ahead of S2, Airbus launches NGSA and selects P&W on code 6 (0.22). Nothing is launched or selected on the record, and Mitchill's first read of any launch date is timing.. Best grid plan there: In S1, the best plan is GTF2: hold at +0.00, tied with GTF2 Solo if selected at +0.00, which does not fire because there is no selection. Our orders therefore take the best value in the expected column. They also keep +22.73 in S2, +19.31 in S4 and +22.00 in S5, where `hold` loses 27.76, 2.85 and 27.76. The unconditional `launch` would give -2.00 in S1.. Premium +0.26 $B: This is a doctrine premium, not a Board-item premium.
- `launch if selected` trails the unconditional `launch` by its expected edge: 3.79 × (pS3 + pS6) − 2.00 × (pS1 + pS7) = +$0.26B at my weights (+$0.34B at Mitchill's).
- I keep it for the red line 'no new engine without a committed airframer' [P-0746][PX-0109][PX-0009]. The edge also fails the Board's +$0.5B franchise-defence bar, so the Board would veto `launch`.
- Objective premium: $0. Both orders meet Restore credibility in S2, S4 and S5, and `launch if selected` meets Capitalise in more columns.
- Total: $0.26B, inside the $1B cap.
- Against `hold`, `launch if selected` is worth +$16.20B at my weights, so no Board premium is needed (the Board's premium for this item is 0).
- Predictions: Column probabilities (sum 1.00): S1 0.28, S2 0.22, S3 0.17, S4 0.05, S5 0.08, S6 0.10, S7 0.10. Expected column: S1, rivals hold.

Reading:
- Airbus is the likelier mover. An NGSA in 2030 with a 2037 EIS fits my own 'mid-next decade' view of the next single-aisle [PX-0093]. Total p(NGSA launched) = 0.57 across S2, S3, S5 and S6.
- If it launches, I expect a shared code with CFM, which is how the A320neo flies today. Pr(P&W on NGSA | NGSA) is about 0.53.
- Boeing's fps is less likely this round, at 0.23 across S4, S5 and S6.
- Rolls-Royce committing to the Joint Venture in 2030 is 0.10.

At these weights:
- `launch if selected` + `hold` = +0.23;
- `launch` = +0.49;
- Do Nothing = -15.97.
- Edge of `launch` over `launch if selected`: +0.26.

If GTF2 is triggered, I expect it ready in 2036 and no earlier. I discount my own dates, so I state no EIS.
- Expected ΔPV +0.23 $B; GM valuation after the round +3.37 $B

- Other moves: Public conditional GTF2 offer on standard terms (public); GTF Hot Section durability kit insertion continues across the fleet (public); Ring-fence the fleet's parts and engineering from GTF2 (private); 2035 cancel test written into the plan (Mitchill's corrected form) (private); Joint Venture review trigger for 2035 (private)

Board review:

| Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| UltraFan NB Solo | launch if selected | approve | Every test that touches the item passes. I checked the grid against the brief: it matches the package. (1) ExCo process. McCabe co-signs `launch if selected` in her test memo and again at the veto check (concur, no binding veto). Watson concurs and invokes no Civil veto. The CFO's withheld co-signatures cover orders the CEO did not give. No red-line flag was raised at the veto check, and the Control breach is advice, stated openly in the rationale. `overrides` is empty. Pass [RX-0213]. (2) Safety and maturity. The conditional form passes by construction: under the rules it launches only if a live airframe selects RR this round and the engine is ready by that airframe's EIS. Launched in 2030, it is ready in 2037. That is zero margin against an NGSA or fps 7-year (EIS 2037) and 3 years against an fps 10-year or fps via Embraer (2040). Watson calls the zero margin a named pass, and test pacing is not compressed. I judged his grounds against the final orders and statement. The statement gives no EIS earlier than launch + 7 and names no airframer date. The partner-shop support plan is in the package (agreements by 2034, a shop and an EIS support team by 2036), and the statement commits to support before entry into service. No Civil ground stands. Pass [RG-0027, RG-0025, R-1476, R-0970, RX-0296, RX-0283]. (3) Airframe on the record. This is the airframe-first form the doctrine prefers: nothing is spent without a selection. Pass [R-1005, R-0994, RX-0058]. (4) Profit over share. Against "UltraFan NB: hold", the "UltraFan NB if selected" row is, S1-S9: 0 / +32.60 / 0 / +28.24 / +44.54 / 0 / 0 / 0 / 0. That is ≥ 0 in every column, so the weighted value is ≥ 0 under any weights. On McCabe's weights (0.30/0.10/0.15/0.10/0.05/0.10/0.10/0.05/0.05) it is +8.31. No premium (`premium` 0). Pass [R-1011, R-1561, R-1582]. (5) Reasonable worst case. The worst plausible column is 0, against the item's own -$8B bill. Pass [RG-0033]. (6) One big programme at a time. `UltraFan WB` and `Trent 1000 upgrade` are at hold, so strain is $0 in every column and there is no overlap. Pass [R-0929, R-1127, RX-0214]. (7) Balance sheet and position weakening. Not applicable. McCabe invokes no balance-sheet veto. If the order triggers, about £0.84bn a year is funded from free cash flow, buybacks give way first, the dividend is untouched, no equity or borrowing is needed and net cash stays positive [RX-0242, RG-0041]. Control's $4B bound is breached at $8.0B in S2, S4 and S5. The breach is stated with its reason. It is scored by Control, not a veto ground, and it arises only on a selection. Approve. | launch if selected; hold |

- Decision rationale: DECISION (step 3, round 1, 2030). My proposal stands unchanged: `UltraFan NB Solo: launch if selected`, with `Joint Venture with P&W`, `UltraFan WB` and `Trent 1000 upgrade` at hold. Both memos recommend exactly these orders. McCabe co-signs. Watson invokes no Civil veto. Neither memo raises a binding veto against the package, and no company red line is breached, so nothing is struck and `overrides` stays empty.

1. GRID, WITH AND WITHOUT EACH BOARD ITEM (brief, S1-S9).
- Only one Board item: `launch if selected`. "UltraFan NB if selected" against "UltraFan NB: hold": 0 / +32.60 / 0 / +28.24 / +44.54 / 0 / 0 / 0 / 0. It is never below hold, so it passes the profit test under any weights.
- Worst column 0, unselected loss 0, against an $8B bill.
- Weights (mine, confirmed by McCabe): 0.30 / 0.10 / 0.15 / 0.10 / 0.05 / 0.10 / 0.10 / 0.05 / 0.05. Nothing is on the record, so each selection column gets 0.1, and S5 (both select us) gets 0.05.
- Expected gain: 0.1 x 32.60 + 0.1 x 28.24 + 0.05 x 44.54 = +8.31.
- Sensitivity (McCabe): at a per-airframer selection weight of 0.15 it is +12.47; at 0.05, +4.16; at 0.036, +2.99. It falls under the $3B cap only below about 0.036.

2. WHY CONDITIONAL, NOT UNCONDITIONAL.
- The unconditional "UltraFan NB Solo" is worth +5.20 expected, with a worst column of -8.00 (the full bill) in S1, S7, S8 and S9.
- Against the conditional form it gains +3.57 only in S3 and S6, at a combined weight of 0.25. It loses 8.00 in S1, S7, S8 and S9, at a combined weight of 0.50. Net: -3.11.
- It would win only if w(S3) + w(S6) > 2.24 x (w(S1) + w(S7) + w(S8) + w(S9)). Here that is 0.25 against 1.12.
- It also breaks the airframe-first red line [RX-0058, R-1005]. It is a Board veto, and McCabe withholds her co-signature.
- Timing: a 2035 launch is ready in 2042 and misses every airframe launched in 2030. This round's conditional order is the only route into one.

3. REJECTED ROWS (expected gain over hold; worst column).
- "if selected + commit to JV": +7.91; -4.00 in S7. A first commit with P&W not on the record: Board veto, red line, co-signature withheld.
- "hold + T1000 upgrade": -0.91; -0.98. Negative in every column. It counts only from the first Re-engine's entry into service, or 2042.
- "if selected + T1000 upgrade": +6.15; -5.98 against A in S2, S4 and S5. That is $5B of strain, more than the upgrade's own bill. Red line and Board veto.
- "hold + UltraFan WB": -3.69; -4.00. No Re-engine is on the record, and the shortfall is far above the Board's $1B cap for UltraFan WB.
- Our profile default of funding the Trent 1000 upgrade in round 1 is reversed on this board. I declare no premium for it and park it. Watson agrees: time on wing carries on in the fleet budget. That is doctrine applied to this grid, not my record.

4. PREMIUM: $0 on every item. The plan is the PV-best grid plan in expectation.
- Holding everything to stay inside Control's $4B line would be an objective premium of $8.31B ($4.16B at half weights). That is above the $3B cap, so I do not take it.

5. CONTROL'S METRICS FOR THE SUBMITTED PLAN, BY COLUMN (own NB money / strain / RR WB share 2050 / RR NB share 2050).
- S1: $0 / $0 / 58.0% / 0%
- S2: $8.0B / $0 / 58.0% / 36.7%
- S3: $0 / $0 / 58.0% / 0%
- S4: $8.0B / $0 / 58.0% / 40.5%
- S5: $8.0B / $0 / 58.0% / 51.2%
- S6 to S9: $0 / $0 / 58.0% / 0%
- Expected own NB exposure: 0.25 x $8B = $2.0B.
- STATED BREACH: in S2, S4 and S5 our own narrowbody money is $8.0B against the $4B bound. I accept it for three reasons: the trigger is a selection, the grid pays +28.24 to +44.54 there, and no other order reaches an airframe launched in 2030 (the Joint Venture is ready 2041 at the earliest on our side).
- McCabe confirms the breach cannot be cured. The earliest exit is 2035, when spend to date is already $5.7B.
- Strain is $0 in every column. WB share is 58%, above the 50% line, everywhere.

6. PATH TO A SHARED PROGRAMME, NEXT ROUND.
- If the order does not trigger and P&W commits on the record: I commit to the Joint Venture at our $4B share, inside the bound, with McCabe's co-signature on an expected gain above 0.
- If it triggers and P&W commits: a 2035 Joint Venture would fold the Solo in, with $5.71B spent to date, $1.71B written off and shares equalised with P&W. Control's metric would still read $5.7B. McCabe's prior is that the fold loses PV against keeping a selected Solo. I propose a fold only if that round's fold row beats keeping the Solo, and I will show the grid first.
- Once the order has triggered, any later widebody programme draws the $5B strain whatever its dates. Answering a Re-engine will therefore cost more, and I record that now.

7. FUNDING AND HURDLE (McCabe, co-signed).
- If triggered: $1.14B, about £0.84bn, a year for 2030-37, about £5.9bn in total. That is 22-23% of FY2026 free cash flow guidance and roughly the whole company-funded R&D line (£0.6-0.9bn a year, MS IS r129).
- It is new money above a flat envelope. I accept her advice on that, but it is not a sign-off ground.
- Funded from free cash flow. Buybacks give way first, and the dividend is untouched.
- No equity, no borrowing for distributions, leverage nowhere near 1.5x, net cash throughout.
- The triggered columns clear mid-to-high-teens hurdles: an implied IRR of about 30-45%. That is her inference.

8. MATURITY AND SUPPORT (Watson, no veto).
- A 2030 UltraFan NB is ready in 2037.
- Against an NGSA or fps 7-year launched in 2030 (entry into service 2037) the margin is zero. That is a named pass, not a veto. Test pacing is not compressed: if gearbox testing needs longer, the date moves, not the test plan.
- Against an fps 10-year or fps via Embraer (2040) the margin is 3 years.
- The trigger fires only if we are ready by entry into service, so the order passes by construction.
- Support plan carried in this package: partner MRO agreements on the Singapore model by 2034; the first partner shop and an EIS support team in place by 2036; high-performing suppliers only.
- The statement says "no earlier than seven years from launch" and "support in place before entry into service". It gives no airframer date.

9. RED-LINE CHECK ON FINAL ORDERS.
- No unconditional launch.
- No commit before P&W.
- No widebody programme beside a narrowbody order.
- No early date disclosed.
- No orphan programme: the conditional order spends nothing unless an airframe uses it.
- No equity.
- No item can lose more than its own bill in any column.
- All clear.

"I don't like options management": this is a choice with a trigger, not an option held open. It costs nothing until an airframer goes first.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (weight 0.30, the single largest column). Nothing is on the record, and I do not expect an airframer or P&W to move first in 2030. Selection columns carry 0.1 each (S2, S4) and 0.05 for S5.. Best grid plan there: In S1, "UltraFan NB: hold" is best at +0.00. "UltraFan NB if selected" ties it at +0.00, since nothing is spent untriggered. Across the weighted columns, "UltraFan NB if selected" is the best plan at +8.31 and is never below hold. That is the plan submitted. Next best: "if selected + commit to JV" +7.91, "if selected + T1000 upgrade" +6.15, "UltraFan NB Solo + commit to JV" +5.60, "UltraFan NB Solo" +5.20.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium. The submitted plan is the PV-best grid plan in expectation (+8.31 at the stated weights) and ties the best plan in S1. The only objective-driven alternative is holding everything to keep own narrowbody money within Control's $4B line. That would cost $8.31B expected, above the $3B cap, so it is not taken. The Control breach in S2, S4 and S5 is stated openly instead.
- Predictions: Column weights: S1 0.30, S2 0.10, S3 0.15, S4 0.10, S5 0.05, S6 0.10, S7 0.10, S8 0.05, S9 0.05.

Most likely is S1: no airframer launches and P&W does not commit first. If an airframer launches in 2030, I expect it to stay with the incumbents (S3 and S6 together at 0.25) more often than to pick us (S2 and S4 at 0.10 each, S5 at 0.05). P&W commits to the Joint Venture first at 0.10. A 787 Re-engine, alone or with an A350 Re-engine, is 0.05 each.

Our plan's ΔPV:
- S1: 0.00
- S2: +32.60
- S4: +28.24
- S5: +44.54
- Every other column: 0.00
- Expected: +8.31

If triggered: own NB money $8.0B, entry into service no earlier than 2037, RR widebody share holds at 58%. Next round I expect to re-test the Joint Venture if P&W commits, and widebody only if a Re-engine is on the record.
- Expected ΔPV +8.31 $B; GM valuation after the round +30.07 $B

- Other moves: Narrowbody support plan before EIS (Watson) (private); Funding plan if triggered (McCabe) (private); Open door to Pratt & Whitney on the Joint Venture (public); Pricing discipline (private)


## Round 2: decision year 2035

**2035: the Joint Venture forms, and three engine sources become two**

- P&W and Rolls-Royce both committed to the Joint Venture on sealed orders. Both solo programmes folded into it; it inherits GTF2's schedule and is ready in 2036. Rolls-Royce writes off $1.71B, and its own narrowbody money settles at $5.71B.
- Both new narrowbodies now count two engine sources, CFM and the Joint Venture engine; CFM/GE's narrowbody share from 2040 falls from 31.7% to 27.0%.
- CFM/GE learned the fps selection from this round's brief and held everything: nothing on its menu could change an engine code.
- Airbus left poaching out (over its cash line, and its stricter condition); Boeing held the 787 Re-engine on its overlap rule. Both kept code 7.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Five years ago we launched a new single-aisle airplane. Here is plainly where it stands. The development is on its plan, and that plan still has its headroom. We haven't built airplanes ahead of a mature design, and we won't. We're working the certification plan with the FAA one step at a time. The data decides our stage gates, not the calendar. I'm not going to give you any new dates today. The heaviest spending years on this airplane are in front of us. That's the pig in the python, and we planned for it. Debt stays first. The balance sheet is where it belongs, and we're going to keep it there through the peak. On engines, CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce are all on this airplane. CFM certifies first, and the others follow on their durability evidence. Our customers and our production system both need more than one source of supply. Our 737 MAX and 787 customers come first while we do this. Stable production and the KPIs set our rates, and we'll keep growing the 787 out of the capacity we've built in Charleston. We still develop with our suppliers on cost-plus and risk-share terms, not fixed price. I think we're turning it. I don't think it's turned. It is so much more important that we do this right than fast, and we still have a lot of work to do. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our next-generation single-aisle aircraft is in its final phase of development and remains on track for entry into service in the second half of the 2030s, on the clock we set when we prepared it. We intend to offer a choice of engines. CFM, Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce are all on the programme, and each engine will join the aircraft once its maturity and durability are proven: we will not compromise on certification. Until the new aircraft enters service, the A320neo and A350 families remain our priority, with a disciplined and controlled production ramp-up, delivery performance for our customers through the transition and continued work to secure our own supply chain, engines and critical materials first. We are funding the programme with financial discipline, within our financial flexibility and our existing shareholder return policy. We will continue to work with our industry partners for a tariff-free, zero-for-zero regime for aerospace trade. Preparing the Airbus of the 2030s means one well-prepared programme at a time, for a stronger and more sovereign European aerospace industry. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year. Let me tell you what we know and what we don't. Both new single-aisle aircraft have launched, and both customers chose to put CFM on them alongside others. We're honored by that. We also know we don't have a birthright on any of it. We keep a place on an airplane the same way we earned it: readiness, durability and support, one customer and one engine at a time. CFM is a 50-50 partnership with our partner, and we do this work together. Our ducted engine is in its last year of development. It goes into service when testing says it's ready, and not a day before. Durability doesn't get traded for schedule. Our work with Embraer continues. There are no new programmes from us this year. We'll fund what we have within our capital allocation framework and keep the balance sheet where it is. RISE remains our next generation. We're all in on the open fan technology, and it stays funded as technology. It's really not for us to say when that architecture enters service. Our customers decide when. The new aircraft come first, and we won't ask anyone to wait. Here's what we don't know: how fast our customers will want to go once they're flying, and how those fleets will split. We won't give you dates or numbers our testing and our customers haven't earned. Commercial matters we work privately, with our customers and with our partner. You'll ask about competition. I won't speak to that. We judge ourselves on our own execution. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Commit to Joint Venture with RR | Bad news first. GTF2 has not yet earned its durability record, and our dates have slipped before. GTF2 is in development for NGSA and fps. It will be ready in 2036 and no earlier, and we are not compressing a single test to hold that date. Before it enters service it has to clear two gates: durability validated in severe-environment testing, and every life-limited part at full life. Those are gates, not claims. If anything has to give, the date moves, not the testing. The fleet still comes first. We keep inserting the Hot Section durability kit across the GTF fleet, and we review the pacing parts every week so that neither the GTF2 ramp nor any Joint Venture work takes parts or engineers from that work. We control what we can control. Airbus has invited us onto its new single-aisle as a risk-sharing partner. We accept on standard commercial terms: we carry our own engine's development, and there is no launch-style discount, no concession and no free upgrade. On fps we follow in sequence once we show maturity and durability. That is the right test, and we will meet it with test data, not with price. Today Pratt & Whitney puts on the record its commitment to a Joint Venture with Rolls-Royce on the next narrowbody engine. It forms only if and when Rolls-Royce also commits, and until then nothing has formed. It shares risk and investment, not technology. The gear, the core and the GTF2 technology stay Pratt & Whitney's, and GTF2's configuration, date and team do not change. We will offer only what can be ready by an airframe's entry into service, and we will not speak for any airframer's plans, dates or rates. Those are theirs to announce. These are responsible bets with responsible paybacks. |
| Rolls-Royce | Commit to Joint Venture with P&W | So, plainly. UltraFan narrowbody stays on plan: ready no earlier than seven years from launch, with support in place before entry into service. If testing needs longer, the date moves, not the test plan. Both airframers have selected all three engine makers, Pratt & Whitney included. So if there is to be a partnership, the time to decide it is now. Last time we said we would commit once Pratt & Whitney's commitment was on the record. Frankly, with decisions made at the same time, waiting for that means deciding too late. So our Joint Venture commitment is on the record today, on one condition: it forms only if Pratt & Whitney commits too. If not, nothing is spent, and we will not hold it open. We don't need partnership for capability, but our preference is that. We launch for any airframer that selects us where we can be ready by its entry into service. Widebody is our core: no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody, and Trent time on wing keeps improving. We won't do anything not profitable. When we commit, we deliver. |

### How each company decided (private during the game)

**Boeing** (Kelly Ortberg, Jay Malave, Stephanie Pope; Boeing Board)

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| Board guidance | Boeing Board: risk appetite: 4.5 of 5, unchanged. We keep the launched 10-year fps whole and back no second development. Every Board item must stay above -$1B in each column, and S1, S2 and S3 all count as plausible. The plan must stay above -2.78 in the risk case (S3), which is Do … |
| Frame | Kelly Ortberg: initial lean: Hold course, and change nothing on the board. - **fps: hold.** Continue the 10-year ramp to EIS 2040 with its headroom. - **fps engine code: 7, resubmitted.** I would rather be explicit than rely on None being read as no change. It gives three sources and … |
| Test (CFO) | Jay Malave recommends: Do Nothing. Tests failed: Buffer test (slip leg), plan C: fps cancel + 787 Re-engine hold; Buffer test (slip leg), plan D: fps cancel + 787 Re-engine launch; Cash path of each bill, plan B (CEO asks 2 and 5); Full cost of a cancel (plans C and D). Vetoes on options: fps: cancel, with 787 Re-engine hold (plan C) (binding); fps: cancel, with 787 Re-engine launch (plan D) (binding); 787 Re-engine: launch, with fps hold (plan B) (not binding) |
| Test (operating head) | Stephanie Pope recommends: Do Nothing. Tests failed: Two-front strain, plan B (787 Re-engine launched in 2035) [doctrine B-0341, H4] (CEO ask 3). Vetoes on options: 737 rate: increase (not binding); fps hold + 787 Re-engine: launch (row B), and cancel fps + 787 Re-engine: launch (row D) (not binding); fps: cancel (rows C and D) (not binding); fps engine code changed to give fewer than two sources (1, 2, 3; or 4, which drops CFM) (not binding) |
| Decide | Kelly Ortberg: Do Nothing |
| Veto check (CFO) | Jay Malave concurs |
| Veto check (operating head) | Stephanie Pope concurs |
| Board review | Boeing Board: not called; no Board item (every launch, commit or cancel order at its default) |

CFO tests:

| Test | Threshold | Result | Numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffer test (slip leg), plan A: fps hold, code 7, 737 rate hold, 787 Re-engine hold (CEO ask 1) | Run as the baseline, not a sensitivity. In the column the brief labels 'Risk case' (S3, Airbus Delay Tactics, covert) the plan may be no more than $2B below Do Nothing. Floor = -0.78 - 2.00 = -2.78 | pass | Confirmed: S3 is the brief's labelled risk case, and Do Nothing is the row 'fps: hold + 787: hold' (hold continues the launched fps), S3 = -0.78, so the floor is -2.78. - Decision rationale: **Decision.** We change nothing on the board: fps hold (the 10-year fps continues to EIS 2040), engine code 7 resubmitted, 737 rate hold and 787 Re-engine hold. Every order is a default. Code 7 matches the code on record, and the Board never vetoes a code. So no item goes to the Board as a change. The submitted row and the default row are the same: fps hold + 787 hold, +0.66 / -0.80 / -0.78 (S1 / S2 / S3). My expected column is S1; the risk case is S3.

**Team rule, applied.**
(1) **Malave's buffer test (slip leg).** S3 is the brief's labelled risk case, and the floor is Do Nothing's -0.78 less $2B, or -2.78.
- A (hold/hold): -0.78, which passes by $2.00B.
- B (hold + Re-engine): -0.13, which passes by $2.65B.
- C (cancel + hold): -18.33, which fails by $15.55B.
- D (cancel + Re-engine): -16.66, which fails by $13.88B.
His veto on C and D binds, and I take it. H5 strikes them anyway.
(2) **KPI doctrine.** The rate is hold only, and the brief reports no live quality, FAA or supply-chain problem. Pope's veto has nothing to bind on.
(3) **Everything else is mine.** The only live choice was A against B.

**My four questions.**
- **Are the KPIs stable?** Yes. The brief reports no live problem, and observed shares have been flat at 40.0% NB and 58.8% WB through 2034.
- **Are market, technology and balance sheet ready?** Yes. Net debt has been $0 since 2029 (base) and 2030 (stress). Malave's stress free cash flow bottoms at $2.52B in 2036, the pig in the python, and net debt holds at $0 through 2040.
- **What if certification takes longer than we think?** On S3, A passes. The grid has no column for our own slip, so I asked for the reset rule in advance: one reset, the bill held, the cash moved right, costed once in full.
- **Is this doing less, better?** Yes. A is Do Nothing.

**Rulings.**
- **H5:** no cancel. Keeping the fps is worth +18.99 / +18.99 / +17.55 against cancelling (787 hold). A cancel would write off $60.32B x 5/10 = $30.16B in 2035 with its debt penalty staying, and it would cut engine sources from 3 to 1. If a gate ever fails, the answer is one conservative reset.
- **H4:** a 2035 787 Re-engine (development 2035-40) overlaps the fps development (2033-40) by 5 years, against a 2-year limit, with $3B of undiscounted strain. B is struck. Even without H4 it would lose:
  - its gain against A is +0.65 / -2.99 / +0.65, inside ε in S1 and S3;
  - it lowers the worst column from -0.80 to -3.79;
  - it roughly halves 2036 stress headroom, from $2.52B to about $1.3B (Malave);
  - on a probability-weighted view, A beats B whenever S2 carries more than about 18% weight (0.65 / 3.64), and the bulletin has not ruled out an A350 Re-engine.
- **H3, second half:** not fired, since the A350 has not been re-engined. If Airbus launches the A350 Re-engine, the 787 Re-engine option closes for the rest of the game. Otherwise the next H4-legal round is 2045 (overlap 0), and it comes back there as a review.
- **H1 and H3, first half:** ruled on in 2030 and spent. H2 cannot bind. H6 is moot. H7 has no trigger: no certification trouble is reported, so the FAA-process gate is open.

**Expected column and best grid plan.** I expect S1. Airbus said publicly that the A320neo and A350 stay its priority until NGSA enters service, and that it builds 'one well-prepared programme at a time'. S2 is close behind. The best grid plan in S1 is fps hold + 787 Re-engine at +1.31, which H4 strikes. A scores +0.66, $0.65B behind and within ε. Doing less and doing it better [BX-1239], and the higher-confidence plan with the higher worst column [BX-0548], settle the tie. In S2, A is best outright.

**Premium (H8), logged.**
- Doctrine premium: $0.65B, for holding the Re-engine (H4, one development at a time).
- Objective premium: $0. No order this round moves narrowbody share.
- Engine code 7 and keeping the fps: $0 each.
- Total: $0.65B, inside the $2B cap.

**Engine code.** Code 7 rests on doctrine, not my record (Low: I have no statement as Boeing CEO on engine selection). Its fitted sources are:
- at EIS 2040: CFM + P&W GTF2 (ready 2036) + RR UltraFan NB (ready 2037), three sources;
- in 2045 and 2050: the same three, with three narrowbody engine makers in the market.

Codes 5 and 6 give 2 sources and codes 1-3 give 1. Only code 7 still leaves two if any one maker slips past 2040. NGSA is also on code 7, which is not a disallowed pair.

**Where doctrine drove the choice.**
- the engine code;
- the strain arithmetic and the H4 limit;
- supplier-ladder detail.

**Where my record drove it.**
- debt first [BX-1305];
- doing less [BX-1239];
- one reset and never a cancel [BX-1318, BX-1321, BX-1251];
- no production-certification concurrency [BX-1263].

**Cash path (Malave, confirmed).**
- Programme cash: $7.24B (2035), $8.44B (2036), $7.84B (2037), $6.64B (2038) and $5.43B (2039).
- Base free cash flow: 8.07 / 7.12 / 7.53 / 8.43 / 9.32.
- Stress free cash flow: 3.47 / 2.52 / 2.94 / 3.83 / 4.72.
- Net debt: $0.0B every year, base and stress. A breaks only if 2036 free cash flow falls about 52% below consensus.
- The grid already nets the debt penalty (10-year fps $19.1B undiscounted, for scale), so it is not subtracted again.

**Open Board items.**
- MAX 7/10 closure: the brief is silent. Our last evidence is 'certification next year' (2025) [BX-1873], and I still treat it as done by inference. Pope's seat tables the written closure at the gate; I won't claim a document the brief doesn't carry.
- The Chief Engineer's readiness view and the FAA-agreed plan come in the same package.
- Each engine maker's durability status comes through the durability reviews.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold (Airbus keeps the A350 and A320neo as its priority until NGSA enters service in 2037). S2 (A350 Re-engine) is close behind; S3 (covert Delay Tactics) is the risk case.. Best grid plan there: fps: hold + 787 Re-engine (+1.31 in S1). H4 strikes it: a 5-year overlap with the fps development against a 2-year limit. The best plan that passes H4 is fps: hold + 787: hold (+0.66), $0.65B behind and within ε. That plan is ordered.. Premium +0.65 $B: Doctrine premium of $0.65B: in my expected column, S1, holding the 787 Re-engine gives up +0.65 against the best grid plan (+1.31 against +0.66). That is the known cost of H4 (a 5-year overlap against a 2-year limit) and of doing less and doing it better [BX-1239]. It would also be $0.65B in S3, and $0 in S2, where hold is best by $2.99B. The objective premium is $0: no order this round moves narrowbody share, and debt repair and the engine enabler are already met. Engine code 7 and keeping the fps cost nothing. The total is $0.65B, inside the $2B cap (H8).
- Predictions: **Airbus and the expected column.**
- S1 is most likely: Airbus does not launch the A350 Re-engine this round. S2 is close behind.
- Covert Delay Tactics (S3) are possible but not expected. If they come, they show up as supply or talent strain around our 2040 EIS, worth $1.44B on the grid.

**Our position under the ordered plan.**
- NGSA enters service in 2037 on plan.
- Our NB share follows the brief: 39% (2037), 38% (2038), 37% (2039), 38% (2040), 43% (2045) and 47% (2050).
- Net debt stays at $0.0B in base and stress through the 2036 cash peak, with stress free cash flow bottoming at about $2.5B in 2036.

**Engines.**
- CFM ducted and P&W GTF2 are ready in 2036 and RR UltraFan NB in 2037.
- The fps enters service in 2040 with three engine sources, and three narrowbody engine makers stay in the market.
- No P&W-RR Joint Venture forms in a way that changes our code.

**Next.** The 787 Re-engine case comes back for review in 2045 only if the A350 has not been re-engined by then.
- Expected ΔPV +0.66 $B; GM valuation after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: fps stage-gate package to the Aerospace Safety Committee (private); Engine durability reviews, with CFM certifying first (public); Supplier readiness through the risk window, engines first (private); Workforce retention and train-before-build (private); Capital allocation: debt first through the fps cash peak (public); 787 growth from Charleston capacity; widebody case reviewed in 2045 (private); 737 MAX and 787 customer commitment (public)

Code 7 fits 3 sources and gives the enabler 3 / 3 in 2040, 2045 and 2050 on every hold plan. Codes 5 and 6 give 2 sources, code 3 gives CFM alone, and codes 1, 2 and 4 drop CFM as a named source, so no. Neither maker is off track on the record, so I have no ground to drop one. Integration load (unpriced, for the safety review): three certification campaigns on shared teams in NGSA's last two years. The A320neo precedent is two engines certified six months apart (P&W Nov 2015, CFM May 2016). Mitigation: CFM certifies first and alone gates EIS, GTF2 follows, and UltraFan NB certifies last. UltraFan's zero margin is a real-world risk the board does not price: on the board an unready maker is simply dropped, but in practice it must never hold NGSA's EIS (rule 8). It follows in sequence after EIS if it needs to. Joint Venture this round: rules.md folds both solo programmes into it, and its engine would be ready no later than the further-along solo (2036). Code 7 still fits it, giving CFM + the Joint Venture engine = 2 sources, still dual. The brief does not give the maker count under a Joint Venture. rules.md gives P&W and RR equal NB shares, so I read it as 3 makers (inference; GM to confirm). Code 4 is never acceptable because it drops CFM. |

| Boeing Rate Increase | No reaction: our own disciplined ramp | not applicable | None is on the record, and the lever was 2030-only. |

- Decision rationale: DECISION BY THE TEAM RULE
Orders: ngsa hold, engine code 7 (unchanged, confirmed in its last changeable round), A350 Re-engine hold, Delay Tactics none. No Board items. Row: +23.37 (S1) / +22.88 (S2). Probability-weighted at 25-30% S2: +23.22 to +23.25. I verified the grid items and the peak-cash arithmetic myself on the brief's numbers.

FRAMING, in my order
- Five pillars: nothing covert, and both colleagues raise no flag on this plan [AX-0081, AX-0073].
- Ramp: no new programme cash or engineering load in NGSA's last two spend years [AX-0010, AX-0054].
- Airbus of the 30s: NGSA whole, with entry into service in the second half of the decade and three engine sources [AX-0020, A-0409].
- Board: this is the Board's own expected package.

STEP 1: DELETE RED-LINE AND PILLAR BREACHES
- Every cancel row: rule 7. Continuing gains $39.65B in both columns. Cancelling writes off $25.37B in 2035 and drops the enabler to 2 sources. Boeing's fps is never a ground.
- Delay Tactics 'both', in any row: rule 4 (two moves), and an integrity flag from both colleagues.
- Any 'bottleneck': Toepfer (as chair of the Internal Control Committee) and Wagner both flag integrity, because the board's move exists to delay fps, not to give us a first claim for our own ramp. A pillars failure cannot be overridden.
- Left: the hold plan, + A350 Re-engine, + poaching, and + A350 Re-engine + poaching.

STEP 2: SURVIVORS OF BOTH TESTS
- Hold plan: passes both.
  - Toepfer: $5.07B in 2035-36 against $5.2B; worst column +22.88; the net-cash bridge holds under the guardrail; no break-even crossed.
  - Wagner: no pillars flag; A320 shield holds; no crunch; NGSA ready for 2037; code 7.
- + A350 Re-engine fails Toepfer twice: peak cash of $6.67B is $1.47B over the line, and the added item is -1.21 in S2. Both are binding soft vetoes. Wagner passes it at the limit but flags 2045 as the better fit.
- + poaching fails Toepfer's cash test: $5.27B, over even the unrounded $5.23B and over on a six-year spread. It also fails Wagner's stricter-condition test, which I keep standing. Both are binding soft vetoes.
- + A350 Re-engine + poaching fails both: $6.87B, and the stricter condition.
- The only survivor is the hold plan. It is also the company default and the reference row, so no tie-break is needed.

STEP 3: OVERRIDES CONSIDERED AND DECLINED (budget unused, one left)
Poaching:
- Its margin of +1.65 / +1.66 over the best passing option clears the team's $1B bar in both columns. An override would require me to record setting aside the team's stricter condition and to override Toepfer's cash test.
- I decline, for four reasons:
  - The Board has stated a Liquidity veto, which is its own test, and I hold no override against the Board. The order would revert to the default after spending the game's only override.
  - $0.6B of the $1B falls in 2037-39, after NGSA enters service, so the recruitment reading weakens.
  - It does nothing for 2045 or 2050.
  - My supply-security bias is about our own ramp, and it is no reason to order Delay Tactics.
- The stricter condition stands, as in 2030.

A350 Re-engine:
- It clears the $1B bar in S1 only (+1.54) and is -1.21 in S2, so it is worth +$0.72-0.85B probability-weighted (breaks even at 56% S2).
- On a Qualified Majority item, the Board's override review requires staying ahead of the best passing option in the worst plausible column. It fails that, and it fails the Board's Liquidity test.
- It returns in 2045 (zero overlap, $1.0B a year) only if no 787 Re-engine is on the record.

ENGINE CODE
- Code 7 is confirmed, on Wagner's supply recommendation: CFM + GTF2 (2036) + UltraFan NB (2037). That gives 3 sources and 3 makers in 2040, 2045 and 2050, and it sits in no barred pair with fps's code 7.
- No maker is off track on the record, and the code is not changed in reaction to Boeing.
- CFM certifies first and gates EIS. UltraFan never holds EIS.

BOARD-GATE LINE (package; no Board items)
- Five pillars: pass, nothing covert.
- Toepfer: pass.
  - Liquidity: $5.07B in 2035-36, nil programme cash in 2037-39, against $5.2B (EUR 4,753m at $1.10); headroom $0.13B.
  - Downside: worst column S2, +22.88.
  - Returns: payout inside 30-50%, at the lower end if ramp cash falls short; no new debt or equity.
  - The $35.52B charge at the 2037 EIS is reported beside EBIT Adjusted.
  - No break-even crossed ($35.52B < $36.4B; EUR 4,753m > EUR 4.61bn; $1.10 > $1.068).
- Wagner: pass. No quality event, no supply crunch, A320 shield held, NGSA ready for 2037, and the three-engine load is reported to the safety review.
- Grid: +23.37 / +22.88. Items held at default, with their values against hold: A350 Re-engine +1.54 / -1.21; one Delay Tactics move +1.65 / +1.66; both +1.18 / +1.19; cancel -39.65 / -39.65.

GAP TO THE BEST GRID PLAN
The best grid plan in S1 is $3.19B above the default. The gap is recorded, not paid for: every route to it fails cash, integrity or the stricter condition, and the Board vetoes each piece.
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold (about 70-75%).\n- Boeing's bulletin puts debt first and 737 MAX and 787 stability first.\n- fps is in development to 2040.\n- The GEnx upgrade has served the 787 since 2033.\n\nS2, a 787 Re-engine, stays plausible at 25-30% and is the worst column: +22.88 on the hold plan, still positive.. Best grid plan there: NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine + one Delay Tactics move: +26.56 in S1 (+23.32 in S2), which is $3.19B over the hold plan in S1.\n\nIt fails on three counts:\n- Toepfer's peak-cash test: $6.87B in 2035-36.\n- The team's stricter condition, plus integrity, on its Delay Tactics part.\n- The Board's stated veto on both parts.\n\nThe best plan that passes every test is the one submitted: NGSA hold (code 7) + A350 hold + Delay Tactics none, at +23.37 / +22.88.. Premium +0.00 $B: No doctrine premium is paid. The submitted plan is the only option that survives the red lines, both colleagues' tests and the Board's would-veto list, so it is both the best passing plan and the company default.\n\nThe gaps to higher rows come from failed tests, not from a premium:\n- $3.19B to the best S1 row;\n- $1.65B to one Delay Tactics move;\n- $1.54B in S1 to the A350 Re-engine.\n\nThe tests that fail are peak cash, the stricter condition and integrity, and the Board's Liquidity veto, against which I hold no override. The gap is recorded, not paid for.\n\nThe $3B premium allowance is not engaged:\n- no 787 Re-engine is on the record;\n- the second Delay Tactics move is a red line in its own right (and -0.47 in both columns).
- Predictions: BOEING, 2035\n- Most likely S1 (about 70-75%): it holds, with fps on its 10-year plan to EIS 2040 and no Rate Increase (the lever was 2030-only).\n- S2, a 787 Re-engine, about 25-30%: it would open a second front while fps is in development and debt comes first.\n- It keeps fps code 7 with CFM as baseline.\n\nENGINE MAKERS\n- P&W: most likely holds GTF2 Solo to 2036. A Joint Venture commitment is possible but less likely with GTF2 two-thirds through. If P&W commits, Rolls-Royce commits, the Joint Venture forms, the solos fold, and NGSA becomes CFM + the Joint Venture engine (2 sources).\n- Rolls-Royce: UltraFan NB on track for 2037, possibly with a widebody move.\n- CFM: ducted engine ready 2036; Embraer partnership 2037.\n\nBOARD\nIt approves the package: nothing to vote and nothing vetoed.\n\nOUR PATH\n- NGSA enters service in 2037 with CFM certifying first.\n- NB share: 61-63% in 2037-39, 62% in 2040 as fps enters, then down to 53% by 2049.\n- 2045 round: the A350 Re-engine is on the table only if no 787 Re-engine is on the record.\n\nEXPECTED PV\n+23.37 in S1; probability-weighted about +23.22 to +23.25.
- Expected ΔPV +23.37 $B; GM valuation after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Engine-maker risk-sharing invitation, reworded (public); Secure our own supply for the A320 family and the NGSA ramp (public); Sequential engine certification, CFM first, and the last engine-code call (private); Payout guardrail and presentation of the 2037 charge (private); A350 Re-engine review gate for 2045 (private); A320neo delivery performance through the transition (public); A220-500 seat-size study (private); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy through industry coalitions (public)

Gates to have in hand on test before certification: (1) the hot section turned on and turned off: reproduce hot-and-harsh distress in dust testing and remove it with the design. Those are the parts that cost LEAP time on wing in the Middle East and India [CX-0161]; the same dust testing started on RISE HPT blades in October 2025 [CX-0222]. (2) Life-limited parts at full life at entry. The public bulletin already sets that bar for another maker's engine, with severe-environment validation first, and CFM certifying first must clear at least the same bar on test. (3) Enhanced inspection of safety-critical parts in new-make shops and MRO from the first engine [CX-0010]. (4) Root cause and fixes accepted by the airframer and the regulator, as with Boeing and the FAA [CX-0003]. (5) Every gate a cross-functional call [CX-0012]. (6) Report back against the date, as with the fuel nozzle [CX-0007]. Float: ready 2036 against NGSA 2037 is 1 year; against fps 2040, 4 years. On the board, a Ducted ready in 2037 still counts from 2037. A slip past 2037 shows first as a gate that does not turn off. NGSA would then enter with two new rival engines ready (2036, 2037) while the CFM slot flies a LEAP derivative until the Ducted is ready. Ghai prices that at about $1.0-1.2B a year; that is his number, not mine. Track record: my LEAP-1B kit moved from 2025 to 1H 2026, about a year [CX-0008, CX-0210]. One year of float is no more than my own record has already used. If a gate fails, the date moves, not the test [CX-0208, CX-0603]. |

My check: 2037 (Boeing 39, Airbus 61): CFM 39 + 61/3 = 59.33, net of the engine moves (+10 Ducted, +5 Embraer, -5 GTF2, -10 UltraFan), over 105 = 56.5%. From 2040, a three-way split on both aircraft: 33.33 / 105 = 31.7%. Keeping the Ducted is worth +16.86 / +17.37 / +17.65 against cancel (row B), S1 / S2 / S3. |

'Launched and ready by 2050' is out of reach on our tests. Rows against hold, S1 / S2 / S3: D Open Fan -55.79 / -35.38 / -40.09; E Open Fan + Lobby -37.34 / -31.37 / -35.99; F Ducted cancel + Open Fan -58.46 / -35.73 / -42.76. This fails for any Open Fan launch. Culp's plan holds the Open Fan, so no order he leans to fails. |

This is the state of the technology, not a product case, and not a veto ground. | fail | I report back against the promise: the record does not show these gates met. (1) Not shown. The fly demo was promised 'by the middle of this decade' [CX-0001, CX-0151] and moved to 'engine ground level and flight test this decade' [CX-0211]. No result is in the record, which ends in October 2025, or in the brief. (2) Not closed. Over 350 programme tests with an early focus on durability [CX-0216]; HPT-blade dust testing on the compact core began in October 2025 [CX-0222]; a Chief Mechanic and Architect for the open fan was named [CX-0223]. No turn-off result is on the record. (3) Not shown in engine test. 'More than 20%' is the design figure [CX-0001] and the physics case [CX-0180], not an engine-test result. (4) Shown as a capability: noise tested with Airbus showed the open fan 'has the capability to achieve lower noise than today's LEAP' [CX-0013], but not on a product engine. The Ducted stands as an H3 defence, and the trigger is now on the public record: two rival NB engines are launched, ready in 2036 and 2037, and selected on both aircraft. Culp's draft calls RISE the next generation, never the Ducted, and gives no fuel-burn number. Pass. Any public RISE fuel-burn number would need an engine test, which the record does not show (RISE gate 3). |

The brief carries no durability or time-on-wing data, and no order trades durability for schedule. This is not a clearance: there is no Ducted test evidence either way, and I say so plainly. Embraer rides on the same engine and carries the same gates. GEnx is a derivative on a proven architecture, ready since 2033, with no durability data either way. 'Support both rates' is at most today's 3,040 a year on the board, or 4,000 if one maker held both full slots. - Decision rationale: DECISION: HOLD EVERYTHING. All five fields are at hold. That is the default, so no order is a Board item.

WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]
The selections are made. NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) both chose code 7, and two rival NB engines are selected on both. The game now is the installed base on two aircraft we share three ways [CX-0422]. This is the last round either airframer can change its code. Nothing on this grid defends against a switch, and S2 and S3 cost $27.30B and $15.70B against S1. So we win on execution, not launches:
- the Ducted ready on test, with durability earned;
- both customers kept on readiness, durability and support, not price [CX-0250];
- RISE kept whole as technology.
We have no birthright [CX-0564].

GRID AGAINST DO NOTHING (hold = Do Nothing = default)
Hold is the best plan in every column: S1 -62.61, S2 -89.91, S3 -78.31.
- Expected column (S1): best plan hold, 0.00 against Do Nothing.
- Worst column (S2): best plan hold, 0.00.
Every alternative loses in every column (S1 / S2 / S3 against hold):
- Embraer cancel: -4.65 / -6.02 / -5.44.
- Ducted cancel, with Embraer falling with it: -16.86 / -17.37 / -17.65.
- Open Fan: -55.79 / -35.38 / -40.09.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -37.34 / -31.37 / -35.99.
- Ducted cancel + Open Fan: -58.46 / -35.73 / -42.76.
Weighted on my weights (.50 / .25 / .25), hold is -73.36:
- Ducted cancel -17.19;
- Embraer cancel -5.19;
- Open Fan -46.76;
- Open Fan + Lobby -35.51.
No weighting changes the ranking. Ghai's weights (.60 / .20 / .20) and equal weights give the same answer.

TESTS
- Additive and paid for the risk: no move off hold beats hold in any column.
- Low case first: S1 is the record, and I book well below it.
- H2, the airframer sets the date: a 2035 Open Fan is ready in 2045. That is eight years after NGSA's entry and five after fps's, so it waits in every column.
- H3, the Ducted only as a defence: the trigger is now on the public record. Two rival NB engines are launched and selected on both aircraft, so keeping the Ducted is the defence the rule calls for.
- Cancel doctrine: the trigger is not met. Both airframes would fly the engine, and the threat is present.
- My 20% line binds RISE, not the Ducted. The Ducted gets 'less than half' the gain [CX-0183] and stands only under H3. The public line never calls it the next generation.
- Durability line [CX-0208]: no order trades durability for schedule. The readiness review is how I enforce that.
- Tie-break: not needed. No alternative is within $1B of hold, and the smallest gap is $4.65B.

DUCTED REREAD (round-1 commitment)
A cancel writes off $4B x 5/6 = $3.33B and saves $0.67B of R&D. Ghai's correction: on the board, the saving is $2.67B including the $2B strain released. The row still loses $16.86-17.65B, because it gives up about $19.6-20.3B of engine value. In S1 the NB share in 2037 would fall from 56.5% to 44.3%. Keep it. Once it is ready in 2036 it can no longer be cancelled, so this hold locks it in. If a test gate fails, we fix the part, not cancel the engine [CX-0005].

OPEN FAN AND THE RISE ALLOWANCE
My installed-base lens pulls hardest here [CX-0555], because this is the last round 'ready by 2050' can be met. But the cheapest row, Open Fan + Lobby, is $31.37B to $37.34B below hold ($35.51B weighted), more than twice the $15B allowance. Ali reports none of the three Board RISE gates closed on the record. So I keep RISE as technology, with no cancel (H5) and no program dividend [CX-0174]. Lobby does nothing without a live Open Fan.

SAFRAN GATE (H7)
No new CFM narrowbody order. Continuing the Ducted and Embraer stays within joint practice: both airframers selected CFM, and the partners split the aftermarket equally [CX-0219]. No Safran position is asserted. GEnx is GE alone.

EXCO
Ghai's binding tests pass on hold, and he and Ali both recommend all-hold, so there are no overrides.
- I adopt Ghai's booking of -77 over my -75.
- I keep my more conservative weights; he agrees they err on the right side.
- Ali's date test passes. 2036 is labelled the board's development time, not a test result.
- Ali gives no flag, and I don't read that as a clearance. The readiness review goes to the full Board before 2037.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, DROVE:
- the Embraer hold (profile §6; my record is silent on Embraer);
- the Lobby rule;
- the supply-load read, which uses the strain rule because Ali has no supply field data.

CAPITAL
No new R&D. The Ducted's last $0.67B in 2035 and the strain already carried come to about $1.0B a year against about $2.5B of room. The 70% payout and the A-range rating are whole [CX-0645].
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. Both airframers keep code 7, and NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) are each fitted CFM + P&W + RR. I pick it from the public record, not from hoped-for selections. Boeing names CFM its baseline, certifying first, and wants more than one source. Airbus says the A320 successor keeps a choice of engines and invites all three makers in. A sole-source switch would contradict both airframers' own words. It is still possible this round, though, and both rival engine makers offer to launch on a sole-source selection. So S2 and S3 carry heavy weight, and I book well below S1.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything, at -62.61 in S1. It is also the best plan in S2 (-89.91) and S3 (-78.31), and the best weighted (-73.36 on my weights). Against Do Nothing it is 0.00 in the expected column and in the worst column (S2).. Premium +0.00 $B: $0.00. Hold everything is the best grid plan in the expected column (S1) and in every other column, so no PV is given up.
- Doctrine premium: $0.00.
- Objective premium: $0.00.
- Running count: this round $0 of $2.0B; the game $0 of $4.0B.
- RISE allowance, stated apart: $0 of $15B, unspent. The cheapest Open Fan row, Open Fan + Lobby, would have needed $31.37B (S2) to $37.34B (S1) against the best non-RISE plan, which is more than twice the allowance. So it was not used.
- For reference, the smallest premium any Board item would have carried is $4.65B (Embraer cancel, S1), which is over the cap.
- Predictions: Column weights, summing to 1.00: S1 .50, S2 .25, S3 .25. All three are plausible on the bulletin.

Weighted values:
- hold -73.36;
- Embraer cancel -78.55 (-5.19 against hold);
- Ducted cancel -90.55 (-17.19);
- Open Fan + Lobby -108.87 (-35.51);
- Open Fan -120.12 (-46.76).
On equal weights hold is -76.94; on Ghai's (.60 / .20 / .20) it is -71.21.

Booking: -77 (Ghai's guide-low figure). That is below the weighted -73.36 and $14.39B below S1. The S1 value is upside until the 2045 bulletin shows the codes locked at 7; then we raise.

Reading, private:
- Both airframers keep code 7 (S1): .50. Each has said on the record it wants a choice of engines, and Boeing names CFM as baseline.
- Airbus moving NGSA to P&W only (S2): .25. P&W's engine is ready first (2036) and offered sole or shared.
- Boeing moving fps to RR only (S3): .25. RR will launch for any airframer that selects it.

To watch in 2045:
- whether the codes lock at 7;
- a Joint Venture commitment, since RR's door is open to P&W and the rules take 10 pp from CFM;
- any widebody Re-engine, which moves the GEnx count year;
- the Ducted's test gates against 2036;
- the state of the RISE gates.
- Expected ΔPV −77.00 $B; GM valuation after the round −69.69 $B

- Other moves: Ducted readiness and durability review at the full Board before NGSA's 2037 entry into service (private); Joint CFM capacity plan with Safran for both airframers' rates (private); Answer Boeing's engine competition and Airbus's risk-sharing invitation through CFM governance (private); Keep RISE funded as technology, and report its gates as the state of the technology (public); CFM parity record for a hold (private)

- Decision rationale: DECISION: `GTF2 Solo: hold`, `Joint Venture with RR: commit` (row 'GTF2: hold + commit to JV': +3.37 / -17.95 / -8.44 / +8.88). It is Eddy's proposal and Mitchill's recommendation, and both pass every veto. overrides is empty.

1. Constraint first [PX-0035][PX-0073].
- On the board GTF2 is ready in 2036: one year before NGSA's 2037 EIS and four before fps's 2040. Eddy's binding readiness test passes on both.
- Bad news first: the real margin is thinner than the board's. The Advantage slipped about a year, and the company prior for a derivative is +1.5 to 2 years, which would land GTF2 around 2037.5-2038, after NGSA's EIS. My own dates have slipped by one quarter to two years [PX-0017][PX-0098][PX-0104].
- So I book nothing on the date. The two gates (severe-environment validation, full-life LLPs at EIS) are gates, not claims, and the date moves, not the testing. Mitchill prices a slip past 2037 at up to 21.32 (the S2 outcome). That is a red-line cost, not traded.
- The Joint Venture must not touch GTF2's configuration, team or date (Eddy's conditions b and c).
- Fleet first [PX-0080]: the 2036-37 ramp is the real draw, so it is ring-fenced under the weekly pacing-parts review, and new capacity is qualified before it ships.

2. GTF2: hold.
- The demand is visible: code 7 on both live airframes, on the record [PX-0109].
- Cancel trails hold in every column: -32.79 / -11.47 / -20.98 / -32.79. That is -29.26 at my weights and -29.05 at Mitchill's. It would write off $1.67B to save $0.33B and throw away two selections.
- I do not harvest a programme its customers chose. This is inference from [PX-0064][P-1342]: we reset what has stopped working, and this has not.
- Mitchill confirms hold carries a selected engine, so ground 2 does not reach it.
- From 2036 GTF2's $2B is sunk and the cancel option lapses.

3. Joint Venture: commit.
- My round-1 trigger is met: code 7 on both live airframes includes Rolls-Royce.
- The item against hold is 0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51, never negative. That is +0.55 at my weights, +0.44 at Mitchill's and +1.38 at equal weights.
- It needs $0 of new cash: GTF2's $2B equals our share, so the fold write-off is $0 (Mitchill). It beats a buyback, which is about $0 NPV at the 10% WACC.
- I have no record of my own on a Rolls-Royce Joint Venture (Low). This is company doctrine (profile.md §6, dashboard_game.md §5; [P-1233][P-1676]) plus Mitchill's cash test and Eddy's technical test as my evidence. I call our GTF partnerships 'critical' [PX-0038].
- Terms: risk and investment shared, never the gear, core or GTF2 technology, which stay P&W modules under P&W design authority.
- Eddy's flag that Rolls-Royce claims favourable geared IP is rebutted in the terms: P&W keeps the gear team and data. No core-technology red-line flag is left standing.
- Architecture loyalty would have kept me on GTF2 alone. The grid says the commit costs GTF2 nothing, so loyalty does not get to cost PV.

4. Veto check.
- Mitchill, ground by ground:
  - ground 1 (dividend or debt): no new R&D; GTF2 cost $0.33B a year, 5.6% of the ~$6B envelope; dividend covered; debt on its pre-ASR path; no envelope stretch.
  - ground 2 (unselected engine): pass.
  - ground 3 (aggressive terms): standard terms in the Airbus and Boeing answers, no buy-in payment.
  - ground 4 (booking upside): $0 booked on the commit, and nothing is presented as formed.
- His two conditional binding vetoes are respected in the statement and other moves:
  - ground 4: Rolls-Royce's commitment and the formation are not presented as done; the +5.51 is not announced; durability is written as gates.
  - ground 3: no concession, discount, fee or fixed-price development.
- Eddy: both grounds pass on the orders as proposed. His standing conditions (configuration freeze; staffing outside the fleet and validation teams) are written into other moves, so neither is invoked.

5. Red lines.
- No new engine without a committed airframer: GTF2 fired on code 7 selections.
- Shared-source relief on code 7 (NGSA and fps) is declared again, as in round 1. On NGSA it is also franchise defence (inference: [PX-0058][P-1292]).
- No core technology to the Joint Venture [P-1233].
- The dividend never funds an engine.
- Value, not share [PX-0055][PX-0100].
- No widebody [PX-0027].
- No airframer dates or rates [PX-0062].

6. Premium: $0 this round.
- No Board item trails hold, and neither a doctrine nor an objective premium is needed.
- Round-1 doctrine premium: $0.26B, reported separately. The cumulative ledger is $0.26B inside the $1B cap.

7. Objective.
- Restore credibility is met (on the board) in every column: on NGSA and fps in S1/S4, fps in S2, NGSA in S3. It holds only while GTF2 is ready by EIS. We do not claim it in public.
- Capitalise is met in S1 and S4 (+3.37 / +8.88) and fails in S2 and S3 in every row. No order moves those columns; only durability data the airframers believe does.

8. Expected column: S1, rivals hold.
- Airbus keeps 'a choice of engines', Boeing wants two or three sources, and Rolls-Royce waits for our commitment to be on the record.
- I lowered S4 from 0.15 to 0.10 on Mitchill's point that orders are sealed and simultaneous, and raised S2 to 0.11 on GTF reputation risk.
- Expected PV +0.39 against hold's -0.16.

What would change my mind:
- Joint Venture terms needing P&W cash beyond GTF2's $2B: back through the gate.
- A slip or parts shortfall that Eddy reports: hold/hold, and the date moves.
- In 2045, the written re-test.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. Code 7 stays on NGSA and fps, Rolls-Royce does not commit in the same sealed round, and GTF2 is ready in 2036 for both airframes.. Best grid plan there: In S1, 'GTF2: hold' and 'GTF2: hold + commit to JV' tie at +3.37 (103.1%), and the brief lists 'GTF2: hold'. The submitted row (hold + commit) equals it in S1 and is never below it in any column (+8.88 against +3.37 in S4). So it is the best expected row: +0.39 at my weights against -0.16 for hold.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium this round. The only Board item, `Joint Venture with RR: commit`, never trails hold (0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51), and `GTF2 Solo: hold` is the default. No doctrine-favoured order differs from the best expected row, and no objective premium is needed: both hold rows meet Restore credibility, and Capitalise fails in S2 and S3 in every row. Reported separately: the round-1 doctrine premium of $0.26B (`launch if selected` over an unconditional launch). The cumulative ledger is $0.26B inside the team's $1B cap. If the commit had been struck, the fallback to hold/hold would have cost $0.55B at my weights ($0.44B at Mitchill's). That would be a red-line cost, not a premium, and it does not arise.
- Predictions: S1 (rivals hold): 0.69. S2 (Airbus moves NGSA to code 5): 0.11. S3 (Boeing moves fps to code 3): 0.10. S4 (Rolls-Royce commits to the Joint Venture): 0.10. Sum 1.00. Expected column: S1.

Reasons:
- Airbus keeps 'a choice of engines' and has invited all three makers. S2 is raised slightly for GTF reputation risk and the thin one-year margin.
- Code 3 cuts against Boeing's own goal of two or three sources, but its condition 'once it shows maturity and durability' is live against us.
- Rolls-Royce says it commits once our commitment is on the record. Orders are sealed and simultaneous, so a 2045 formation is likelier than one in 2035. I moved S4 down from my prior of 0.15 on Mitchill's point.

Row values at these weights: hold + commit +0.39; hold -0.16; cancel -29.42; cancel + commit -25.76. Commit item +0.55; cancel item -29.26.

Beyond this round (not booked): the codes on both airframes are likely fixed after this round, since the next round is 2045 and after both EIS dates. The Joint Venture's live question becomes the 2045 re-test.
- Expected ΔPV +0.39 $B; GM valuation after the round +8.88 $B

- Other moves: Answer Airbus's NGSA risk-sharing invitation on standard commercial terms (public); Answer Boeing's fps engine competition: GTF2 proposed under code 7, following in sequence on durability data (public); GTF Hot Section durability kit insertion continues across the fleet, charged (public); Joint Venture with Rolls-Royce: terms of P&W's standing commitment (public); Fleet ring-fence and weekly pacing-parts review (contain first) (private); Joint Venture integration: staffing and configuration freeze (private); 2045 hold-or-withdraw re-test of the standing Joint Venture commitment (private); Durability data cadence to the airframers, gates not claims (private)

This is the last round in which either airframer can change its code before NGSA's EIS; the next round is 2045, after both EIS. GEARBOX STATUS: the public record still reads 'in development; ready 2037', unchanged since 2030. The brief reports no slip and no test result. So I report no slip, and I claim no test result the record cannot show. Gearbox sizing (a briefing constraint) remains the named maturity risk. On the plan of record, 2037 needs no compressed testing. If the endurance test needs longer, I tell the Committee first and the date moves, not the test plan. EFFECT ON NGSA: under the rules, a maker that is not ready by EIS is dropped. A slip past 2037 therefore loses NGSA; fps still has 3 years. The nearest grid measure is S2 (Airbus to code 6): +8.75 against +30.07 in S1, i.e. -21.32, with NB share in 2050 falling from 36.5% to 19.7%. GEARBOX: P&W's gearbox could answer our gearbox-sizing constraint (dashboard_game.md §3). That is inference; I have no evidence of my own. It applies to the growth engine, through full testing. | Board item | Value | Decision | Ground | Alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Joint Venture with P&W | commit | approve | Every test that touches this item passes. I checked the package's rows against the round 2 brief grid, and they match. (1) ExCo process. McCabe co-signs `Joint Venture with P&W: commit` in her test memo and again at her veto check, where she concurs and invokes no binding veto. Her binding withholds (the upgrade, UltraFan WB, and a commit paired with a cancel) apply to orders the CEO did not give. Watson concurs and invokes no Civil veto. Both veto checks show red_line_breach false. The frame's partner red line set four conditions, and all are met: co-signature; an explicit rebuttal of last round's public word and of the ExCo's 'P&W first' default, set out in four reasons in `rationale`; a public statement saying why we move first; and a 2045 `withdraw` trigger. `overrides` is empty. Pass [RX-0213, RG-0044]. (2) Safety and maturity, judged on the final orders. If P&W also commits in 2035, the Joint Venture forms and folds GTF2 (ready 2036) and our Solo (ready 2037). Its engine is then ready no later than 2037: zero margin against NGSA's 2037 EIS (a named pass) and 3 years or more against fps 2040. If P&W does not commit, nothing forms and nothing is spent. I checked Watson's three reserved grounds against the final text. (a) No early date: the statement says 'no earlier than seven years from launch', there is no Joint Venture date, 'a partnership can still serve them' was struck for his wording, and nothing is said on airframer dates. (b) No compressed maturity: the rationale counts on no UltraFan-core, P&W-gearbox engine by 2037, and a folded engine carries its own frozen test plan. (c) Support: a joint partner-shop support plan before the first EIS is carried as a private other move. No Civil ground stands. Pass [RG-0027, RG-0025, R-1476, R-0970, RX-0296, RX-0284, RX-0283]. (3) Partner alignment. P&W's own commitment is not on the record (2030: 'no Joint Venture commitment this round'). The other limb is met: fps and NGSA both carry code 7 on the public record, and code 7 includes P&W. Weighted value is at or above 0. Pass [R-1010, R-0318, R-0916]. (4) Profit over share. "UltraFan NB: hold + commit to JV" (+30.07 / +8.75 / +18.26 / +32.35 / +29.98 / +29.98) against "UltraFan NB: hold" (+30.07 / +8.75 / +18.26 / +30.07 / +29.98 / +29.98) gives 0 / 0 / 0 / +2.28 / 0 / 0 for S1-S6. At McCabe's weights (0.65 / 0.10 / 0.05 / 0.10 / 0.05 / 0.05) that is +0.23 (+27.57 against +27.34). At the board's illustration it is also +0.23, and it is at least 0 under any weights. No premium; `premium` is 0. Pass [R-1011, R-1561, R-1582]. (5) Reasonable worst case. Worst plausible column 0, against the item's own -$4B bill. Pass [RG-0033]. (6) One big programme at a time. `UltraFan WB` and `Trent 1000 upgrade` are at hold. Strain is $0 in every column. The Solo folds into the Joint Venture on formation, so two narrowbody programmes never run side by side. Pass [R-0929, R-1127, RX-0214]. (7) Declared premium: not applicable, since this is a narrowbody item and carries none. Balance sheet and position weakening: not applicable. McCabe invokes no balance-sheet veto, and no other move needs equity, leverage above about 1.5x or borrowing for distributions [RG-0041, RX-0261]. Control's $4B bound is not met: $8.0B in five columns, $5.7B in S4, $7.77B expected. The commit is the only order that improves it. The breach is stated with its cause and is not a veto ground. Approve. | commit; hold |
