  - cancel + Joint Venture: -30.16 (-36.35; -4.00 against "hold + commit to JV" in S4);
  - Trent 1000 upgrade: -6.01 (-6.03);
  - UltraFan WB: -8.80 (-9.00).

WHY THE SOLO STAYS
Keeping the Solo beats a cancel in every column, by 15.03 to 36.35. Both live airframes carry code 7, and the engine is ready in 2037, by NGSA's 2037 EIS and fps's 2040. It is not an orphan, so a cancel breaks the company rule [R-1573]. This is the last round a cancel is possible; it is closed. When we commit, we deliver [RX-0124].

WHY THE JOINT VENTURE NOW: rebutting last round's word and the ExCo's 'P&W first' default
Last round I said publicly that "we commit once its commitment is on the record". The ExCo's team default is partner first once P&W has committed. I am departing from the letter of both, for four reasons.
(1) Orders are sealed and simultaneous. P&W's commitment can reach the record only a round after it is made, so waiting means committing in 2045 at the earliest. By then NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) are in service, and a fold would write off about $4B of a ready engine. The word would defeat its own purpose.
(2) The substance of the word survives. Its point was no Joint Venture without a partner and an airframe. Under the rules the Joint Venture forms only in a round when P&W is also committed. Both live airframes carry code 7, which includes P&W, and that meets the Board's partner limb [R-1010]. If P&W does not commit, nothing forms and nothing is spent.
(3) The fold condition I set last round is met: the fold row beats keeping the Solo in S4 and ties it elsewhere.
(4) It is a choice with a trigger, not options management [RX-0051]: withdraw in 2045 by default. It is the same logic as last round's `launch if selected`.
The Board said it would not ask me to break a public word for +$0.23B. I am not breaking it for the money. I am squaring it in public, in today's statement, and giving the reason. "There's no change of strategy" [RX-0098]: partnership is our stated preference [RX-0118]. On cash, the +2.28 in S4 is in substance the $2.29B of Solo spend avoided, with our narrowbody share unchanged at 36.5% in 2050.

MATURITY: no claim of a new engine by 2037
A Joint Venture formed in 2035 folds GTF2 (ready 2036) and our Solo (ready 2037). It carries a folded engine on its own frozen test plan, ready no later than 2037 on the board's rule; Watson confirms it. I do not count on an UltraFan-core, P&W-gearbox engine in service by 2037. That configuration would be a later engine with its own full programme. No Joint Venture date is disclosed.

VETO CHECKS
- McCabe co-signs the commit: worst column 0; capex £0.84bn a year on hold, £0 in S4 after the fold; funded from free cash flow, buybacks giving way first.
- Her binding withholds (the upgrade, UltraFan WB, a cancel paired with the commit) all apply to orders I do not give.
- No balance-sheet ground arises.
- Watson invokes no Civil veto. I have met the three grounds he reserved for step 4:
  (a) No Joint Venture ready date, explicit or implied. I struck 'a partnership can still serve them' and used his wording, 'if there is to be a partnership, the time to decide it is now'. No UltraFan NB date before 2037. Nothing on airframer dates.
  (b) No claim of a new configuration by 2037.
  (c) The joint partner-shop support plan before the first EIS is carried as a private other move.
- Red-line flags: none stands. The cancel, the upgrade and UltraFan WB are all struck. Strain stays at $0. `overrides` is empty.

PREMIUM
$0. The submitted plan is the best expected grid plan, and no item is held below the best plan.

CONTROL'S METRICS (own NB money / strain / RR WB share 2050 / RR NB share 2050)
- S1: $8.0B / $0 / 55.2% / 36.5%
- S2: $8.0B / $0 / 55.2% / 19.7%

Expected own NB money: $7.77B. Stated plainly: no order this round brings it within $4B. Hold leaves $8.0B, a cancel $5.7B, and a fold in S4 $5.7B. The cause is the 2030 trigger on a double selection. We never cancel or fold for the metric alone.

2045 PLAN
If our commitment stands and P&W commits only in 2045, the Joint Venture would form after both airframes are in service. A ready Solo would then fold, with about $4B written off and shares equalised. So the 2045 default is `withdraw`, unless that round's grid shows the commit at or above withdraw in every column, the worst column at or above 0, and fleet support undisturbed. Both McCabe and Watson back this.

DOCTRINE NOTE
My own record is Medium on narrowbody launch behaviour and Low on Joint Venture terms. The partner test, the 2045 trigger and the ramp ranges are doctrine and McCabe's numbers, not my record.

## Round 3: decision year 2045

**2045: everyone holds; neither airframer re-engines its widebody**

- Both new narrowbodies are in service. The live levers were the Re-engines, the Open Fan and Rolls-Royce's widebody engines, and every company held.
- Airbus: the A350 Re-engine was worth +$0.89B if Boeing held, under its $1B bar, and no widebody engine would be ready for it.
- Boeing: its Board would have vetoed the 787 Re-engine on its business case: +$0.30B if Airbus held, −$1.26B if Airbus re-engined too. The enabler is met at two sources, "not the three we planned".
- Rolls-Royce: both widebody programmes carried the $5B strain beside the Joint Venture.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Our new single-aisle airplane has been in service for five years now, so let me tell you plainly where it stands. It's in production, it's flying, and our customers have two engine sources on it: CFM, and the engine from Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce. Every step up in production is gated on our KPIs: rework, escapes, traveled work, supplier shortages and training. The data sets the pace, not the calendar. I'd much rather be a month late than go a month early. Safety and quality come first, ahead of rate. We review how both engines are doing in service, every time our Board's safety committee meets, and we keep our suppliers healthy, engines first. On the 787, we're not starting a new development this year. We'll keep growing that airplane out of the capacity we built in Charleston, at the pace our KPIs allow. We'd rather do less and do it better. When there's a business case for the next step on the widebody, we'll bring it, and not before. The balance sheet is where it belongs, and it stayed there through the heaviest spending years. Debt stays first. How and when we return cash to our shareholders is a decision for our Board, and you'll hear about it when the Board has made it. We still develop with our suppliers on cost-plus and risk-share terms, not fixed price. I'm not going to give you any new dates today. We have more share to earn back, and we'll earn it one airplane and one customer at a time. The financials will follow our production performance. We've come a long way. We still have work to do. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our new single-aisle aircraft is in service, and we intend to offer a choice of engines. Every engine joins the aircraft, and stays on it, on proven maturity and durability, never on the calendar. Our priority is the fleet in service, with a disciplined and controlled production ramp of the new single-aisle and the A320neo family and delivery performance for our customers. We continue to secure our own supply chain, engines and critical materials first. The A350 family, passenger and freighter, remains the backbone of our widebody offer, and we will keep developing it and offering it on value to our customers. We will take a further step on the A350 only when the technology, the engine and the business case are all ready. We manage the company with financial discipline, within our financial flexibility and our existing shareholder return policy. We will continue to work with our industry partners for a tariff-free, zero-for-zero regime for aerospace trade. We prepare the next generation of Airbus aircraft on technology gates, one well-prepared programme at a time, for a stronger and more sovereign European aerospace industry. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year. Let me tell you what we know and what we don't. Both new single-aisle aircraft are flying, and CFM is on both of them alongside others. We're honored by that. We also know we don't have a birthright on the next order. We keep our place the same way we earned it: readiness, durability and support, one customer and one engine at a time. CFM is a 50-50 partnership with our partner, and we do this work together. Our ducted engine is in service on both new single-aisle aircraft. Its durability record gets earned in the field, and we'll tell you what the data says as it comes in. If the data shows us something, we'll fix it, together with our customers. Durability doesn't get traded for anything. Our work with Embraer continues, and so does our work on the GEnx. There are no new programmes from us this year. The job now is the fleet we've put in the air. New engines arrive on time for our customers' deliveries first, then the shop work behind the fleet. We'll fund that within our capital allocation framework and keep the balance sheet where it is. RISE remains our next generation. We're all in on the open fan technology, and it stays funded as technology. It's really not for us to say when that architecture enters service. Our customers decide when, and we won't ask anyone to wait. Here's what we don't know: how these fleets will mature in service, and what our customers will want next. We won't give you dates or numbers that our testing and our customers haven't earned. Commercial matters we work privately, with our customers and with our partner. You'll ask about competition. I won't speak to that. We judge ourselves on our own execution. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Bad news first. An engine earns its durability record one flight hour at a time, and our new engine's record on the new single-aisle aircraft is still being earned. Our dates have slipped before, and the GTF fleet taught us what it costs to say more than the data shows. So we will report time on wing as fleet data, and we will claim nothing beyond the hours flown. We have learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. The fleet data will show whether we have, not this statement. Here is what is on the record. The Joint Venture with Rolls-Royce formed in 2035, when both partners committed. What we said then, that nothing had formed, no longer stands. Its engine is built on GTF2, has been ready since 2036, and is on both new single-aisle aircraft alongside the other engine makers. The Joint Venture shares risk and investment, not technology. The gear, the core and the GTF2 technology stay Pratt & Whitney modules, under our design authority. It is one engine. Any change to its configuration would have to earn its own durability record first. The fleet still comes first. We keep inserting the Hot Section durability kit across the GTF fleet, on standard commercial terms. We review the pacing parts every week. Our rule is that new-engine production may not take parts or engineers from fleet recovery, and new capacity is qualified before it ships a part. We control what we can control. On capital, our order of priority does not change: the dividend first, then debt back to pre-ASR levels, then buybacks. There is no new engine programme from us this year. Any further investment would come to our Board on its own return case. Our terms stay standard: no discounts, no concessions, no free upgrades. We do not speak for our airframer customers' plans, dates or rates, or for Rolls-Royce. Those are theirs to announce. |
| Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | So, plainly. The Joint Venture with Pratt & Whitney formed in 2035, and its engine is in service on both new narrowbodies. We said that when we commit, we deliver. We did. Now the fleet comes first: durability in service, life-limited parts at full life, and support through the partner-shop network. If anything has to give, we fix the fleet first and anything new waits. Widebody is our core: no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody, and Trent time on wing keeps improving. We price for value, not share, on standard commercial terms with both airframers. Frankly, there is nothing to launch this year, and we will not launch for the sake of it. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

- Decision rationale: **Decision.** Every order is at its default:
- fps: hold;
- fps engine code: null;
- 737 rate: hold;
- 787 Re-engine: hold.

That is the Do Nothing row: +0.66 in S1 and +0.34 in S2. No order goes to the Board as a change.

**How the team rule ran (teams.md §9)**

1. *Malave's buffer test.* The brief labels no risk-case column. Malave and I read the rule the same way: there is no slip leg this round, the test is not applicable, and no binding veto stands.
   - For the Board, he ran S2 as if it were the risk case. The floor is +0.34 - 2.00 = -1.66.
   - Do Nothing passes at +0.34. The Re-engine row passes at -0.92, $0.74B clear.
   - This is advice only.
2. *KPI doctrine veto (Pope's seat).* It is moot. 737 rate is hold only, since the Rate Increase was a 2030-only lever. The brief reports no live quality, FAA, supply-chain or certification problem.
3. *My decision.* The only live choice is the 787 Re-engine.

**The 787 Re-engine against hold**

Its item value is +0.30 in S1 and -1.26 in S2.
- **Doing less, better [BX-1239].** +0.30 is inside ε ($1B), so the plan with less to execute wins.
- **Doctrine's own rule.** A Re-engine needs a gain above ε, and the A350 must not have re-engined. The first condition fails. This is doctrine (dashboard_game.md §6; profile §6), not my record.
- **Higher-confidence plan [BX-0548].** Hold's worst column is +0.34; the Re-engine's is -0.92.
- **Expectation.** Hold wins at any S2 weight above 19.2% (0.30 / 1.56). At 50% it leads by $0.48B (+0.50 against +0.02).
- **The Board.** It would veto a launch on both of its tests:
  - +0.30 is below its $1B launch test;
  - -1.26 in S2 breaches its -$1B downside limit.
- **Option value.** If Airbus re-engines the A350 now, H3 closes the widebody and hold is right by $1.26B. If it does not, the case comes back in 2050 as a review.

Cash does not decide it. Malave shows the $5B is fundable: it peaks at $1.35B in 2048, and stress free cash flow stays at or above about $15.6B. Value decides it.

Pope adds two points, both advice:
- the 5-year clock has no headroom against our +1 to +4-year slip prior, and $3.3B of cost growth would erase the S1 gain;
- only GE is ready for a 2050 EIS, so the 787 would drop from two engine sources to one.

Both point the same way.

**Expected column: S2, narrowly.** Airbus tied its A350 priority to "until the new aircraft enters service". NGSA has been in service since 2037, and doctrine expects an A350 Re-engine attempt (profile §7, doctrine, not my record).

S1 is close behind, because Rolls-Royce says it will run no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody. Malave plans on about 50%.

I hold no Airbus evidence of my own (Low). Hold is the right order in either column, so the call on the column changes only the premium log.

**Rulings on the hard rules**
- **H1 and H3, first half:** ruled in 2030, spent, not in force.
- **H2:** cannot bind.
- **H3, second half:** not fired, since no A350 Re-engine is on the record.
- **H4:** a 2045 Re-engine (development 2045-50) would overlap the fps development (2033-40) by 0 years, so strain is $0.
- **H5:** nothing to cancel.
- **H6:** the rate is hold only.
- **H7:** no trigger; the FAA-process gate is open.
- **H8:** premium $0 in S2. If S1 turns out to be the column, $0.30B is logged as doctrine premium. Either is inside the $2B cap.

My own red lines are all respected. There is:
- no concurrency;
- no fixed-price development;
- no walking away from a core customer;
- no blame on the FAA or on suppliers;
- no new dates.

**For the Board (it holds me to the case I bring [BG-0022])**
- Submitted row: fps hold + 787 hold, +0.66 (S1) / +0.34 (S2). No item departs from its default, so there is no item-at-default row to show.
- For reference only, the Re-engine row is +0.96 / -0.92, an item value of +0.30 / -1.26.
- The ExCo does not treat S2 as a formal risk case, because the brief labels none. Malave ran it as one for advice.
- Objective cell for the chosen plan, in both columns: 2029 / yes / yes ($0.0B) / 2 (2).

|---|---:|---:|---:|

Net debt is $0 at every checkpoint, base and stress.

**Capital returns.** For the Board's reserved decision I bring Malave's sizing (inference; neither of us has a record on returns):
- a common dividend of about $3B a year;
- a flexible buyback of $0-5B a year;
- the two capped at $8B a year, under half of stress free cash flow;
- no new equity and undrawn revolvers.

It comes with the rating view: solidly investment grade on these metrics, to be confirmed against the agencies.

**Where doctrine, not my record, drove the choice:**
- the Re-engine's ε rule;
- the read on an A350 Re-engine;
- the operations tests in Pope's seat.

The capital-return proposal is inference.
- Expected scenario: S2, narrowly: Airbus launches the A350 Re-engine.

For S2:
- Airbus tied its A350 priority to 'until the new aircraft enters service', and NGSA has been in service since 2037.
- Doctrine expects an A350 Re-engine attempt (profile §7; doctrine, not my record).

For S1 (rivals hold), close behind: Rolls-Royce says it will run no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody.

The brief labels no risk case, and the ExCo does not treat S2 as a formal one. Malave ran it as one for the Board, as advice only.

Hold is the right order in either column.. Best grid plan there: In S2, the best plan is fps: hold + 787: hold (Do Nothing) at +0.34 (100.3%), ahead of fps: hold + 787 Re-engine at -0.92 by $1.26B.

That is the plan I submit. For reference, the best plan in S1 is the Re-engine row at +0.96, which is $0.30B ahead of hold and inside ε.. Premium +0.00 $B: The premium is $0 in my expected column, S2, where the submitted plan is the best grid plan by $1.26B.

If S1 turns out to be the column, the hold costs $0.30B against the Re-engine row (+0.66 against +0.96). I log that now as doctrine premium for three reasons:
- doing less, better [BX-1239];
- doctrine's rule of a Re-engine only when the gain exceeds ε;
- the higher-confidence plan, whose worst column is +0.34 against -0.92 [BX-0548].

The objective premium is $0. No order moves narrowbody share, and debt repair and the engine enabler are met in both rows. The engine code (null, no change) costs nothing.

This round's total is $0, or $0.30B if S1, inside the $2B cap (H8). Cumulatively, with Round 1 ($0.46B) and Round 2 ($0.65B), the total is $1.11B, or at most $1.41B.
- Predictions: **Airbus.** I put the A350 Re-engine at roughly 50-60%: NGSA's EIS has released its one-programme-at-a-time priority, but Rolls-Royce's widebody caution holds it back. No new narrowbody move is likely.

**Engine makers.**
- If Airbus re-engines the A350, Rolls-Royce is the likeliest to answer with a widebody engine. If not, it holds.
- CFM holds and keeps RISE as technology.
- The P&W-Rolls-Royce Joint Venture holds on the narrowbody.

**Boeing on the board.**
- Narrowbody share: 43% in 2045, rising 1 pp a year to 47% from 2049.
- Widebody share: flat at 58.8% (100 a year).
- Operating profit: $15.7B in 2045, rising to $16.9B from 2049.
- In S2 the A350 Re-engine trims our widebody value: the Do Nothing row falls from +0.66 to +0.34.

**Balance sheet.** Free cash flow is $20.94B base and $16.35B stress in 2045, and $21.83B and $17.23B in 2050. Net debt is $0 throughout.

**Problems.** No live quality, FAA or supply problem is expected. The Joint Venture engine's durability is the watch item.

**ΔPV.** +0.34 in S2 (expected), +0.66 in S1, about +0.50 if the two are weighted equally.
- Expected ΔPV +0.34 $B; GM valuation after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: KPI-gated fps production ramp, with every step reported to the Aerospace Safety Committee (public); In-service durability reviews of both fps engine sources (public); Supplier health, engines first: keep fragile suppliers funded and hot (private); 787 growth from Charleston capacity, KPI-gated (public); Capital-return proposal for the Board's reserved decision, sized on the stress case (private); Widebody review in 2050, held only if the A350 has not been re-engined (private); No fixed-price development; no new dates (public)

It reviews pacing parts weekly so that Joint Venture work takes no parts or engineers from that insertion. No completion figure is on record. (c) Either plan's public line can open with the five pillars and use windows: 'at the end of the decade' for a Re-engine. No five-pillars flag on either plan. |

Observed NB engine shares in 2040-44: CFM 27.0% / P&W 36.5% / RR 36.5%. My arithmetic on 2,000 aircraft x 2 engines: - The Joint Venture engine (the P&W + RR shares) is about 2,920 of 4,000 NB engines a year (73%). P&W's GTF was 960 (24%) in 2026. - CFM is about 1,080 against 3,040 in 2026 (36%). Watch items for the safety review, in order: (1) Joint Venture engine durability. It carries most of the volume and inherits GTF2's schedule (ready 2036). In 2035 P&W said GTF2 'has not yet earned its durability record'. Since then the record shows no slip and no durability event, but it also holds no durability data. (2) CFM capacity across NGSA, fps and the aftermarket, which Boeing flags as a watch item. On the record CFM's NB volume is about a third of its 2026 level, so no bind is visible. The aftermarket is not on the record. The brief does not break out Joint Venture engine deliveries to NGSA. |

| (7) A350 Re-engine engine readiness: a committed engine source by EIS 2050 | (Inference.) A named, committed engine maker with an engine ready by the Re-engine's EIS, on the record before the vote. Sources: the Board's 2045 ask ('name and secure its engine before the vote'); rules.md, under which a maker is fitted only if its engine is ready by the airframe's EIS; and company rule 8, under which no engine pushes an airframe's EIS. Timing: - No UltraFan widebody is on the public record. - Under rules.md an UltraFan WB is ready 6 years after launch. A 2045 launch is ready in 2051, one year after a 2050 EIS. - To be ready by 2050 it had to launch by 2044, in the 2035 round, and it did not. Second maker: none on the record. - The GEnx upgrade (ready 2033) and the Trent 1000 upgrade serve the 787. - CFM/GE said in 2035: 'no new programmes from us this year'. - P&W has no widebody engine on this board. Nothing of ours is in development, and rule 7 would bar an NGSA cancel in any case. |

- Decision rationale: DECISION: hold on every lever. The orders are ngsa hold, NGSA engine code null (locked at entry into service), A350 Re-engine hold and Delay Tactics none. There are no Board items and no override.

THE TEAM RULE, STEP BY STEP
(1) Delete red-line and five-pillars breaches. Neither row breaks a pillar.
- Rule 3 holds: no 787 Re-engine is on the record.
- Rule 6 holds: one launch, 0 years of overlap.
- Rule 7 holds: no NGSA cancel.
- Rules 4 and 5: Delay Tactics are closed, because fps has been in service since 2040.
- The A350 Re-engine does fail the company's own launch test (profile §6: at least $1B over Do Nothing). It reaches +0.89 at best, short by $0.11B. 'Do Nothing is a legitimate choice wherever the red lines or the $1B test bind' [A-0440].

(2) Survivors: options that pass both colleagues' tests.
- Hold passes every test in both memos.
- The Re-engine carries two binding soft vetoes:
  - Toepfer: the added-move robustness test fails (+0.89 in S1, -0.50 in S2, under $1B in every column);
  - Wagner: engine production readiness fails. Rolls-Royce is the A350's only source and said 'no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody'. An UltraFan widebody launched in 2045 is ready only in 2051 under rules.md, a year after a 2050 entry into service. No second maker is on the record.
- Neither veto rests on safety, quality, the ramp or a supply crunch.

(3) Highest-grid survivor in the expected column (S1): hold, at +23.37 (S1) and +23.26 (S2). Hold is also the company default, within $1B of the best S1 plan (gap $0.89B). It is the best plan in S2 and the more robust row: its worst column is +23.26, against +22.76.

(4) Override: none is possible.
- The margin over the best passing option (hold) is +0.89 in S1, under the $1B bar, and -0.50 in S2. Weighted, it is +0.54 / +0.47 / +0.40 at 25 / 30 / 35% S2.
- The Board said it would veto any launch carried by an override.
- The override budget stays unused.

(5) Tie-breaks within $1B all favour hold:
- later capex start: none, against $1.0B a year from 2045;
- strain: equal at $0;
- Board items: 0 against 1 (Qualified Majority, $5B, about EUR 4.55bn).

WHY, IN MY ORDER
- Five pillars: nothing covert. Safety leads, and the engine-supply report goes to the safety review [AX-0081, AX-0073].
- The ramp: no new load while the NGSA fleet matures. Joint Venture engine durability and CFM capacity are the watch items [AX-0010, AX-0054].
- Preparing the Airbus of the next decades: the A350 Re-engine is my derivative instinct [AX-0019, AX-0082]. A derivative without a committed engine is not prepared, and the grid does not clear the company's own bar.
- The Board: it named hold as equally acceptable, and it vetoes nothing in it.

WHAT WE GIVE UP, PLAINLY
- +0.89 in S1, about +0.47 weighted at 30% S2.
- A 2050 return (entry into service 2055) is bounded below about $0.61B on today's mechanics (Toepfer's inference), so in practice we are forgoing the A350 Re-engine for this game.
- I accept that as the doctrine premium. It is within the $1B default; the $3B allowance is not engaged.
- Wagner's own note stands as a lesson: the engine gap existed in 2035 too, so any return now needs a committed engine on the record before the vote.

PACKAGE LINE FOR THE BOARD (no items to vote)
- Five pillars: pass, no flag.
- Toepfer: hold passes all tests. Nil programme cash in 2045-2049 against $5.2B. Worst column S2 at +23.26. Net cash rises about $2.45B a year after the dividend on the FY2025 floor. Returns stay inside 30-50%, with no buyback proposed.
- Wagner: pass. No quality event, no supply crunch, ramp shield intact. The engine code is locked: 2 sources and 3 makers.
- Grid: +23.37 (S1) / +23.26 (S2).
- Re-engine held: +0.89 / -0.50, two soft vetoes respected, no override.
- Rule 3 check: no 787 Re-engine on the record.

Boeing's moves set none of this. The order is the same in both columns.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), at about 70%. Boeing's stated priority is 737 MAX and 787 production stability and growing the current 787 out of Charleston. The GEnx upgrade has served the 787 since 2033, and Rolls-Royce plans no new widebody programme.

S2 (Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine) is about 30% (range 25-35%). It is more plausible than in 2035 because Boeing's fps cash peak (2036-37) is past, and it is the worst plausible column for both plans.. Best grid plan there: The best plan in S1 is NGSA hold + A350 Re-engine, at +24.26 (S1) and +22.76 (S2). It is not a survivor: it carries two binding soft vetoes (Toepfer's added-move robustness test and Wagner's engine readiness test), it fails the company's $1B launch test by $0.11B, and no override can carry it.

The chosen plan is the highest-grid survivor and the company default: NGSA hold + A350 hold, at +23.37 (S1) and +23.26 (S2). It is also the best plan in S2.. Premium +0.89 $B: The premium is $0.89B in S1, about $0.47B weighted at 30% S2. In S2, hold is $0.50B better.

It is paid under the doctrine default: hold is within $1B of the best S1 plan. Four further reasons:
- the A350 Re-engine fails the company's own $1B launch test;
- it carries two binding soft vetoes, cash robustness and engine readiness, that no override can reach;
- it has no committed engine;
- hold is the more robust row.

The $3B allowance is not engaged. Nothing is paid for the scored objectives.
- Predictions: Boeing:
- About 70%: Boeing holds the 787 and keeps growing its rate out of Charleston, with no Rate Increase.
- About 30%: it launches a 787 Re-engine with entry into service in 2050. That costs our hold plan only $0.11B (to +23.26) and closes any A350 Re-engine return under rule 3.

Engine makers:
- CFM/GE launches no new programme, and RISE stays funded as technology with no date.
- Rolls-Royce launches no UltraFan widebody (it counts only once a re-engined airframe is in service) and keeps to Trent time-on-wing work.
- The P&W-RR Joint Venture engine and CFM keep supplying both new narrowbodies, with 3 makers in the market.

Our position:
- Narrowbody share falls to 53% by 2049 and stays there to 2060. A350 widebody share holds at 41.2%.
- No Board vote is needed, since the package carries no Board items.
- Expected ΔPV is about +23.34 (+23.37 in S1, +23.26 in S2).
- Expected ΔPV +23.34 $B; GM valuation after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Engine-supply and fleet status report to the Board's twice-yearly safety review (private); Engine-maker risk-sharing invitation kept on standard terms (public); Compete on A350 and A350F price and product (public); A350 Re-engine gate for 2050, with three conditions (private); Secure our own supply, for our own ramp only (public); Delivery performance on NGSA and the A320neo (public); Capital-return guardrail and the net-cash bridge (private); A220-500 stays a study (private); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy through industry coalitions (public)

- Decision rationale: DECISION: HOLD EVERYTHING. All five fields are at hold. That is the default (Do Nothing), so no order is a Board item.

WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]
The narrowbody selections are settled. NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) are both in service on code 7, so the codes are locked. The Joint Venture formed in 2035, and its engine has been ready since 2036. No order on this menu buys back a point of NB share.

So the game is the installed base we have:
- 1,079 CFM NB engines a year on two aircraft we share three ways;
- the LEAP fleet behind them;
- 152 GEnx a year from 2042.
"Our major economics happen in and around the first and second" shop visit [CX-0422].

We win it on execution, in this order: safety, quality, delivery, cost [CX-0658]. That means:
- durability earned in the field, not on analysis [CX-0009];
- OE ahead of spares [CX-0529];
- support that earns the next order, with no birthright [CX-0564];
- RISE kept whole as technology [CX-0214, CX-0469].

GRID AGAINST DO NOTHING (S1, rivals hold: the only column, so it is both the expected and the worst column)
- Hold everything: -69.69 (56.4%). This is the best plan, 0.00 against Do Nothing.
- Open Fan: -95.96, which is -26.27 against hold. That is the $8B bill plus an $18.27B wait penalty: CFM's 27% NB share goes to zero for 2045-2056. My check is $18.28B on 1,079 engines a year.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -91.58, which is -21.89 against hold. Ghai reconciles it: a $9B bill ($8B + $1B) plus $12.89B of NB value lost. With Lobby, CFM keeps 7.9% rather than 0%. The grid charges no second $2B strain, so I adopt his correction of my frame and the Board's $11B. Lobby is worth +4.38 inside the row, and only against a penalty the Open Fan creates.
- Lobby alone is not on the grid. It is -1.00 for no board effect (Ghai), not $3B as my frame allowed.
- Tie-break: not needed. The smallest gap to hold is $21.89B, far outside ε.

TESTS
- Additive and paid for the risk: no row beats hold. S1 is the only column, so no choice of weights changes this.
- Low case first: S1 is the public record. I book below it at -71.
- H2, the airframer sets the date: a 2045 Open Fan is ready in 2055. That is 18 years after NGSA's entry and 15 after fps's, so it waits in the only column. Ali confirms the dates. The one exception, the RISE allowance, would need $21.89B against a $15B cap. Not used.
- H3: the Ducted was the defence the rule calls for, and it is in service. I make no new claim for it, and I never call it the next generation.
- My 20% line binds RISE only. None of Ali's RISE gates is closed on the record.
- Durability line [CX-0208]: a hold trades nothing. The fleet durability report is how I enforce it now.
- H5: RISE stays funded, with no cancel and no program dividend. H8: the statement names no rival, the Joint Venture included, and blames no one.
- H9: premium $0.

A CORRECTION TO MY FRAME (Ali's reconciliation)
My frame said the Ducted had 'its +10 pp NB counting'. Ali shows that the observed shares reconcile only without it:
- 2040: 33.33 + 5 (Embraer) - 10 (Joint Venture) = 28.33 over 105, which is 27.0%, matching the bulletin;
- with the Ducted's +10 it would be 36.5%.
I checked his arithmetic and it holds for 2037-2040. I don't know the board's reason. It changes no order, because the Ducted can only be held. It does change what we say: we claim no NB share for the Ducted, in public or to the Board. Its case is durability and support. If the board's numbers ever show the +10, that is upside, and it is not booked.

FORWARD LOOK (2050, the last round)
- Open Fan: -17.05 against hold.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -15.39, ready in 2060, after the NB window closes in 2056 (Ghai's figures, which match mine).
The second is $0.39B over the allowance, but that is not the point. It would wait in the only column, so H2 binds and the Board vetoes it. It could not meet 'ready by 2050' either. We don't revisit it on hope.

SAFRAN GATE (H7)
There is no new CFM narrowbody order. Fleet support for the Ducted and Embraer on both aircraft stays within joint practice: both airframers fly CFM, and the partners split the aftermarket equally [CX-0219, CX-0184]. No Safran position is asserted. GEnx is GE alone.

EXCO
Both memos recommend all-hold. Every binding test passes, so there are no overrides.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, DROVE THREE THINGS:
- the Embraer hold (profile §6; my record is silent on Embraer);
- the Lobby rule;
- the supply-load read (the strain rule, with my ramp haircut applied to shop visits too, since Ali has no supplier field data).

CAPITAL
- No new R&D. The 70% payout and the A-range rating are whole [CX-0645].
- No new facility. Capex stays within 2-3% of revenue and goes to MRO turnaround.
- Booking: -71. Hold at -69.69 less the larger of two mutually exclusive off-grid WB exposures ($1.13B), rounded down. That raises last round's -77 now that the codes have locked, as we said we would [CX-0654].
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. It is the only column the GM gave. Both new narrowbodies are in service on code 7, so the codes are locked, and the Joint Venture's engine has been ready since 2036. I take it from the public record. The off-grid widebody moves (a Trent 1000 upgrade, or a Re-engine followed by UltraFan WB) are named and labelled as the low case, never weighted into a column. Both airframers' statements argue against a Re-engine (debt first; one programme at a time). The rival engine maker says it starts no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything, at -69.69 (56.4%) in S1. Against Do Nothing it is 0.00 in the expected column and in the worst column, which are the same column.

The other rows against hold:
- Open Fan: -26.27 (-95.96);

OFF-GRID LOW CASE (labelled, not weighted; Ghai's arithmetic, inference)

- Decision rationale: CONTROL WHAT YOU CAN CONTROL. Orders: GTF2 Solo: hold, Joint Venture with RR: hold. These are the brief's only legal values (§3). The grid row 'GTF2: hold' is +8.88 $B (108.1%) in S1, its only column: engine value +10.88 less R&D 2.00.

NO BOARD ITEM IS AVAILABLE, AND WHY
- The cancel window closed in 2035. GTF2 folded into the Joint Venture that year, the engine has been ready since 2036, and an engine can be cancelled only before it is ready.
- A withdraw is illegal, because the Joint Venture formed in 2035 and withdraw is legal only before formation.
- launch and launch if selected cannot be ordered, because no live airframe is waiting to enter service: both new narrowbodies are in service.
- So the 2045 hold-or-withdraw re-test written in 2035 (Eddy's condition d, Mitchill's three-part rule) has nothing left to decide. The Board's request to align it with the Premature exit test is moot for the same reason. For the record, its lapse conditions are checked and none has lapsed:
  - code 7 includes Rolls-Royce on both airframes;
  - nothing is billed beyond $2B;
  - P&W is not trimmed (36.5% each);
  - the modules are kept.
- Miss a cycle does not apply: both cycles are taken.

DECISION BY THE TEAM RULE
- I start from Eddy's proposal: hold/hold.
- Binding vetoes: none stands. Mitchill invokes none of his four grounds on either order. A fitted engine is not an unselected one, and the booked +8.88 is all on the public record. Eddy invokes neither technical ground: no order puts an engine on an airframe, and the brief shows no live fleet problem.
- Conditional vetoes, all respected:
  - Mitchill ground 4: no time-on-wing or 'proven' claim, no objective claim, no lifecycle value as cash, no derivative or 2050 value.
  - Mitchill ground 3: the kit is charged; no concessions.
  - Eddy grounds 1 and 2: no configuration change without its own durability programme; the fleet ring-fence and qualified capacity.
- Safety and durability constraint first [PX-0035][PX-0073]. On this board it binds through other moves, not orders. The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080]: the 1,460 rate waits if the weekly review shows the fleet short.
- Overrides: none.

PREMIUM AND OBJECTIVE
- premium 0. Hold/hold is the only and best plan.
- The cumulative ledger stays at $0.26B of the $1B cap (the round-1 doctrine premium; $0.34B at Mitchill's weights).
- Both objectives are met on the board, with $0 of PV given up.

WHOLE-PROGRAMME VALUE AGAINST PLAN (for the Board, P-1191; Mitchill's numbers, which I trust over my own)
- 2030: launch if selected fired on code 7. Expected PV was +0.23 at my weights and -1.72 at Mitchill's; booked 0.00. That grid's both-select-us column (S5) read +22.00, but it assumed no Rolls-Royce NB engine. Rolls-Royce launched UltraFan NB in 2030 and both airframes took code 7, so by 2035 hold read +3.37.
- 2035 grid:
  - hold: +3.37 / -17.95 / -8.44 / +3.37;
  - hold + commit: +3.37 / -17.95 / -8.44 / +8.88.
  - Expected: +0.39 (mine), +0.07 (Mitchill's). Booked: +3.37.
- Outcome: +8.88, the S4 column. The commit added +5.51 over hold.
- Measures:
  - +8.81 over Mitchill's 2035 expected and +10.60 over his 2030 expected;
  - 13.12 below the 2030 S5 column;
  - return of $10.88B of PV engine value on $2.00B of R&D, 5.4x;
  - board-basis payback on lifecycle value: 2040 nominal, 2041 discounted to 2026. Cash payback is later and not on the board.
- Cost against plan: GTF2 cost its plan, $2.00B, about $0.33B a year in 2030-35, 5.6% of the ~$6B envelope (Mitchill's phasing, not board data). The brief now shows the $2.0B in 2035 rather than 2030. It is the same undiscounted $2.00B booked once, so no value changes. The fold write-off was $0. Mitchill's $2B contingency is released. The Joint Venture asked for no P&W cash beyond the $2B. No new R&D and no write-off this round.

WHAT I OWN
(1) The upside came in the column we weighted lowest: S4 at 0.10 (mine) and 0.08 (Mitchill's). In a sealed, simultaneous round, Rolls-Royce's conditional commit was as free and as dominant for it as ours was for us. We should have weighted it that way. The order was right because it was weakly dominant (0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51), not because we read Rolls-Royce well. The lesson for sealed rounds: when a rival's order is free and conditional, weight it as likely.
(2) The 2030 S5 figure of +22.00 was never the plan. It assumed a market without a Rolls-Royce narrowbody engine.
(3) The Board's 2035 tightening of the statement was not taken up. We published 'GTF2's configuration, date and team do not change', which could be read as a promise of 2036. The 2036 date held on the board. That is counter-evidence to my optimism on dates, not a licence: I state no new date this year.
(4) The pre-EIS reporting to the Governance and Public Policy Committee cannot be evidenced. Our 2035 orders said 'with the next package'. That is on us. It is replaced from today by a standing cadence: weekly to ExCo, quarterly to the Committee, at once on any escape.
(5) Eddy has no fleet data: no hours, AOG, pacing-parts count or kit progress. His parts passes in 2030, 2035 and today rest on the board. So the statement says less, and makes no durability or time-on-wing claim.
(6) The 2040 step from 990 to 1,460 engines a year was +47.5% in one year, and our last escape came from ramp capacity. I order the records look-back as containment, not as a finding.

CAPITAL: dividend first, then debt back to pre-ASR levels, then buybacks.
- Analyst context to 2030E only (Mitchill): the dividend is covered 2.3-2.4x and net debt is 0.76-0.94x EBITDA, with debt back to pre-ASR levels by about 2028-30E (his approximation, inference). Buybacks are the fill-in at $4.0-5.0B a year.
- Past 2030, this rests on board numbers alone.
- The $13.2B against $8.7B (2045, nominal) is lifecycle value at engine price x 1.6, not free cash flow.

BIAS CHECK
- Optimism on dates: none stated.
- Architecture loyalty: the Joint Venture engine is GTF2. The test now is the time-on-wing record, not loyalty. Any Rolls-Royce content is a configuration change and needs its own appropriation and durability programme.
- Value capture: the kit stays charged.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD: the Joint Venture governance positions [P-1233][P-1744][P-1240][P-2004] and the Committee cadence are company doctrine and Eddy's seat inference. My record has no Joint Venture evidence (Low).

2050: unless that brief prices a Board item, hold. Any merged derivative, UltraFan NB content or cash call comes through the gate, and nothing is booked before the record.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. It is the only column on the 2045 grid.. Best grid plan there: 'GTF2: hold' (GTF2 Solo: hold, Joint Venture with RR: hold): +8.88 $B (108.1%) in S1. It is the only row, and so the best.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium. Hold/hold is the only legal plan and the best on the grid, so no doctrine-favoured order trails the best expected PV, and no Board item exists. The cumulative ledger stays at $0.26B of the team's $1B cap: the round-1 doctrine premium, $0.34B at Mitchill's weights.
- Predictions: S1 = 1.00 (rivals hold), the expected column and the only column on the 2045 grid.
- Expected ΔPV +8.88 $B; GM valuation after the round +8.88 $B

- Other moves: GTF Hot Section durability kit insertion continues across the fleet, charged (public); Joint Venture governance: one engine, P&W modules, P&W design authority (public); Fleet ring-fence, weekly pacing-parts review and a records look-back on the 2040 capacity step (private); Standing durability and parts report to the Governance and Public Policy Committee (private); Fleet-data cadence to Airbus and Boeing (private); Capital-order note to the Finance Committee (private); Whole-programme value against plan, reported to the Board (private)

DEFENCE: the Trent time-on-wing work inside the existing budget, as an other move: - longer-life HP turbine blades on the Trent 1000 and Trent 7000; - life extension for life-limited parts; - Trent XWB shop-visit cost down. No programme, no strain. |
| Technology pull-through | A cancelled, idle or folded UltraFan still feeds the Trent fleet. A qualitative note; grid values are never adjusted for it (inference). | not applicable | The Solo folded in 2035 onto GTF2's frozen test plan, so UltraFan technology did not go into the Joint Venture configuration. - It stays RR's and feeds Trent time on wing: a 2-way flow. - That is also how our technology is protected under P&W's 'risk and investment, not technology' terms (inference). - On the record we hold no module design authority in the Joint Venture engine. What we bring there is risk, investment and the support network. - RX-0072 ('we are actually bringing a technology') applies to any later engine under our own design authority, and the governance note should say so plainly. - The IAE lesson is alignment between partners (R-0318). Qualitative; the grid is unchanged. |

- Decision rationale: DECISION
Every field is at hold:
- `UltraFan NB Solo: hold`
- `Joint Venture with P&W: hold`
- `UltraFan WB: hold`
- `Trent 1000 upgrade: hold`
This is my framing proposal, unchanged. Both memos recommend the same orders, and there are no Board items. Do Nothing on widebody (Trent only) is right because no airframe can use a new widebody engine in time [RX-0058].

GRID (brief rows, S1-S3)
- "UltraFan NB: hold": +32.35 / +32.35 / +32.61. Components: engine value +38.07, R&D -4.00, write-offs -1.71. It is the best plan in every column.
- Weights S1-S3: 0.80 / 0.12 / 0.08. McCabe confirmed them.
  - S1 (rivals hold): neither airframer has signalled a Re-engine. Boeing puts 787 customers and Charleston capacity first; Airbus runs "one well-prepared programme at a time".
  - S2 (787 Re-engine): 0.12. Boeing is the likelier mover, with fps in service since 2040.
  - S3 (both Re-engines): 0.08.
- Expected PV: +32.37 at my weights, +32.38 at the Board's illustration (0.80 / 0.10 / 0.10), +32.40 at McCabe's stress weights (0.60 / 0.20 / 0.20). Worst column +32.35. Premium $0.

ROWS STRUCK, as gain over hold
- "UltraFan NB: hold + T1000 upgrade": -6.47 in every column, so -6.47 under any weights. Worst -6.47 against its own $2B bill. Without the $5B strain it is still -1.47: about +0.53 of engine value against $2B.
  - It fails the four-part gate on business model [RX-0030].
  - The franchise test is not triggered: widebody share is 55.2%, above the line.
  - The shortfall is $6.47B, above the $3B premium cap.
- "UltraFan NB: hold + UltraFan WB": -9.00 / -8.27 / -8.02, so -8.83 at my weights and the Board's. Worst -9.00 against a $4B bill.
  - No 787 or A350 Re-engine is on the record, and Boeing has not named UltraFan.
  - Launched now, it is ready in 2051, a year after a 2045 Re-engine's 2050 EIS, so widebody share in 2050 stays 55.2%.
  - The shortfall is far above the $1B UltraFan WB sub-cap.
- Upgrade and UltraFan WB together: exclusive, not allowed.

VETO CHECKS
- McCabe withholds her co-signature on both widebody launches. Her binding withholds touch only orders I do not give. She invokes no balance-sheet ground: board debt is unchanged, no equity, leverage well below about 1.5x, no borrowing for distributions.
- Watson invokes no Civil veto at hold. The Joint Venture engine has been ready since 2036, a year before NGSA's 2037 EIS and four before fps 2040. No support-before-EIS test arises.
- He reserved step 4 for the final text. I kept to his cautions:
  - no date for any new programme, retrofit, kit or growth engine;
  - no Joint Venture date beyond the record;
  - nothing on Re-engines or any airframer's dates or rates;
  - no claim that an UltraFan-core engine sits in the Joint Venture;
  - no Joint Venture terms beyond the record.
- The statement says only that the Joint Venture formed in 2035 and its engine is in service on both new narrowbodies, which is on the public record.
- `overrides` is empty.

RED-LINE CHECK
No colleague flagged a breach. All four fields are at hold, so:
- no UltraFan launches without a programme to fly it;
- no early date;
- no overlap and no strain (Control's strain line stays at $0);
- no cancel;
- no item can lose more than its own bill.
Nothing to strike.

CLOSED ITEMS
- Last round's private 2045 withdraw trigger was written for a P&W that had not committed. P&W committed in 2035 and the Joint Venture formed, so the trigger is moot and closed. Withdraw is not an order this round.
- The Solo folded in 2035; its $1.71B write-off is sunk.

WHAT THE ROUND IS REALLY ABOUT: quality of earnings, not share [RX-0054]
- The cash now sits in the installed fleet: about 10,233 engines in our share by end-2044, 1,460 more a year.
- The Trent 1000 is the movie I have seen before [RX-0087, RX-0114].
- Watson names the gap plainly. P&W holds design authority over the gear and the core and is still inserting its Hot Section kit across the GTF fleet. We carry an equal share of risk without the hot-section data.
- McCabe names the money side. Who pays for a fix in a P&W module is not settled on the record; plan on half of a Trent 1000-type bill (about £1.1bn), with a worst case of about £2.2bn.
- Both go into the governance note to the Board before 2050, a private move. The test I apply to the partner is binary: it credits what we bring and shares data in proportion to the risk we carry, or the relationship is transactional [RX-0050, RX-0072]. That test on a partner is inference, not my record.
- I accept McCabe's correction: the Joint Venture halves what we share (support, any retrofit), not the early-unit losses on our own 1,460 engines a year.

CONTROL'S METRICS for the submitted plan, the same in every column
- Own narrowbody money: $5.71B against $4B. NOT MET, and final. It is the $4B Joint Venture share plus the $1.71B written off at the 2035 fold. The cause was the 2030 double selection. No narrowbody order is open, and we never cancel or fold for the metric.
- Strain: $0. MET.
- RR widebody share in 2050: 55.2%. MET.
- RR narrowbody share in 2050: 36.5%. MET.
- Airbus widebody airframe share in 2050: 41.2% / 40.2% / 41.2%. The S2 A350 exposure is watched qualitatively.

WHERE THIS IS DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD
- My record is Low on Joint Venture terms and on rivals. The partner alignment, retrofit-liability and data-access asks are company doctrine (the IAE lesson, R-0318) and McCabe's and Watson's inference.
- The funding order and the ramp numbers are McCabe's.
- The readiness and support views are Watson's.
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold (weight 0.80). Neither airframer has signalled a Re-engine: Boeing puts 737 MAX and 787 customers and Charleston capacity first, and Airbus its A320neo and A350 families, one programme at a time. S2, a 787 Re-engine, is 0.12; S3, both Re-engines, is 0.08. Hold is the best plan in every column, so the choice does not depend on the weights.. Best grid plan there: "UltraFan NB: hold" (every field at hold): +32.35 in S1, and +32.35 / +32.61 in S2 / S3. It is the best grid plan in all three columns. Expected +32.37 at 0.80 / 0.12 / 0.08.. Premium +0.00 $B: None. The submitted plan is the best grid plan in every column. No doctrine-favoured order is kept against the grid. The upgrade would need $6.47B and UltraFan WB $8.83B, both above their caps ($3B, and $1B for UltraFan WB), and neither is ordered.
- Predictions: Rivals this round:
- Boeing: no 787 Re-engine (about 0.88 including S1). It keeps 737 MAX and 787 rates and fps first.
- Airbus: no A350 Re-engine (about 0.92). A320neo, A350 and NGSA first.
- P&W: holds the Joint Venture as formed. It keeps the GTF Hot Section kit insertion going and says nothing new on terms.
- CFM/GE: holds, with RISE funded as technology and no new product order.
- Codes on NGSA and fps stay 7.
Our position on hold:
- RR narrowbody share 36.5% and widebody share 55.2% in 2050 in every column.
- ΔPV +32.35 in S1 and S2, +32.61 in S3. Expected +32.37.
- Strain $0. Own narrowbody money $5.71B, final.
2050, the last round: I expect every field at hold again. A widebody row passes only if a Re-engine goes on the record and the grid clears the caps. McCabe's advance test makes that unlikely.
- Expected ΔPV +32.37 $B; GM valuation after the round +32.35 $B

- Other moves: Fleet first on the Joint Venture engine in service (public); Quarterly fleet report to the Safety, Energy Transition & Tech Committee (Watson) (private); Joint Venture governance and alignment note to the Board before the 2050 round (private); Funding discipline and quarterly report to the Board (McCabe) (private); Trent time on wing and fleet support inside the existing budget (Watson) (public); Commercial terms with Airbus and Boeing (public); The 2050 test, set in advance (private)


## Round 4: decision year 2050

**2050: everyone holds again, and the final state is the 2035 state**

Every company held, and no value has changed since 2035. A Re-engine ordered in 2050 would enter service only in 2055. Airbus's A350 Re-engine was worth +$0.57B if Boeing held and −$0.41B if not; Boeing's 787 Re-engine +$0.05B or −$1.00B. Boeing's Board turned to capital returns sized on the stress case.

### Orders (as adjudicated)

| Player | Orders | Public statement |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Do Nothing | Our new single-aisle airplane has been in service for ten years now. It's in production, it's flying, and our customers have two engine sources on it: CFM, and the engine from Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce. We're holding the line where the data tells us to hold it. Every step in production has been gated on our KPIs: rework, escapes, traveled work, supplier shortages and training. That doesn't change. The data sets the pace, not the calendar. I'd much rather be a month late than go a month early. Safety and quality come first, ahead of rate. Both engines are reviewed for how they're doing in service every time our Board's safety committee meets, and we keep our suppliers healthy, engines first. Our development and support work with suppliers stays on cost-plus and risk-share terms, not fixed price. On the 787, we're not starting a new development. We'll keep growing that airplane out of the capacity we built in Charleston, at the pace our KPIs and our customers' orders allow. We'd rather do less and do it better. The balance sheet is where it belongs, and debt stays first. How and when we return cash to our shareholders is a decision for our Board, and you'll hear about it when the Board has made it. I'm not going to give you any new dates today. We have more share to earn back, and we'll earn it one airplane and one customer at a time. The financials will follow our production performance. We've come a long way. We still have work to do. |
| Airbus | Do Nothing | Our core principles of safety, quality, integrity, compliance and security will remain at the heart of all we do. Our new single-aisle aircraft is in service, and we intend to offer a choice of engines. Every engine joins the aircraft, and stays on it, on proven maturity and durability, never on the calendar. We review the fleet and our engine partners' durability and capacity with our Board as a matter of routine, and we keep working with our partners to keep every source healthy. Our priority is the fleet in service, with a disciplined and controlled production ramp of the new single-aisle and the A320neo family and delivery performance for our customers. We continue to secure our own supply chain, engines and critical materials first. The A350 family, passenger and freighter, remains the backbone of our widebody offer. We will keep developing it and offering it on value to our customers. We have looked again at a further step on the A350. Today the engine and the business case are not both there, so we are not taking that step. As we have said, we will take it only when the technology, the engine and the business case are all ready. We manage the company with financial discipline, within our financial flexibility and our existing shareholder return policy. We will continue to work with our industry partners for a tariff-free, zero-for-zero regime for aerospace trade. We prepare the next generation of Airbus aircraft on technology gates, one well-prepared programme at a time, for a stronger and more sovereign European aerospace industry. |
| CFM/GE | Do Nothing | Safety, quality, delivery and cost, always in that order. That doesn't change this year. Let me tell you what we know and what we don't. Both new single-aisle aircraft are in service, and CFM is on both of them alongside others. We're honored by that. We don't have a birthright on any order. We keep our place the same way we earned it: readiness, durability and support, one customer and one engine at a time. CFM is a 50-50 partnership with our partner, and we do this work together. Our ducted engine is in service on both new single-aisle aircraft. Its durability record is earned in the field, and we'll tell you what the data says as it comes in. If the data shows us something, we'll fix it, together with our customers. Durability doesn't get traded for anything. Our work with Embraer continues, and so does our work on the GEnx. There are no new programmes from us this year. The job is the fleet we've put in the air. New engines for our customers' deliveries come first, then the shop work behind the fleet. We'll fund that within our capital allocation framework and keep the balance sheet where it is. RISE remains our next generation. We're all in on the open fan technology, and it stays funded as technology. It's really not for us to say when that architecture enters service. Our customers decide when, and we won't ask anyone to wait. Here's what we don't know: how these fleets will mature in service, and what our customers will want next. We won't give you dates or numbers that our testing and our customers haven't earned. Commercial matters we work privately, with our customers and with our partner. You'll ask about competition. I won't speak to that. We judge ourselves on our own execution. It's that simple, ain't that hard. |
| Pratt & Whitney | Do Nothing | Bad news first. All of our new single-aisle business now rides on one engine, and that engine's durability record on the new single-aisle aircraft is still being earned, one flight hour at a time. Our dates have slipped before, and the GTF fleet taught us what it costs to say more than the data shows. We have said we learned our lesson from the prior iterations of the GTF. Take that as a commitment, not a finding, and measure us on the fleet data, not on this statement. Our customers have said plainly where the bar is. An engine joins an aircraft, and stays on it, on proven durability, and any durability finding is a live problem. That is fair, and we accept it. We answer it with the engine and with the data, not with price. Our rule is that time on wing, removals and fix campaigns go to both airframers as the fleet flies, and we claim nothing beyond the hours flown. Our terms stay standard: no discounts, no concessions, no free upgrades. Which engines go on an aircraft, and how many sources it carries, is our customers' decision and theirs to announce. What is on the record: the Joint Venture with Rolls-Royce formed in 2035. Its engine has been ready since 2036 and is on both new single-aisle aircraft alongside the other engine makers. The gear, the core and the GTF2 technology stay Pratt & Whitney modules, under our design authority. It is one engine, and any change to its configuration would have to earn its own durability record first. The fleet still comes first. Insertion of the Hot Section durability kit continues across the GTF fleet, charged, on standard commercial terms. Our rules: we review the pacing parts every week, new-engine production may not take parts or engineers from fleet recovery, and new capacity is qualified before it ships a part. If we find a problem, you will hear it from us, with containment and a dated recovery. We control what we can control. On capital, our order of priority does not change: the dividend first, then debt back to and held at pre-ASR levels, then buybacks. There is no new engine programme from us this year. Any further investment would come to our Board on its own return case. We do not speak for our airframer customers' plans, dates, rates or sourcing, or for Rolls-Royce. |
| Rolls-Royce | Do Nothing | So, plainly. The Joint Venture with Pratt & Whitney formed in 2035, and its engine is in service on both new narrowbodies. Now it has to earn its keep in the fleet. The fleet comes first: durability in service, life-limited parts at full life, and support through the joint partner-shop network. We will report what the fleet shows, and claim nothing more. If anything has to give, we fix the fleet first and anything new waits. Widebody is our core: no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody, and Trent time on wing keeps improving. We price for value, not share, on standard commercial terms with both airframers. Frankly, there is nothing to launch this year. We won't do anything not profitable. I will say that categorically. |

- Decision rationale: **Decision.** Every order stays at its default:
- fps: hold;
- fps engine code: null;
- 737 rate: hold;
- 787 Re-engine: hold.

That is the Do Nothing row: +0.66 in S1 and +0.57 in S2. No order goes to the Board as a change.

**How the team rule ran (teams.md §9)**
1. *Malave's buffer test.* The brief (round4.md §3) labels no risk-case column. Malave and I read the rule the same way: there is no slip leg this round, the test does not apply, and no binding veto stands.
   - For the Board he ran S2 as if it were the risk case. The floor is +0.57 - 2.00 = -1.43.
   - Do Nothing passes by $2.00B. The Re-engine row (-0.43) passes by $1.00B.
   - This is advice only.
2. *KPI doctrine veto (Pope's seat).* It is moot. 737 rate is hold only, and Pope states explicitly that the brief reports no live quality, FAA, supply-chain or certification problem.
3. *My decision.* The only live choice is the 787 Re-engine. Both colleagues recommend all hold, code null, and their non-binding objections to the Re-engine match mine.

**The 787 Re-engine against hold: +0.05 in S1, -1.00 in S2**
- **Doing less, better [BX-1239].** +0.05 is far inside ε ($1B), so the plan with less to execute wins.
- **Doctrine's Re-engine rule.** A Re-engine needs a gain above ε (dashboard_game.md §6; doctrine, not my record). It fails.
- **Higher-confidence plan [BX-0548].** Hold's worst column is +0.57; the Re-engine's is -0.43.
- **Expectation.** A - B = -0.05 + 1.05p, where p is the weight on S2, so hold wins at any S2 weight above 4.8%. At Malave's 30%, hold leads +0.63 to +0.37. At 50%, it leads +0.62 to +0.14. Even at 0%, the Re-engine's +0.05 sits inside ε.
- **Execution.** The 5-year clock has no headroom against our +1 to +4-year slip prior. About $0.90B of cost growth, 18% of the $5B bill, would erase the S1 gain on its own (Malave and Pope agree).
- **Sourcing.** Only GE's GEnx upgrade is ready for a 2055 EIS. The UltraFan WB could not be ready before 2056, and Rolls-Royce says it will run no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody. The 787 would drop from two engine sources to one, a supply risk the board does not price.
- **The Board** would veto a launch: it fails the $1B launch test in either column, and S2 sits at its -$1B limit.
- **Cash does not decide it.** The plan is fundable: stress free cash flow stays at or above $16.17B. Value decides it.
- **No option value.** 2050 is the last decision year, so I close the widebody question for this game and grow the 787 out of Charleston [BX-1317, B-2336].

**Expected column: S1 (rivals hold).**
- Airbus held the A350 Re-engine in 2045 and said so in public. It now gates any further A350 step on 'the technology, the engine and the business case' all being ready.
- Rolls-Royce says it has nothing to launch and will run no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody.
- My Round 3 read of S2 was wrong, and I own it [BX-1319]. Malave agrees on S1 and keeps 30% S2 as a conservative baseline.
- My own evidence on Airbus is Low. The order is the same in either column, and nothing here answers Airbus [B-2313].

**Rulings on the hard rules**
- **H1 and H3, first half:** ruled in 2030 with evidence; spent, not in force.
- **H2:** cannot bind.
- **H3, second half:** not fired. No A350 Re-engine is on the record, and Airbus held it in 2045.
- **H4:** a 2050-55 Re-engine would overlap the fps development (2033-40) by 0 years, so strain is $0. H4 does not strike it.
- **H5:** nothing to cancel.
- **H6:** the rate is hold only.
- **H7:** no trigger. The brief reports no certification trouble, so the FAA-process gate is open.
- **H8:** $0.05B of doctrine premium is logged (S1). The running log is 0.46 + 0.65 + 0.30 + 0.05 = $1.46B, with Round 3's $0.30B counted after the fact. Each round, and the total, is inside the $2B cap.

My own red lines are all respected:
- no production-certification concurrency;
- no fixed-price development;
- no walking away from a core customer;
- no blame on the FAA or attribution to suppliers;
- no new dates.

**For the Board (it holds me to the case I bring [BG-0022])**
- Submitted row: fps hold + 787 hold, +0.66 (S1) / +0.57 (S2).
- No item departs from its default, so there is no item-at-default row.
- For reference, the Re-engine row is +0.71 / -0.43, an item value of +0.05 / -1.00.
- The ExCo does not treat S2 as a formal risk case.
- Objective cell in both columns: 2029 / yes / yes ($0.0B) / 2 (2).

**Debt path.** Malave's rebuild, which Pope confirms:
- 2050 free cash flow is $21.83B base and $17.23B stress.
- Net debt is $0.0B at end-2035, 2040, 2045 and 2050, in base and stress.
- With a Re-engine, stress free cash flow would have been 16.76 / 16.52 / 16.33 / 16.17 / 16.45 over 2050-54, still at $0 net debt. That is not taken.

**Capital returns.** I bring the proposal updated to 2050 as a reserved matter, not a game order:
- a dividend of about $3B;
- a buyback of $0-5B, authorised yearly;
- an $8B cap, 46% of stress free cash flow;
- under stress, net cash of about $44B by end-2054, with revolvers undrawn and no new equity.

It is conditional on the agencies' written confirmation of the rating. We infer none.

**Where doctrine, not my record, drove a choice:**
- the Re-engine's ε rule;
- the read on Airbus;
- Pope's operations tests (KPIs, strain, suppliers).

The capital-return proposal is inference.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold.

Why S1:
- Airbus held the A350 Re-engine in 2045, said so publicly, and now gates any further A350 step on 'the technology, the engine and the business case' all being ready.
- Rolls-Royce says it has nothing to launch and will run no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody.

My Round 3 read of S2 was wrong, and I own it [BX-1319]. Malave agrees on S1 and plans on about 30% S2 as a conservative baseline. My own evidence on Airbus is Low.

The brief labels no risk case, and the ExCo does not treat S2 as a formal one. Hold is the right order in either column, so the call on the column changes only the premium log.. Best grid plan there: In S1, the best grid plan is fps: hold + 787 Re-engine at +0.71 (100.5%), ahead of fps: hold + 787: hold (Do Nothing) at +0.66 by $0.05B. That is well inside ε.

I submit Do Nothing because:
- doing less, better breaks the tie [BX-1239];
- it is the higher-confidence plan, with a worst column of +0.57 against -0.43 [BX-0548];
- doctrine requires a Re-engine gain above ε.

The $0.05B is logged as doctrine premium. In S2, Do Nothing is the best plan outright, by $1.00B.. Premium +0.05 $B: In my expected column, S1, holding the 787 Re-engine costs $0.05B against the Re-engine row (+0.66 against +0.71). I log it as doctrine premium for three reasons:
- doing less, better [BX-1239];
- doctrine's rule that a Re-engine needs a gain above ε (dashboard_game.md §6);
- the higher-confidence plan, whose worst column is +0.57 against -0.43 [BX-0548].

The objective premium is $0. No order moves narrowbody share, and debt repair and the engine enabler are met in both rows. The engine code (null, unchanged) costs nothing. If S2 occurs, the premium is $0 after the fact.

Running H8 log:
- Round 1: $0.46B;
- Round 2: $0.65B;
- Round 3: $0.30B, logged $0 at the time and counted after the fact since Airbus held;
- Round 4: $0.05B.

The total is $1.46B, $0.54B inside the $2B cap, and each round is inside the cap on its own.
- Predictions: **Airbus.** I put the A350 Re-engine at about 20-30%:
- it held in 2045 and gates any step on technology, engine and business case;
- Rolls-Royce's widebody caution holds it back.

No new narrowbody move from Airbus is likely; NGSA's production ramp is its priority.

**Engine makers.**
- CFM holds, with RISE kept as technology and no launch.
- The P&W-Rolls-Royce Joint Venture holds on the narrowbody.
- Rolls-Royce launches no widebody engine unless Airbus re-engines the A350.
- P&W holds.

**Boeing on the board.**
- Narrowbody share flat at 47% (940 a year).
- Widebody share flat at 58.8% (100 a year).
- Operating profit of $16.9B a year from 2049.

**Balance sheet.** 2050 free cash flow is $21.83B base and $17.23B stress, with net debt $0 throughout.

**Problems.** No live quality, FAA or supply problem is expected. The Joint Venture engine's durability is the watch item.

**ΔPV.**
- +0.66 in S1 (expected);
- +0.57 if Airbus re-engines the A350;
- about +0.63 at a 30% S2 weight.
- Expected ΔPV +0.66 $B; GM valuation after the round +0.66 $B

- Other moves: KPI-gated fps production, with every step reported to the Aerospace Safety Committee (public); In-service durability reviews of both fps engine sources (public); Supplier health, engines first: keep fragile suppliers funded and hot (private); Third-source contingency for the fps, outside the board, held ready but not triggered (private); Widebody question closed for this game: 787 growth from Charleston capacity, KPI-gated (public); Capital-return proposal for the Board's reserved decision, updated to 2050 and sized on the stress case (private); No fixed-price development; no new dates; no blame (public)

**Airbus** (Guillaume Faury, Thomas Toepfer, Lars Wagner; Airbus Board)

- Decision rationale: DECISION: every lever at its default. NGSA hold (code locked), A350 hold, Delay Tactics none. The package carries no Board items and no override. Row: +23.37 (S1) / +23.33 (S2).

I applied the team rule step by step.

1. RED LINES AND FIVE PILLARS
- Neither option breaks one, so nothing is deleted.
- Rule 3 is met: no 787 Re-engine is on the record.
- Rule 6 is met: one launch at most, 0 years of overlap, and the A220-500 stays a study.
- Rules 4 and 5: Delay Tactics have been closed since fps entered service in 2040, and only 'none' is open.
- Rule 7: no NGSA cancel exists or is wanted.
- Neither Toepfer nor Wagner raises a five-pillars flag on either plan, and nothing in the package is covert.

2. SURVIVORS
Hold passes every test in both memos. The A350 Re-engine fails two binding soft vetoes:
- Toepfer's added-move robustness test. It is the same ground as the company's $1B launch test and as condition 1 of our own gate. The item is +0.57 (S1) / -0.41 (S2), $0.43B short in S1 and negative in S2.
- Wagner's engine-readiness test, condition 3 of our gate. No committed engine ready by a 2055 entry into service is on the record. Rolls-Royce, the only A350 source, says 'no new widebody programme beside the narrowbody', and an UltraFan widebody launched in 2050 is ready only in 2056.
Neither failure rests on safety, quality, the ramp or a supply crunch. Both are soft vetoes, and I respect both.

3. HIGHEST SURVIVOR IN THE EXPECTED COLUMN
Hold is the only survivor. Even if both tests had passed, the doctrine default would keep hold: it is within $1B of the best S1 plan (gap $0.57B).

4. OVERRIDE
None is available. The margin over the best passing option (hold) is +0.57 in S1, under the $1B bar. On this Qualified Majority item it also trails hold by $0.41B in S2, the worst plausible column. The Board said it would veto any override. The game's one override stays unused.

5. TIE-BREAKS (all favour hold)
- no early entry into service;
- less concurrency and strain;
- a later capex start (hold has none);
- fewer Board items (none against one).

THE GRID
Expected column: S1, about 75% (range 65-80%). Worst plausible column: S2, about 25% (range 20-35%). Boeing's 2045 statement covered that year only, so S2 is not ruled out.

| | S1 | S2 | at 25% S2 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Hold | +23.37 | +23.33 | +23.36 |
| Re-engine | +23.94 | +22.92 | +23.69 |
| Item (Re-engine minus hold) | +0.57 | -0.41 | +0.33 |

- The item breaks even at 58% S2. Weighted at 20 / 25 / 30 / 35% S2 it is worth +0.37 / +0.33 / +0.28 / +0.23.
- Hold is the more robust row: its worst column is +23.33 against +22.92.
- Wagner adds a point the grid cannot see: the board prices the Re-engine without an engine. Production cannot.

THE GATE
The ExCo's 2050 gate, recorded in 2045, is applied as written and not set aside. Condition 1 fails, condition 2 holds and condition 3 is not met. The hold is recorded as a business-case decision, not a reaction to Boeing. It closes the A350 Re-engine for the game.

WHAT HOLD FORGOES
+0.57 in S1, about +0.33 weighted at 25% S2. This is the doctrine premium inside the $1B default, recorded and not hidden. Toepfer's 2045 bound (under $0.61B) is borne out. The $3B allowance is not engaged.

BOARD-GATE LINE (package; no Board items)
- Five pillars: pass, no flag (Toepfer and Wagner).
- Toepfer's test: pass. Nil programme cash in 2050-2055 against the $5.2B line. Worst column +23.33. Net cash rises about $2.45B a year after the dividend. Returns inside 30-50%, no equity raise, no dividend cut, no new buyback proposed.
- Wagner's test: pass. No safety, quality, ramp or crunch flag. The engine code is locked, with 2 sources and 3 makers in 2050.
- Grid ΔPV: +23.37 (S1) / +23.33 (S2).
- The A350 Re-engine is held. Item +0.57 / -0.41, both soft vetoes respected, no override.

IN MY ORDER OF PAID OBJECTIVES
- Five pillars: safety leads. The engine-supply report goes to the safety review [AX-0081, AX-0073, AX-0015].
- The ramp: no new load while the NGSA fleet matures. Joint Venture durability and CFM capacity remain the watch items [AX-0010, AX-0054].
- Preparing the next decades: NGSA is in service with two sources. A derivative without a committed engine is not prepared. The next generation is prepared on technology gates, not on Boeing's calendar [AX-0020, AX-0019, AX-0082].
- The Board: it approves a hold and vetoes nothing in it.
- Expected scenario: S1 (rivals hold), about 75%, range 65-80%. Worst plausible column: S2 (Boeing launches a 787 Re-engine), about 25%, range 20-35%. Boeing's 2045 line ('not starting a new development this year') and its Charleston 787 growth 'in place of a second development' lower S2, but that statement covered 2045 only, so S2 is not ruled out for 2050.. Best grid plan there: NGSA: hold + A350 Re-engine, +23.94 in S1 (+22.92 in S2). It is not chosen because it fails two binding soft vetoes: Toepfer's added-move and $1B test (gate condition 1), and Wagner's engine readiness (gate condition 3). The doctrine default (hold, +23.37) is within $1B of it, and no override can reach $1B. Hold is the best plan in S2 (+23.33) and the more robust row.. Premium +0.57 $B: This is the doctrine premium. Hold gives up +0.57 in S1 against the best S1 plan (the A350 Re-engine), or about +0.33 weighted at 25% S2 (+0.23 to +0.37 over 20-35%). In S2, hold is $0.41B better. It is paid because the Re-engine fails the company's $1B launch test and two of the ExCo's three gate conditions (short of $1B; no committed engine ready by 2055), and because hold is within $1B of the best S1 plan, so the default carries. The $3B allowance is not engaged. Nothing is paid for the 60/40 objective or the engine enabler.
- Predictions: Boeing:
- About 75%: Boeing holds the 787, keeps growing it out of Charleston capacity, gives no new dates, and makes no Rate Increase.
- About 25%: it launches a 787 Re-engine, entering service around 2055. That costs our hold plan only $0.04B (to +23.33).
- fps keeps its KPI-gated 5-a-month steps.

Engine makers:
- CFM/GE launches no new programme, and RISE stays funded as technology with no date.
- Rolls-Royce launches no UltraFan widebody and keeps to Trent time-on-wing work inside its budget.
- P&W launches nothing new and continues Hot Section kit insertion, charged.
- The Joint Venture engine and CFM keep supplying both new narrowbodies, with 3 makers in the market in 2050.

Our position:
- Narrowbody share stays at 53% (1,060 a year) to 2060, and A350 widebody share holds at 41.2% (70 a year).
- No Board vote is needed: the package has no Board items, and the Board approves the hold.
- Expected ΔPV is about +23.36 (+23.37 in S1, +23.33 in S2).
- Expected ΔPV +23.36 $B; GM valuation after the round +23.37 $B

- Other moves: Engine-supply and fleet status report to the Board's twice-yearly safety review, with an unpriced contingency view (private); Engine-maker risk-sharing invitation kept on standard terms (public); Compete on A350 and A350F price and product (public); A350 Re-engine hold recorded as a business-case decision, closing it for the game (private); Secure our own supply, for our own ramp only (public); Delivery performance on NGSA and the A320neo (public); Capital-return guardrail and the net-cash bridge (private); A220-500 stays a study (private); Prepare the next generation on technology gates (public); Zero-for-zero trade advocacy through industry coalitions (public)
