- Decision rationale: DECISION: HOLD EVERYTHING. All five fields are at `hold`. Every order is the default, so none is a Board item.

WHAT GAME, HOW WE WIN [CX-0467]
The narrowbody game was settled when fps entered service in 2040. NGSA (2037) and fps (2040) are both flying CFM + P&W + RR with their codes locked. No order on this menu buys back a point of NB share. The game now is the fleet we've put in the air: 1,079 CFM NB engines a year and 152 GEnx, and the shop visits behind them [CX-0422, CX-0630]. We win it on safety, quality, delivery and cost, in that order [CX-0658]. Both airframers now judge engines on proven durability, and only field data moves that [CX-0009, CX-0564].

THE GRID (S1, rivals hold, the only column, so it is both the expected and the worst column)
- Hold everything: -69.69 (56.4%). This is the best plan, 0.00 against Do Nothing.
- Open Fan: -86.74 (45.8%), -17.05 against hold. That is the $8B bill plus $9.05B of NB value lost over 2050-56: CFM goes from 27.0% to 0%, losing 1,079 engines a year.
- Open Fan + Lobby: -85.08 (46.8%), -15.39 against hold. That is a $9B bill plus $6.39B lost, with CFM keeping 7.9% and no second strain. Lobby is +1.66 inside this row, but only against a penalty the Open Fan itself creates.
- Lobby alone: not a grid row. It costs $1B for no board effect, so -1.00.
Ghai, Ali and my own check (work/r4_decide_check.txt) all reconcile to the cent.

Board pack (each item's submitted row minus the row with that item at its default): ducted, Open Fan, Embraer partnership, emissions lobbying and genx are each 0.00 expected and 0.00 worst. There is $0 of new R&D and no new strain.

TESTS
- Additive and paid for the risk: Hold passes. Both Open Fan rows fail, losing $9.05B and $6.39B beyond their bills in the only column.
- Airframer sets the date (H2): A 2050 launch is ready in 2060. That is 23 years after NGSA, 20 after fps, and 4 after the board stops counting NB (2056). It waits in every column. The one exception, the RISE allowance, would need $17.05B, or $15.39B with Lobby, against a $15B cap. It would still wait, so it fails the Board's no-wait test as well.
- Ducted only as a defence (H3): The Ducted is in service. We make no new claim for it, and no NB share claim, following Ali's reconciliation (27.0% fits only without the +10 pp).
- Can the suppliers carry it: Hold adds no project. The $2B strain is the 2030 stack, already in the hold row.
- Next generation: Only RISE is called the next generation. My 20% line stands, and it is not shown in engine test, so a RISE product today would rest on analysis alone [CX-0217]. Durability is not traded [CX-0208].
- Low case first: Booked at -71.

THE END-GAME CASE, NAMED AND TESTED
The installed-base lens says launch RISE now to seed 'the next 20, the next 30 years' [CX-0555]. I put that to Ghai and Ali on purpose, and it fails both.
- Ghai's numbers: 1,079 incremental engines a year from 2060 in perpetuity is worth $14.55B, less than the $17.05B cost. Breaking even takes about 2,000 incremental engines a year through 2076, on an airframe nobody has announced. That fails volumes first.
- Ali's gates: flight test not shown, no compact-core turn-off, the fuel-burn gain not shown in engine test.
- The 'launched' half of the objective is one test, 'launched and ready by 2050', and a 2050 launch is ready in 2060. A premium spent on it buys nothing.
Technology funding already seeds the next airframe. A product launch adds only a bill and a wait. The end of the game is not a reason to stretch the test. RISE stays whole as technology: no cancel and no program dividend [CX-0174, CX-0398, CX-0214, CX-0205].

SAFRAN GATE (H7)
- Ducted, Open Fan, Embraer, Lobby: all at hold, so no gate is triggered. Fleet support and customer data stay within joint practice, with the aftermarket split equally [CX-0184, CX-0219]. No Safran position is asserted.
- An Open Fan launch would have needed a stated reason why Safran would agree to a RISE product with no airframer path, and there is none.
- GEnx is GE alone.

RED LINES
No breach of H1, H2, H3, H5, H7, H8 or H9, or of my 20% and durability lines. No airframer is refused. The statement names no rival and makes no reply to any engine-source remark. It gives no date, rate, capacity, price or fuel-burn number. It claims nothing for the Ducted beyond 'in service' and 'earned in the field', and it presents no years in service as a durability record. It takes up Ali's caution: 'New engines for our customers' deliveries come first' replaces 'on time'.

EXCO DELIBERATION
Both memos recommend holding everything, and neither raises a binding veto against it. Ghai's binding vetoes (any Open Fan, Lobby alone, any premium, terms in public) are not triggered. Ali's date ground passes every row. His RISE and airframe findings are advice, and they point the same way. There are no overrides. This is not a tie, so the tie-break isn't needed. It would favour hold anyway: safety and quality before a new programme.

WHERE DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD, DROVE THE CALL
Embraer and Lobby follow company doctrine; my record is silent on both. So does the supply load (the strain rule), checked against Ali's memo, which has no supplier field data. The capital line (no new facility, capex within 2-3% of revenue) is Ghai's.

BIASES, NAMED
- The installed-base lens: tested above, and rejected.
- Deference to airframers: no airframer has asked for RISE. In this round that deference agrees with the grid.
- Guide low: booked at -71, below hold.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. It is the only column the GM gave, and the public record supports it. Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce say there is nothing to launch this year. Rolls-Royce rules out a new widebody programme beside the narrowbody, and its Trent work is funded inside its fleet budget, not as the Trent 1000 upgrade order. Airbus holds the A350 Re-engine, and Boeing starts no new 787 development.. Best grid plan there: Hold everything: -69.69 (yield 56.4%) in S1. Open Fan is -86.74 (-17.05) and Open Fan + Lobby is -85.08 (-15.39).. Premium +0.00 $B: $0.00. Hold everything is the best grid plan in S1, the only column, so no PV is given up.
- Doctrine premium: $0.00.
- Objective premium: $0.00. The Open Fan leg cannot be met (a 2050 launch is ready in 2060), and no lever on this menu moves the NB-share leg.
- Running count: $0 of $2.0B this round, and $0 of $4.0B for the game.

RISE allowance, stated apart: $0 of $15B. It ends the game unspent.
- The cheapest Open Fan row, Open Fan + Lobby, would need $15.39B against the best non-RISE plan, $0.39B over the allowance. The allowance is declared in full and cannot be topped up from the $2.0B cap.
- It would also wait in the only column (H2), which fails the Board's no-wait test.
- The Open Fan alone would need $17.05B.
- Predictions: COLUMN WEIGHTS: S1 1.00 (the only column). Hold is -69.69, Open Fan + Lobby -85.08, Open Fan -86.74. Off-grid moves are labelled, never weighted.

BOOKING: -71. That is hold less the larger exclusive 2050-ordered WB exposure ($0.50B), with $0.81B left for the unsized items, rounded down. Never booked above -69.69. Last round we booked -71 and came in at -69.69.

OFF-GRID LOW CASE (labelled, not weighted; Ghai's arithmetic, inference)
- Trent 1000 upgrade ordered in 2050, counting from 2053: GEnx goes from 44.8% to about 40.0% WB, about $0.41B.
- UltraFan WB after a 2050 Re-engine, counting from 2056: GEnx goes to about 35.2%, about $0.50B.
- These two are exclusive.
- A lone Re-engine: unsized, about $0.063B per WB point over 2055-61.
- A 10% delivery shortfall: about $0.91B NB and $0.57B WB.
- A Ducted durability finding across both aircraft: about $0.336B per NB point over 2050-56. A reserve is booked in the quarter we find the need.

PRIVATE READING FOR THE CLOSE OF THE GAME
- The rivals and both airframers hold.
- CFM stays at 27.0% NB through 2056, 1,079 engines a year.
- GEnx stays at 44.8% WB through 2061, unless a Trent 1000 upgrade or a Re-engine is ordered in 2050, which the record argues against.
- The likeliest off-grid move is still a widebody step by the rival WB engine maker, and the booking covers it.
- Upside, not booked: the Ducted's +10 pp, and any shift to a third source on fps.

TO WATCH
- Ali's first field data on the Ducted on both aircraft.
- Any airframer finding on any engine source.
- Any agreed airframer volume.
- The RISE gates.
- Expected ΔPV −71.00 $B; GM valuation after the round −69.69 $B

- Other moves: Fleet durability report to the full Board: the Ducted on NGSA and fps, the Embraer application and the GEnx upgrade (private); Customer durability data given through CFM governance, jointly with Safran (private); Joint CFM capacity plan held with Safran at the board's volume, no new facility (private); Fleet support and aftermarket for the Ducted, LEAP, Embraer and GEnx fleets, on standard terms (private); Keep RISE funded as technology, and report its gates as the state of the technology (public); CFM parity record for a hold (private)

Still to be earned after this year: +2.39 in 2026 PV. That is about $23.6B discounted to 2050, or +$32.7B of nominal lifecycle value, which is not cash. What the board does not count: - (a) Deliveries after 2056. The table shows +5.8 to +6.3 a year for 2057-60 (+$24.2B nominal, about $1.1B in 2026 PV), uncounted. The aftermarket of engines delivered through 2056 sits inside the 1.6 multiplier (inference), but its timing is not shown. - (b) Codes are locked at EIS, so the board prices no share loss from a durability finding. In practice the 36.5% lasts as long as time on wing does (inference), and I book nothing either way. - (c) There is no line for an in-service charge. The last one, powder metal, was a $5.4B sales charge, $2.9B on operating profit at Pratt's share. - (d) Negative engine margin and partner shares. The three entries for the Audit Committee should therefore be: in-service durability, the 2040 capacity step, and the concentration of P&W's NB share on one engine. |

Rounds 2 and 3 added $0. The ledger is inside the $1B cap. Confirmed. |

It names time on wing, removals and fixes as the measures. - My flag clears. |

- Decision rationale: CONTROL WHAT YOU CAN CONTROL. Orders: GTF2 Solo: hold, Joint Venture with RR: hold. These are the brief's only legal values (§3). The grid row 'GTF2: hold' is +8.88 $B (108.1%) in S1, its only column: engine value +10.88 less R&D 2.00. predictions S1 = 1.00, expected ΔPV +8.88, premium 0, and the cumulative ledger stays at $0.26B of the $1B cap.

NO BOARD ITEM IS LEGAL, AND WHY
- The cancel window closed in 2035. GTF2 folded into the Joint Venture that year, the engine has been ready since 2036, and an engine can be cancelled only before it is ready.
- A withdraw is not legal: the Joint Venture formed in 2035, and a withdraw is legal only before formation.
- launch and launch if selected cannot be ordered: no airframe is waiting to enter service (NGSA since 2037, fps since 2040).
- So the Board's standing tests (Premature exit, Committed airframe) have nothing to bite on, and Miss a cycle does not apply: both cycles were taken.

DECISION BY THE TEAM RULE
- I start from Eddy's proposal, hold/hold. Mitchill recommends the same.
- Binding vetoes: none stands. Mitchill invokes none of his four grounds: no new cash; the engine is fitted on both airframes, so it is not unselected; there is no terms order; the booked +8.88 is all on the public record. Eddy invokes neither technical ground: the engine was ready in 2036, against EIS dates of 2037 and 2040, and the brief shows no live fleet problem.
- Conditional vetoes, all respected in the moves and the statement:
  - Mitchill ground 4: no 'proven', no time-on-wing claim, no published fleet figure, no objective claim, no lifecycle value as cash, no derivative value.
  - Mitchill ground 3: the kit is charged and the answer to Boeing carries no concession.
  - Eddy ground 1: no configuration change without its own durability programme.
  - Eddy ground 2: the ring-fence; the rate waits if the fleet is short.
- Safety and durability constraint first [PX-0035][PX-0073]. On this board it binds through the other moves, not the orders. The fleet beats a new engine [PX-0080].
- Overrides: none.

ANSWER TO BOEING'S 'ONE SOURCE' LINE
- It is fair, and it is the right test. Eddy calls it common-mode risk: one engine, one configuration, one LLP set and one casting, forging and powder-metal supply serve both airframes. One finding touches both fleets at once.
- We answer with product and data, not price [PX-0055][P-0768][PX-0100]:
  - a public rule that fleet data goes to both airframers as flown;
  - a private data pack that matches what Boeing's safety committee reviews, split by environment with hot-and-harsh shown separately.
- No concession, discount, free upgrade, discounted spares or time-on-wing guarantee. Mitchill confirms that ground 3 passes.
- Whether Boeing wants a third source is Boeing's to say [PX-0062].

WHOLE-PROGRAMME CLOSE-OUT AGAINST PLAN [P-1191] (Mitchill's numbers, which I trust over my own)
- 2030: launch if selected fired on code 7. Expected +0.23 (mine) and -1.72 (his); booked 0.00. Against Do Nothing it was worth +16.20 (mine) and +13.69 (his). The +22.00 S5 column assumed no Rolls-Royce NB engine and was never the plan.
- 2035 grid:
  - hold: +3.37 / -17.95 / -8.44 / +3.37;
  - hold + commit: +3.37 / -17.95 / -8.44 / +8.88.
  - Expected: +0.39 (mine) and +0.07 (his). Booked: +3.37, with the commit at $0. The outcome was S4, +8.88, so the commit realised +5.51 over hold. The cancel he advised against would have written off $1.67B to save $0.33B, against rows of -32.79 to -11.47.
- 2045 and 2050: expected = booked = +8.88. That is +8.81 over his 2035 expected and +10.60 over his 2030 expected.
- Return: $10.88B of PV engine value on $2.00B of R&D, 5.44x on the board's basis. It beats a buyback at about $0 NPV at the 10% grid rate.
  - It is a value multiple gross of production cost, not a cash return.
  - Against the PV of the phased R&D it is about 10x; we do not book that figure.
- Payback on lifecycle value: 2040 nominal (cumulative +4.4 against 2.00) and 2041 discounted (+2.15). Cash payback is later and not on the board.
- Cost: GTF2 cost its plan, $2.00B, about $0.33B a year in 2030-35 (5.6% of the envelope, on his phasing). The fold write-off was $0. There has been no R&D or write-off since 2035, no Joint Venture cash beyond $2B, and no use of the stretch.
- Value split, on my arithmetic confirmed by Mitchill: about +8.43 from deliveries through 2050 and +2.39 from 2051-56 (total +10.82 against the brief's +10.88; the gap is rounding).

WHAT I OWN
(1) The upside came in the column we weighted lowest: S4 at 0.10 (mine) and 0.08 (his). In a sealed round, Rolls-Royce's conditional commit was as free for it as ours was for us. The order was right because it was weakly dominant (0 / 0 / 0 / +5.51), not because we read Rolls-Royce well.
(2) The 2030 S5 figure of +22.00 was never the plan.
(3) The Board's 2035 statement tightening was not taken up. The 2036 date held on the board, which is counter-evidence to my date optimism, not a licence; I state no date this year.
(4) The pre-EIS reporting to the Governance and Public Policy Committee cannot be evidenced.
(5) The 2045 look-back went out undated, against Eddy's advice. It is dated now: the Committee's next quarterly meeting, and at once on any finding.
(6) We still have no fleet data on the board. Eddy says plainly "I don't have it" on hours, removals, campaigns, AOG, pacing parts and kit insertion. His parts passes in every round rested on the board. So we disclose rules and commitments, not claims, and log the gap as a control gap for the Audit Committee.

CAPITAL: dividend, then debt, then buybacks.
- The analyst context runs to 2030E only: the dividend is covered 2.3-2.4x and net debt is 0.76-0.94x EBITDA. Past 2030 the line rests on the board alone, so it is written as an order of priority, not as a 2050 status.
- The $14.7B against $9.7B (2050, nominal) is lifecycle value at engine price x 1.6, not free cash flow.

BIAS CHECK
- Optimism on dates: none stated. The look-back date is a reporting date.
- Architecture loyalty: the test now is time on wing, not the gear.
- Value capture: the kit stays charged, and Boeing gets no concession.

DOCTRINE, NOT MY RECORD
My record has no Joint Venture evidence (Low). The following are company doctrine and Eddy's seat inference:
- the Joint Venture governance [P-1233][P-1744][P-1240][P-2004];
- the Committee cadence;
- the Audit Committee entries.
Applying 'miss a cycle' [PX-0071] and demand visibility [PX-0109] to engines was inference throughout.

READ BACKWARDS, MY QUESTIONS
- Was the demand visible? Yes: two airframers put us on the record.
- Was durability mature at EIS? The board says ready. The data has not shown it yet, and I won't claim it.
- Was it a responsible bet with a responsible payback [PX-0010]? On the board, yes: 5.4x, and payback in 2040-41 on lifecycle value.
- Is the dividend safe [PX-0081]? Nothing on this board touches it; past 2030 that rests on the board alone.
- Expected scenario: S1: rivals hold. It is the only column on the 2050 grid.. Best grid plan there: 'GTF2: hold' (GTF2 Solo: hold, Joint Venture with RR: hold): +8.88 $B (108.1%) in S1. It is the only row, and so the best.. Premium +0.00 $B: No premium. Hold/hold is the only legal plan and the best on the grid, so no doctrine-favoured order trails the best expected PV and no Board item exists. The cumulative ledger closes the game at $0.26B of the team's $1B cap: the round-1 doctrine premium at my weights ($0.34B at Mitchill's). Rounds 2, 3 and 4 added $0.
- Predictions: S1 = 1.00 (rivals hold). It is the only column on the 2050 grid and the expected column. Mitchill also has S1 = 1.00.
- Expected ΔPV +8.88 $B; GM valuation after the round +8.88 $B

- Other moves: GTF Hot Section durability kit insertion continues across the fleet, charged (public); Joint Venture governance: one engine, P&W modules, P&W design authority (public); Fleet-data rule toward Airbus and Boeing (the fact of the rule only) (public); Data pack to both airframers' in-service reviews (private); Fleet ring-fence, weekly pacing-parts review and a DATED records look-back on the 2040 capacity step (private); Standing durability and parts report to the Governance and Public Policy Committee (private); Top enterprise risks to the Audit Committee and the full Board (private); Joint Venture technical governance, private clauses (private); Capital-order note to the Finance Committee (private); Whole-programme close-out against plan, reported to the Board (private)

- Decision rationale: DECISION
Every field is at hold. This is my framing proposal, unchanged, and both memos recommend it:
- `UltraFan NB Solo: hold`
- `Joint Venture with P&W: hold`
- `UltraFan WB: hold`
- `Trent 1000 upgrade: hold`
None of these is a Board item. Do Nothing on widebody (Trent only) is right because no airframe can use a new widebody engine in time, and neither widebody order earns its bill [RX-0058].

GRID (brief rows, S1 / S2 / S3), weights 0.80 / 0.12 / 0.08
- "UltraFan NB: hold": +32.35 / +32.35 / +32.70. Expected +32.38 (+32.385 at the Board's 0.80 / 0.10 / 0.10, +32.42 at McCabe's stress 0.60 / 0.20 / 0.20). Worst column +32.35. Best row in every column.
- "UltraFan NB: hold + T1000 upgrade": +25.63 / +25.63 / +26.08. Against hold: -6.72 / -6.72 / -6.62, so -6.71 weighted. Worst -6.72, 3.4x its $2B bill. Without the $5B strain it is still -1.72 / -1.72 / -1.62. It counts only from 2053, so 2050 widebody share does not move.
- "UltraFan NB: hold + UltraFan WB": +23.35 / +23.68 / +24.38. Against hold: -9.00 / -8.67 / -8.32, so -8.91 weighted (-8.90 at the Board's weights). Worst -9.00, 2.25x its $4B bill. Without the strain it is -4.00 / -3.67 / -3.32. It would be ready in 2056, a year after a 2050 Re-engine's 2055 EIS.
- Upgrade plus UltraFan WB together: not allowed (exclusive).
The verdict does not depend on the weights: hold is best in every column.

VETO CHECKS
- McCabe: she withholds her co-signature on both `Trent 1000 upgrade: launch` and `UltraFan WB: launch`, on hurdle and downside. These are binding, and I give neither order. No balance-sheet veto arises: no new money, debt unchanged, no equity, leverage well below about 1.5x.
- Watson: no Civil veto at hold. Nothing is compressed, no early date is disclosed, and no narrowbody launch, trigger or commit needs a support plan. He cleared the draft statement. The one line I added ('We will report what the fleet shows, and claim nothing more') has no date, no terms and no claim of RR module content, so it stays inside his test. He keeps his re-check of the final text.
- No revision is needed. `overrides` is empty.

RED LINES (checked before return)
- McCabe's note that an UltraFan WB launch would also breach 'no UltraFan launch without a programme that can fly it'. Confirmed and struck: no UltraFan WB launch is ordered.
- Overlap: no UltraFan is in development, so the upgrade rule is not engaged. No Solo stands, so the UltraFan WB-beside-Solo rule is not engaged.
- Strain: none ordered. Control's strain line stays at $0.
- Disclosure: no new programme, retrofit, kit or growth-engine date. Nothing on Re-engines or any airframer's dates or rates. No Joint Venture terms beyond the record. No claim of RR module technology in the in-service engine.
- Cancel: none is open, and none is needed. No orphan programme stands.
No red-line flag is left unresolved.

PREMIUM: $0, item by item. No doctrine-favoured order is kept against the grid.

WHAT THE GRID DOES NOT SHOW
1. Content per engine. McCabe's rebuild puts about +38.6 of our +38.07 engine value in narrowbody and about -0.5 in widebody. The board credits our 1,460 engines a year at full value, which matches our cash only at about half the content and revenue of each Joint Venture engine. Each 10 points below half costs about $7.7B. So work share and revenue share at 36.5% each heads the governance note.
2. Durability. P&W says the record is 'still being earned' and is still inserting its Hot Section kit across the GTF fleet. Boeing says that if the Joint Venture engine stumbles, the fps is back on CFM alone. The share risk and the retrofit bill sit off the board. We cannot see the hot-section or life-limited-part data, so getting it is the note's first operating ask. Actually, I've seen this movie before [RX-0087]: the Trent 1000 is the precedent we will not repeat.

IF A RE-ENGINE GOES ON THE RECORD THIS ROUND (orders are sealed, so this is the answer after the game)
- The airframe goes on the record first. For UltraFan WB, that means an A350 Re-engine on the record, or a 787 Re-engine on which Boeing has named UltraFan.
- The strain is shown: $5B, permanent, beside the Joint Venture.
- The case is made on its own return against hold, inside the caps ($1B for UltraFan WB).
- Readiness is Watson's: UltraFan WB at launch + 6 against the Re-engine's EIS. On this brief, 2056 misses a 2050 Re-engine's 2055 EIS by a year.
- Until then, we defend the widebody through the fleet we have: Trent XWB time on wing and shop-visit cost, on standard terms.

RECORD AND DOCTRINE
- From my record: the cash framing, 'won't do anything not profitable', choices not options.
- Company doctrine and my colleagues' inference, because my record is Low there: the Joint Venture governance asks (the IAE lesson). The cash figures are McCabe's; the readiness and support views are Watson's.
- Expected scenario: S1, rivals hold (weight 0.80). Neither Re-engine is on the record or announced. Boeing said 'On the 787, we're not starting a new development this year' and grows the 787 out of Charleston capacity. Airbus holds the A350 Re-engine 'until the technology, the engine and the business case are all ready'. S2 (787 Re-engine) is 0.12 and S3 (both Re-engines) is 0.08. Hold is the best plan in every column, so the choice does not depend on the weights.. Best grid plan there: "UltraFan NB: hold" (every field at hold): +32.35 in S1, and +32.35 / +32.70 in S2 / S3. It is the best grid plan in all three columns. Expected +32.38 at 0.80 / 0.12 / 0.08.. Premium +0.00 $B: None. The submitted plan is the best grid plan in every column, and no doctrine-favoured order is kept against the grid. To carry them, the upgrade would need $6.71B (above the $3B cap) and UltraFan WB $8.91B (above its $1B sub-cap). Neither is ordered.
- Predictions: Rivals this round (final round, 2050):
- Boeing: no 787 Re-engine, p about 0.80 (S2 + S3 = 0.20). It keeps growing the 787 from Charleston on KPI gates, with fps production steps gated the same way.
- Airbus: no A350 Re-engine, p about 0.92 (S3 = 0.08). It sells the A350 family on value, on the existing teams.
- P&W: holds the Joint Venture as formed, on its public terms (one engine, P&W modules, P&W design authority). It keeps inserting the GTF Hot Section kit across the fleet, charged. No new programme.
- CFM/GE: holds. RISE stays funded as technology, with no date. No new product order.
- Codes: unchanged. NGSA stays locked at code 7, and fps stays fitted CFM + P&W + RR, with the Joint Venture engine counted as one source.
- Our ΔPV: +32.35 in S1, +32.35 in S2, +32.70 in S3. Expected +32.38.
- Control: own narrowbody money $5.7B in every column (final); strain $0; RR widebody share in 2050 55.2 / 55.2 / 58.0%; RR narrowbody share 36.5%; Airbus widebody airframe share 41.2%.
- Board: no items to review; every field is the default.
- Expected ΔPV +32.38 $B; GM valuation after the round +32.35 $B

- Other moves: Fleet first on the Joint Venture engine in service (public); Quarterly fleet report to the Safety, Energy Transition & Tech Committee (Watson) (private); Joint Venture governance note to the Board, delivered now (Watson and McCabe) (private); Funding discipline and quarterly report to the Board (McCabe) (private); Trent time on wing and fleet support inside the existing budget (Watson) (public); Commercial terms with Airbus and Boeing (public)


## Against the previous game

One choice differs, Boeing's fps engine selection (ordered in 2030, kept in 2035), and it moves the engine makers' values by $31.40B (CFM/GE) and $11.81B each (P&W and Rolls-Royce). Both games used the same dashboard (combined_dash.py at the Game_v3.txt snapshot), the same twenty agents, the same rules (bar one clarifying sentence, see Method) and the same four decision years. This game added Control's objectives, the GM's net-debt tracker and the 2026 analyst notes to the companies' files. Each company's decision record shows why its orders matched or differed.

**One choice differs.**
- Boeing's fps engine selection: CFM alone (code 3) in the previous game, all three makers (code 7) here, each ordered in 2030 and kept in 2035. Ortberg's doctrine lean was code 3 in both games; here, with the enabler and his Board's advice, he framed and ordered code 7.
- Every other order in all 20 company-rounds is the same, including Boeing's 10-year ramp, both Joint Venture commitments and every hold.

**Same orders, different reasons.**
- Boeing's ramp: in the previous game the $0.46B was a doctrine premium and the Board's 7-year advice was "Not taken". Here the Board offered the 10-year as an approved alternative for the stress-case debt line, and Ortberg split the premium into doctrine and objective parts.
- Airbus's code 7: previously "supply certainty and customer choice"; here "the enabler trap".
- Rolls-Royce's 2030 launch: the same order, now with the $4B breach priced and stated.

**The engine makers' values move with one selection.**
- P&W and Rolls-Royce +$11.81B each: from 2040 fps is split among all three makers, with the Joint Venture engine beside CFM, where before it was all CFM. CFM/GE −$31.40B.
- The three engine makers together are $7.79B worse off, so the change is not a pure transfer (inference: the makers' discount rates and lifecycle values differ).
- The airframers' values are identical to the cent.

**On Control's yardstick, the previous game's orders score one objective lower.**
- Boeing's first enabler line fails there (fps on CFM alone, one source); every other added objective scores the same, including Rolls-Royce's $5.71B breach and Boeing's repaired balance sheet.
- Among the base objectives, CFM/GE's narrowbody dominance flips: met there (51.1% at worst), missed here (27.0%). P&W's ΔPV objective flips the other way: missed there (−$2.93B), met here (+$8.88B).

## The analyst models: prediction against the game

Before this game each company's profile gained a note on the July-August 2026 analyst models: Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs company models, and S&P Capital IQ consensus where the files had it. The notes are real-world context; the dashboard's numbers still set every payoff. Two things come from them directly: Boeing's net-debt tracker (start, free cash flow, historical level) and the stress case, set from the spread of estimates and Boeing's record. Below, what the models say, and how their implications for each company's decisions compare with what the agents then did.

| Company | Fact | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Boeing | Net debt $22.53B at end-2026 (consensus mean, 5 estimates); GS $22.55B, MS $23.01B. Mid-2026 gross debt $45.9B. | Consensus 'Net Debt'; GS BA BS r63-r64; MS BA Balance_Sheet_Annual |
| Boeing | Consensus free cash flow 2027-2030: $5.67B, $9.86B, $13.03B, $15.04B ($45.8B over 2026-2030). Free cash flow was negative in five of the seven years 2019-2025. | Consensus 'Free Cash Flow'; MS BA Cash_Flow_Annual r77 |
| Boeing | Net debt averaged about −$0.6B in 2010-2018, a small net cash; the objective's historical level is $0, rounded from it. | GS BA BS r64 |
| Boeing | The brokers split on capital allocation: GS buys back $42B in 2027-2030 and ends 2030 with $17.4B of net debt; MS buys back none and ends with $16.2B of net cash. No new airplane is in any forecast. | GS BA CFLO r45, BS r64; MS BA Balance_Sheet_Annual 'Net Debt' |
| Airbus | Net cash in every year from 2010A to 2030E; about EUR 31.8-33.1bn in 2030E, with free cash flow above EUR 10bn. Neither model includes an A320 successor. | MS CFS r86, r61; GS Balance Sheet r65; MS Airbus r38 |
| CFM/GE | The 737 MAX is 100% CFM; CFM holds 52-59% of the A320neo family against the GTF (2025A). GS GE has LEAP deliveries falling to 1,000 by 2040E with no successor. | MS GE CES Annual_Driver r51; GS SAF Propulsion r34; MS SAF Civil OE r184; GS GE Revenue build r9 |
| CFM/GE | The aftermarket carried 75% of GE CES revenue in 2025A; GE returns about 94-124% of free cash flow. | MS GE CES_Annual r53; MS GE Cash_Flow_Annual r96 |
| P&amp;W | On the GS legacy model the GTF loses money on every new engine sold and turns net positive only in 2028E. The 2023 durability precedent cost $5.4bn of sales. | GS RTX GTF Analysis r14, r24; MS RTX P&W_Annual r29 |
| P&amp;W | RTX net debt is below 1x EBITDA by 2030E; $10-16bn is left after shareholder returns over 2026E-2030E. | MS RTX Balance_Sheet_Annual r82; GS RTX BS r80; calc. from MS RTX Cash_Flow_Annual r72/r40/r41 and GS RTX CFLO r47/r34/r35 |
| Rolls-Royce | About half of group profit rests on widebody; 85% of Trent deliveries in 2025A were for airframes with no rival engine. Company-funded R&amp;D is only £0.6-0.9bn a year to 2030E. | MS Civil r14, Civil OE r82-r87; MS IS r129 |
| Rolls-Royce | Neither bank models a Rolls-Royce narrowbody engine (GS narrowbody units zero to 2031E). The Trent ramp cost about £3.0bn of new-engine losses over nine years. | GS Civil Aerospace r61; MS Civil OE r52 |

From the analyst note in each company's profile folder (analyst_models_2026.md), each checked against the extracted model files. MS = Morgan Stanley, GS = Goldman Sachs; A = actual, E = estimate. Money is nominal in the reporting currency. The tracker's validation: with base cash flow only it gives Boeing net debt of $16.49B at end-2027 and $6.70B at end-2028 in 2026 dollars, against consensus $16.57B and $6.90B.

**Boeing: the analysts' cash flow decided the debt objective before the game did.**
- The tracker reproduces consensus net debt within $0.2B and reaches zero in 2029 with no new programme. The fps then competes with returns to owners, not with debt repair, as the note predicted: "Under debt first, programme money after 2029 comes out of what would otherwise go to owners." (GS models $42B of buybacks in 2027-2030.)
- "A launch in the 2030 round mostly tests the hold years", the note said, and it did: the 7-year ramp failed the stress case at the 2035 hold year, not the 2030 deadline. The 10-year's later, lower spending passed.
- The note flagged engine selection as "a supply-risk choice the models do not price". Boeing took it as one, on the enabler.

**Airbus: money was not the limit, as the note said, but its own cash line was.**
- The banks show net cash above EUR 31bn in 2030E. Airbus's agents measured NGSA against one year's free cash flow instead: $5.07B a year against a $5.2B line, 97% used. That line, with its other rules, kept the A350 Re-engine and Delay Tactics out in 2030 and 2035.
- "Keep two sources if the board allows it": NGSA flies two.

**CFM/GE: the note named its biggest risk, and half of it came true: Boeing's slot was shared, not lost.**
- "Losing a new Boeing narrowbody to a rival, including a Pratt & Whitney and Rolls-Royce Joint Venture, costs more than losing an Airbus slot." Sharing fps cost CFM/GE $31.40B against the previous game.
- "A Ducted launch is the defence if a rival engine is selected": it was launched in 2030 and is worth 10 points before re-normalising, but it could not undo selections sealed in the same round.

**P&amp;W: launch against a committed airframer, and share the risk.**
- The note's advice, prefer "launch only if an airframer selects P&W" and a Joint Venture to share new-engine losses, is exactly what P&W did.
- Its durability precedent ($5.4bn of sales in 2023) is the risk Boeing now names in public. P&W answered "with product and data, not price".

**Rolls-Royce: the note's two warnings split.**
- "Do not stack projects in the 2030 round": kept. No widebody programme was ever on the record.
- "An UltraFan narrowbody Solo launch repeats the Trent ramp at bigger volume" and "The Joint Venture fits the R&D budget better": the conditional Solo it chose cost $1.71B more than its Joint Venture share.

## Method

How the game was run
- A replay. The previous ExCo-and-Board game (run dash-2050-exco) replayed from the start in a fresh run,
dash-2050-obj: the same dashboard, agents, rules and decision years. Three things were added to the companies' files
before 2030. Otherwise the public rules gained one clarifying sentence (spend beyond a maker's Joint Venture share is
written off in the fold year) and the GM's model one check (below); both games replay exactly.
- Control's objectives (listed under Control's objectives), in Boeing's, Airbus's and Rolls-Royce's private
briefs, with their metrics for every plan on the payoff grid. CFM/GE's and P&W's objectives were unchanged. Boeing's brief also carried its net-debt path, base and stress, and a table of the
engine sources each engine code would give.
- The GM's balance-sheet tracker for Boeing (below).
- The analyst notes: a note per company on the July-August 2026 Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs models and,
for Boeing, Capital IQ consensus, each checked against the extracted model files. The notes are context; the dashboard
sets every payoff.
- Dashboard. Your combined_dash.py, run headless (never in Streamlit) at the Game_v3.txt snapshot. Solved by
the game master (GM), it reproduces the snapshot: airframers 4 Pure Nash and 12 Near-Nash, engines 1 Pure Nash and 11
Near-Nash.
- Players. 20 AI agents. In each company, the CEO, CFO and operating head of the default 2026 executive
committee, each profiled from that person's own public words, and the Board, profiled from transcripts, filings and web
search, with a culture score. Where an executive's record was thin, the seat's tests were derived from the role or
company doctrine and labelled low confidence (Pope, Toepfer, Wagner). They are AI agents, not the people.
- Each round, in each company. The Board gives guidance; the CEO frames the choice; the CFO and operating
head test it independently; the CEO decides; the CFO and operating head run a veto check, and the CEO revises once if
a binding veto or red-line flag stands; each Board item goes to Board review, with one more revision and a reversion
to the default if a Board veto stands. Each company's chain ran as its own workflow, in parallel; nothing passed
between companies.
- Information. The public bulletin plus a private brief per company. Only the GM saw the dashboard and the
other companies' briefs. The isolation hook denies other companies' files and briefs, the dashboard, the snapshot, GM
code, earlier reports and runs, and the GM's scratch files. An audit of every tool call found all 20 company-rounds
clean.
- Rounds. Four sealed, simultaneous rounds: 2030, 2035, 2045, 2050. Each round's value is the GM valuation of
the state after that round, assuming nobody moves again. The final round's value is the game outcome.
How Control's objectives are scored
- They never change a payoff. The dashboard's ΔPV, including its own debt penalty on $45B of debt, is
untouched. A company could give up value for an objective only within its premium cap, and had to log it.
- Boeing's net debt (total debt less cash and short-term investments) at year end, in 2026 dollars. Start:
$22.53B at end-2026, the consensus mean. Base free cash flow: consensus for 2027-2030 ($5.67B, $9.86B, $13.03B,
$15.04B nominal), deflated at the dashboard's 2.2% CPI, then flat at $13.79B; the stress case is one-third lower. Each
year: free cash flow = base + 79% of (the game's profit change against its baseline − programme cash) − 3% real
interest on any debt the game adds; net debt = last year's in today's money − free cash flow, never below the
historical level once there (surplus goes to owners). Programme cash: each bill spread over its development years on
a standard curve (5% in year 1 of 10, peaking at 14% in year 7), plus the two-front strain over the fps and 787
Re-engine overlap; the 737 rate increase spent evenly to 2032. Profits count from the first decision year, as played.
Historical level $0, rounded from the 2010-2018 average of −$0.6B.
- Engine sources. The independent engine sources fitted to a new narrowbody in production in 2040, 2045 and
2050: CFM, P&W's GTF2, Rolls-Royce's UltraFan NB, or the P&W-Rolls-Royce Joint Venture engine, which counts as
one. Engine makers in the market: those with narrowbody share above zero in 2045 and 2050.
- Rolls-Royce's own narrowbody money: $8B for UltraFan NB Solo, $4B for its Joint Venture share; a Solo
cancelled or folded counts its spend to date. Strain: the dashboard's one-off $5B whenever a narrowbody and a widebody
engine programme are both on the record.
Rules the GM added to the static dashboard (as in the previous game)
- Time. A programme enters service, or an engine is ready, after its development time from the decision year:
fps 7 or 10 years, fps via Embraer 10, NGSA 7, Re-engines 5; ducted 6, GTF2 6, UltraFan NB 7, UltraFan WB 6, Trent and
GEnx upgrades 3, the Embraer partnership 7, the Joint Venture 6 or an inherited schedule; Open Fan 10, never before
2045.
- Cancellations write off the bill × years elapsed ÷ development years. Delay Tactics are covert; none
was ordered.
- Engines. The dashboard's engine game is applied year by year, splitting each airframer's narrowbody share
equally among the engines fitted to it. Each narrowbody engine move counts from the later of its ready year and the
first new narrowbody's entry into service (2037 here); widebody engine upgrades count from the first Re-engine's entry
into service, or 2042.
- Joint Venture. It forms when both makers commit. It folds in solo programmes, writing off spend beyond each
maker's share, and inherits the most advanced folded schedule. A Joint Venture formed after an airframe's entry into
service does not rewrite the engines fitted before it (a check added for this game).
- Tests. Dashboard reproduction, the engine move combinations and the snapshot equilibria; both games replay
exactly, and no played year changes; the isolation tests.
What the numbers are
- ΔPV and yield. ΔPV is the change in present value at 2026 against the status quo (nobody launches
anything), at each company's dashboard WACC. Yield = 100 + 100 × ΔPV ÷ enterprise value.
- Dashboard conventions. Airframe programme spend is charged in full at entry into service. Engine R&D is
undiscounted. Engine value is the lifetime value per engine delivered.
- Regret. The final ΔPV a company would have had with its best alternative orders in that round, holding every
other order in the game as played (later rounds re-run), minus its actual final ΔPV.
- S1, S2, …. The scenario columns of each company's own payoff grid in that round's brief; they differ by
company and round. Ids in brackets, such as [BX-1239], are the agents' evidence citations. "(inference)" marks a
reading of the record that no single number states.
- Your dashboard alone. The table below sets each final value next to your dashboard alone. The airframers
match exactly. For the engine makers the dashboard fixes a 50/50 airframer split and locks P&W to GTF2 Solo; the
GM's Joint Venture and year-by-year split account for the difference.

### Your dashboard alone against the GM valuation, final state

| Player | Dashboard alone ΔPV $B | GM valuation ΔPV $B | GM minus dashboard |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Boeing | +0.66 | +0.66 | +0.00 |
| Airbus | +23.37 | +23.37 | +0.00 |
| CFM/GE | −71.07 | −69.69 | +1.38 |
| Pratt & Whitney | +9.71 | +8.88 | −0.84 |
| Rolls-Royce | +34.91 | +32.35 | −2.55 |

- Boeing: No difference. The airframer values are your dashboard's own, at the game's dates.
- Airbus: No difference. The airframer values are your dashboard's own, at the game's dates.
- CFM/GE: Dashboard alone −$71.07B. Both partners' Joint Venture moves −$7.09B: the dashboard locks P&W to GTF2 Solo for the engine selections, so there only Rolls-Royce's Joint Venture move applies. The year-by-year split +$8.46B: until fps enters service in 2040, Boeing's narrowbody share is the CFM-only 737 MAX, where the dashboard splits both airframers three ways from 2037.
- Pratt & Whitney: Dashboard alone +$9.71B. Both Joint Venture moves +$2.75B. The year-by-year split −$3.59B.
- Rolls-Royce: Dashboard alone +$34.91B. The fold-in write-off −$1.71B. Both Joint Venture moves +$2.75B. The year-by-year split −$3.59B.

With a fixed 50/50 split and the board's own engine moves, the GM engine model gives the board's numbers exactly.
